# Changelog

All notable changes to this project will be documented in this file.

The format is based on [Keep a Changelog](https://keepachangelog.com/en/1.0.0/),
and this project adheres to [Semantic Versioning](https://semver.org/spec/v2.0.0.html).

## [Unreleased]

### Fixed

- A RETURNING clause names its columns after what the caller wrote ([#894](https://github.com/nao1215/filesql/issues/894)). SQLite names an unaliased result column after the text of the expression that produced it, so an expression translation rewrote would answer under the helper's name; a select list carries the caller's text back as an alias to stop that, and the RETURNING list -- written by a second function over the same select items -- did not. `INSERT INTO t (a) VALUES (1) RETURNING a::text` therefore answered a column called `postgresql_cast(a, 'text')` where the same expression in a SELECT answered `a::text`, and the helper's name went on into whatever the rows were rendered as: a CSV header, a JSON key, a column heading. Every construct lowering rewrites was affected, in all three dialects and on the RETURNING of INSERT, UPDATE and DELETE. A list of result columns is now written by one function wherever it stands, so the two cannot drift apart again, and an alias the caller wrote still wins over the preserved text.

- A column list on a table reference renames the columns or is refused ([#895](https://github.com/nao1215/filesql/issues/895)). `SELECT * FROM (SELECT a, b FROM t) s(x, y)` was read and then dropped, so the query answered a and b rather than x and y, and a query that went on to use the new names failed with SQLite reporting `no such column: s.x` for a name the caller had bound -- which reads as a fault in the query rather than in the translation. SQLite takes a result column's name from a select list, so a derived table's list is now moved onto the query it stands in front of, and a compound query takes it on the first branch, which is where SQLite reads the names from. Where there is no select list to move it onto -- a base table, a table-valued call, a VALUES list, a select list that ends in a star, or a count of names that does not match the count of items -- it is refused with `ErrUnsupportedSyntax` naming the construct and pointing at `WITH name (columns) AS (...)`, the spelling SQLite does take and that this package already translated correctly. This changes queries that used to translate: the shapes that cannot be expressed now fail at translation rather than answering the old names in silence.

- ALTER TABLE names the constraint it cannot add ([#896](https://github.com/nao1215/filesql/issues/896)). Everything after ADD was read as a column definition, so `ALTER TABLE t ADD CONSTRAINT ck CHECK (a > 0)` was refused with "the column type CK is not supported", reporting the constraint's name as a type, and `ADD PRIMARY KEY (a)`, `ADD UNIQUE (a)`, `ADD CHECK (a > 0)` and `DROP CONSTRAINT ck` were refused as unreadable SQL, which they are not in any of the three dialects. SQLite can only add, drop and rename columns, so all of them are still refused; what changes is that the refusal is `ErrUnsupportedSyntax` and says which construct has no SQLite form, and that an index declared the same way points at CREATE INDEX or DROP INDEX. The keywords a column can also be named after are told apart by what follows them, so `ADD COLUMN "check" INT` and a bare column named `unique` still translate, and only a parenthesized list makes one of them a constraint.

## [0.53.0] - 2026-08-31

### Breaking Changes

- `parser.FileType` names a format and no longer fuses in the codec wrapping it, so it has eight constants instead of sixty-four. The fifty-six that named a combination -- `parser.CSVGZ`, `parser.TSVBZ2`, `parser.JSONLLZ4` and the rest of the seven-by-eight grid -- are gone, along with `parser.IsCompressed` and `parser.BaseFileType`, which existed only to ask which half of such a constant you were looking at. This is the change the root package's own `FileType` made earlier and that this package was left out of, on the reasoning that a format-and-codec name was the honest description at the lower level. It was not: the cross product in the type left five switches over all fifty-six combinations to be written out by hand, so adding a codec meant seven new constants and an edit to every one of them, and the names collided where the two axes met -- `parser.JSONLZ4` was lz4-compressed JSON while `parser.JSONLLZ4` was lz4-compressed JSONL, one letter apart with nothing in either to say which reading was right. That ambiguity is not hypothetical; it mis-sorted one case while this change was being made. `DetectFileType` now looks through a compression extension and answers the format, so "data.csv" and "data.csv.gz" are both `parser.CSV`, and `Parse` reads the bytes it is given rather than unwrapping them, because a codec is something an `io.Reader` already expresses. Migration: open a compressed file through `filesql.NewCompressionFactory().CreateReaderForFile(path)`, which detects the codec and opens the file in one call, or wrap a stream whose codec you know with `filesql.NewCompressionHandler(filesql.CompressionGZ).CreateReader(r)`, and hand the reader that comes back to `Parse` with the format `DetectFileType` gives. `prep` is unaffected in its own vocabulary: it re-exported formats only, and `prep.FileType` with its `FileType*` constants is untouched, though a caller who passed it one of the parser's fused constants passes the format now and unwraps the stream itself. Loading a compressed file through `filesql.Open`, `AddPath`, `AddFS` or `AddReader` is unchanged in every way.

### Changed

- The codec a path names is worked out in one place. Six copies of the eight compression extensions had accumulated across two packages -- the switch that reads a codec out of a path, two loops that strip one, the glob patterns a directory scan matches, the test for whether a path is compressed, and the parser's own detection -- which is the drift the codec package was made to end when the readers and writers had the same problem. That package now owns both directions: `All` is the codecs that compress and `FromPath` reads one out of a path and returns the path without it. The lookup takes the longest matching extension rather than the first, so it no longer depends on the order of the list; ".gz" ends with zlib's ".z", and every one of the six copies had been correct only because gzip happened to be tried first. Nothing a caller sees changes, checked over 252 paths spanning all seven formats, all eight codecs, upper and lower case, and nested directories. A test walks the codecs by value rather than through the list so one missing from it fails there, and another holds `CompressionType` against the codecs the codec package knows, since the two enums are bridged by a bare numeric conversion at five call sites and nothing had said they must agree.

### Fixed

- A comparison tag on an integer field decides two integers by the integers ([#890](https://github.com/nao1215/filesql/issues/890)). `prep`'s `eq`, `ne`, `gt`, `gte`, `lt`, `lte`, `min`, `max` and `len` each held their parameter as a `float64` and read the cell with `strconv.ParseFloat`, so two `int64` values past 2^53 that round to the same double were compared as one number: `eq=9007199254740993` accepted a cell holding 9007199254740992, `min` and `len` accepted it too, and `gt=9007199254740992` rejected 9007199254740993, a value that is greater than the threshold. The row that should have been refused was written to the reader the processor returns, and the row that should have passed was dropped from it under `WithValidRowsOnly`, with nothing to say the number had been rounded. The parameter is now kept as the number it spells rather than only as the double, a comparison between two integers is decided by the integers whatever their size, and a rejection quotes the parameter as the caller wrote it rather than the double it rounded to. A cell or a parameter that spells a fraction or an exponent still goes through `float64`, so `gt=1.5` and `eq=1e3` read as before.
- A cross-field comparison decides two integers by the integers ([#891](https://github.com/nao1215/filesql/issues/891)). `prep`'s `eqfield`, `nefield`, `gtfield`, `gtefield`, `ltfield` and `ltefield` read both cells with `strconv.ParseFloat` on any field that is not a string, so on an `int64` field `nefield` reported 9007199254740993 as equal to 9007199254740992 and `gtfield` reported it as not greater. This is the same fault as the one in the comparison tags, in the second of the two places this package compared numbers; both now read a number through one type, so the two orderings cannot drift apart again. The fallbacks are unchanged: a pair of cells that spells fractions is compared as doubles, a comparison on a string field orders the strings, and a pair that spells no number at all falls back to the text.
- excluded_unless reads its pairs the way required_unless reads them ([#889](https://github.com/nao1215/filesql/issues/889)). A `excluded_unless` naming more than one field and value pair forbade a value unless every pair matched, while `required_unless` -- the tag it is documented to negate -- asks for a value unless any one pair matches. The two disagreed on every row where some pairs match and others do not, so `excluded_unless=Kind paid Tier gold`, written to mean that a note is allowed on a paid or a gold row, refused the note on every gold row of a free account. One pair matching is now enough to allow a value, which is what the dialect this package follows does and what the mirror tag already did, and the message a refusal carries joins its conditions with "or" rather than "and". A tag naming no pair still asks for nothing. This changes which rows `excluded_unless` refuses for any tag naming two or more pairs; a tag naming one pair is unaffected, since all and any are the same condition there.

- Reading a path refuses one that is not a regular file, wherever the read starts ([#887](https://github.com/nao1215/filesql/issues/887)). `ExcelSheetsInFile` and `CompressionFactory.CreateReaderForFile` open the path they are given, so both blocked forever on a named pipe -- `ExcelSheetsInFile` is the call a caller makes to look at a workbook before deciding to load it, which is exactly the moment they do not yet trust the path. The same open is what an in-place save reads the source through for its compression, its text encoding and its line terminator, so a source replaced by a named pipe after the load hung `Close`, which takes no context at all. Every place this package opens a path it was given rather than one it created now goes through one helper that stats first and refuses what is not a regular file: the compression factory, the Parquet reader that takes a source's schema before an in-place save replaces it, the recorded-source reads that rebuild an ACH or Fedwire structure, and the three the loader opens directly. Grepping for `os.Open` in the package now finds the helper and nothing else on the reading side. The compression factory covers most of them; a load still refuses such a path earlier, while collecting, where it can name the entry before anything is opened. A caller who means to read a pipe opens it themselves and passes the reader to `CompressionHandler.CreateReader` or to `DBBuilder.AddReader`, where the blocking is their own and the documentation already says a read blocked inside a source cannot be interrupted. Measured on the package's own load benchmarks, plain and for each codec, the extra stat is inside the noise and the allocation counts are unchanged.

- DBBuilder.AddFS refuses a source that is not a regular file, the way a path does ([#885](https://github.com/nao1215/filesql/issues/885)). The fix that stopped a path opening a named pipe covered the two routes a path takes and missed the third, so the same directory answered at once through `AddPath` and blocked through `AddFS(os.DirFS(dir))` -- inside the open syscall, where the context does not reach. A filesystem collects its names two ways, `fs.Glob` for each supported pattern and an `fs.WalkDir` for what the globs did not reach, and a glob returns names with no entry to read a type from, so the check runs once over the collected names where the two routes have joined. `fs.Stat` follows a symbolic link on an `os.DirFS`, so a link to a regular file still loads, and an embedded filesystem holds only regular files, so nothing about `embed.FS` changes. A glob matches a directory too, and a directory named `exports.csv` is one the walk has already collected the contents of, so its own name goes rather than being refused -- which is what a path naming the same directory does. Measured on a new benchmark over a filesystem of 500 files, the collection costs 1.4% more time and 2% more allocations, which is one stat on a path that then opens and reads every one of them. A refusal this package raised inside a directory walk is also passed through rather than wrapped as an I/O failure: it is already worded and already carries its own sentinel, so wrapping said the walk had broken and made the error match `ErrIOOperation` for something that is not one.

- A write refuses a destination that is not a regular file ([#883](https://github.com/nao1215/filesql/issues/883)). The staged write replaced whatever was at the path it was told to write, so a dump onto a named pipe turned it into a regular file with nothing said -- a dump into a directory someone else had set up destroyed the pipe that was in it and reported success -- and a directory in the way failed from inside the backup step, reporting a file the caller never named: `write /out/.users.csv.bak264152240: copy_file_range: is a directory`. Both are now refused before anything is staged, naming the path and what kind of file it is: `cannot write /out/users.csv: it is a directory and a write replaces a file`. The check costs nothing, because the stat it reads is the one already there to carry the destination's permissions over, and it runs on the file a symbolic link resolves to, so an export and an in-place save both still write through a link to a regular file. The naming is shared with the load side rather than written twice, so a kind is described the same way wherever it is refused.

- A source that is not a regular file is refused rather than opened ([#881](https://github.com/nao1215/filesql/issues/881)). A load took a supported extension as proof that a path was a file, so a named pipe called `pipe.csv` was opened like any other, and opening a pipe for reading blocks until a writer opens the other end -- inside the `os.Open` syscall, where no deadline and no cancellation reach it. `OpenContext` therefore never returned, and one such entry anywhere under a scanned directory was enough: a program loading a directory it did not create had no way to time it out, which is worse than any refusal. A character device did not block, but only because it reports a size of zero and the emptiness check ran first, so the answer depended on what a device happened to say about itself. Such a path is now refused with `ErrUnsupportedFormat`, naming it and what kind of file it is, the way an empty file and an unreadable one already are; passing over it would drop something a name ending in `.csv` says the caller meant to load. A regular file reached through a symbolic link still loads, since that is what auto-save writes back through: the kind is read from the directory entry, which carries it without a stat of its own, and only a link costs the stat that resolves it. Measured on a new benchmark over a directory of 500 files, the scan is unchanged in time and allocations.

- The package documentation says how an LTSV label differs from a header cell ([#879](https://github.com/nao1215/filesql/issues/879)). The Column Name Handling section states its rules without naming a format and mentions LTSV only to say the duplicate check runs per record for the same reason, and two of the rules read differently there. A label is trimmed as it is read, so the trimming has already happened when the case fold runs rather than being a rule applied beside it: a record holding `" A:1\ta:2"` is refused as one label typed twice, where `" A"` beside `"a"` in a CSV header is two columns. And a label that is empty names its column the empty string rather than taking the name of its position, since a position names a column of a header and an LTSV record does not have to carry the labels the record before it carried. Both follow from the trim, which is deliberate and which the LTSV writer already knows about -- it refuses a column name either rule would change rather than writing a label that reads back as a different name. What was missing was the section saying so, and a test that puts a label and a header cell holding the same characters side by side.

- prep no longer documents its reader as preserving the format it read ([#877](https://github.com/nao1215/filesql/issues/877)). `Processor.Process` led its list of formats with "The returned io.Reader preserves the original file format", and three of the seven entries under it say otherwise: JSON is served as JSONL, and XLSX and Parquet as CSV. The example directly beneath named a format outright rather than reading `ProcessResult.OutputFormat`, so a caller who followed either one declared the format they read to `AddReader` and got an error about their data instead of about the mismatch -- a JSON array comes back as `failed to parse JSON: invalid character '{' after top-level value`. The bytes were always right: every one of the seven formats loads back to the table a direct load gives, once the declared format comes from the result. The sentence now says the format follows what was read and is not always the same one, and the example converts `OutputFormat` through the four values it can take. A test loads a file of each of the seven formats through prep and compares the table against a direct load, which fails for exactly the three converted formats when the input format is declared instead.

- A comment in front of BEGIN or COMMIT no longer hides the keyword from the transaction guard ([#872](https://github.com/nao1215/filesql/issues/872)). The guard read a statement from its first non-whitespace character, so `/* commit the batch */ COMMIT` and `-- done\nCOMMIT` looked like neither the start nor the end of a transaction. SQLite committed them, and the guard went on counting the transaction as open, so a `Close` with auto-save on refused the save with "the database was closed while a transaction was still open" for a transaction the caller had committed -- and the rows they committed never reached the file. The same reading in the other direction left `/* batch */ BEGIN` uncounted, so a transaction still open at close was passed off as a clean close instead of being reported. A comment carrying a query tag or a note is ordinary in SQL kept in a file or built from a template, and neither symptom named the comment. Whitespace and comments -- a line comment to its newline, a block comment to its terminator, any number of either in a row, and an unterminated one to the end -- are now skipped wherever the guard reads a keyword, so a statement behind one counts as the statement it is.

- A savepoint taken while no transaction is open counts as opening one ([#873](https://github.com/nao1215/filesql/issues/873)). `SAVEPOINT batch` outside a transaction begins one in SQLite and `RELEASE batch` commits it, but the guard read neither keyword, so a database opened with `EnableAutoSave` and left with a savepoint open closed with nil and wrote the file from the rows the connection had before -- the same silence the BEGIN spelling was fixed out of. A savepoint is now counted when it is the outermost one, and the RELEASE that names it ends the transaction; a savepoint taken inside a transaction is not one of its own, so releasing that one leaves the transaction around it open, and `ROLLBACK TO` still ends nothing. The name is matched the way SQLite matches it, without regard to case and through whichever quoting it was written in, so `SAVEPOINT "the batch"` is released by `RELEASE "the batch"` and by `RELEASE [the batch]` alike, and the connection keeps the savepoints as a stack rather than one name, because SQLite lets a name be taken twice and a RELEASE then releases the innermost of them.

- Auto-save on commit saves when the transaction is committed by a COMMIT statement ([#874](https://github.com/nao1215/filesql/issues/874)). `EnableAutoSaveOnCommit` writes after each committed transaction, but the write hung off the `database/sql` transaction wrapper alone, so a transaction spelled as `BEGIN` and `COMMIT` statements -- which the guard already read well enough to count as open and then closed -- committed without saving, and the rows waited for the close-time save. Nothing was lost, but the timing is the whole of what that option offers, and a program reading the file between commits saw stale contents for as long as the database stayed open. A statement COMMIT or END now runs the same commit-time save `tx.Commit` runs, and reports it the same way when it fails. A statement ROLLBACK still saves nothing, which is the distinction the guard did not draw before: it read COMMIT, END and ROLLBACK as one effect.

- A BEGIN refused because a transaction is already open no longer stops that transaction being counted ([#875](https://github.com/nao1215/filesql/issues/875)). SQLite has no nested transaction, so a second `BEGIN` fails and the first is untouched, but the guard read the failure as the end of a transaction: it dropped the count auto-save reads and released the gate that makes transactions queue. `Close` therefore returned nil and wrote the file from the rows the connection had before the caller's still-open transaction, and a second transaction could start beside one that was open and writing -- the wait the gate exists to provide. Only the statement that opened the transaction may close it now, so a BEGIN or a savepoint run inside a transaction is an ordinary statement whose error comes back from SQLite with nothing else touched.

- A workbook written back to itself goes to the sheets it was read from, whatever they are named ([#866](https://github.com/nao1215/filesql/issues/866)). A table's name is its sheet's name run through the table-name sanitizer, which turns spaces, hyphens and dots into underscores, prefixes a leading digit and drops whatever is left over, and the save spelled the sheet back out of the table name -- which that mapping cannot do. A workbook whose sheet was named `Q1 Sales`, `Q1-Sales`, `Rev.1`, `2024` or `(draft)` therefore could not be saved at all: the save asked for a sheet the file never held and failed with `sheet Q1_Sales does not exist`, from `Close`, which callers routinely defer and discard. The failure took the whole workbook with it, so in a file where one sheet was named plainly and another was not, the edits to both were gone. Sheet names carrying a space and year sheets named for the year are ordinary spreadsheets, and only a sheet whose name is already a valid table name -- `Orders`, `Q1_Sales`, a name in any script -- was saveable. The save now asks the workbook it is about to replace which sheet became which table, running the same function the load ran, so the two agree by construction and no reverse mapping is needed. The reverse mapping is still what names a sheet for a table that has none.

- A table of a workbook with no sheet of its own becomes a new sheet instead of failing the save ([#867](https://github.com/nao1215/filesql/issues/867)). The check for whether a sheet was already there read the error from excelize's `GetSheetIndex`, but that reports a missing sheet as index -1 with no error and errors only on a name no sheet could carry, so the branch that exists to create the sheet was unreachable and a real error was swallowed in its place. A table created during a workbook session is picked up by the save because its name carries the workbook's prefix, found no sheet, and failed the save with `sheet Extra does not exist` -- discarding the edits to the sheets that did exist along with the new table. The index is what says whether the sheet is there, so it is what is read now, and an unusable name is reported rather than passed off as a sheet to create. The same shape at the one other call site, the check that removes the default sheet from a workbook this package built, reads the index too.

- A Parquet file written back to itself keeps the schema it declared ([#864](https://github.com/nao1215/filesql/issues/864)). The save built its schema from three shapes -- int64, double and string, every one of them optional -- so a save with nothing edited turned a DECIMAL into a string, a TIMESTAMP, a DATE and a BOOLEAN into integers, and a required column into a nullable one. The values were unaffected, so a reload through this package said nothing was wrong; a Parquet file's types are what every other reader of it goes by, and a DECIMAL read as a string no longer sums while a BOOLEAN read as an integer no longer filters. Nothing in the database remembers the types, so the file is now read for its schema before it is replaced and each value is rebuilt from the text its column holds. A column keeps its type only when every one of its values, rebuilt and rendered again the way a load renders it, comes back as itself; one that does not is a value the caller set that the type cannot hold, and narrowing it would write a different number into their file -- `999999999999.99` in a DECIMAL(9,2) became `2764472.31` under a conversion without the check -- so the column is written the way an export writes it instead. The widths an integer annotation names and a decimal's precision are checked as well, because nothing downstream enforces either. A required column holding a null the caller set becomes optional, which is the one thing the data demands. Measured on a new benchmark over 50,000 rows of seven typed columns, the save costs 1.19x the time and 1.17x the memory it did, and an export, which has no file to keep faith with, is unchanged.

- A cell an in-place save changes is written as what its column holds rather than always as text ([#862](https://github.com/nao1215/filesql/issues/862)). Every cell was written as a string, which is the rule an export follows so that one table dumped twice does not disagree with itself. In a workbook that meant changing one value of a numeric column left that one cell as text while its neighbors, which the save left alone, stayed numbers -- so a spreadsheet summing, averaging or charting the column read it one row short, and sorting put the edited row after every number. The reload through this package was unaffected, since the value was still there and the column was still inferred as INTEGER, so nothing said it had happened. A value of a column SQLite declares INTEGER or REAL is now written as the number it spells, through an int64 where the digits ask for one so a value past what a float64 holds exactly is not rounded on its way into the file. The line stays where the unchanged-cell comparison already draws it: a spelling this package keeps as text -- a zero-padded code, a literal past int64, a number with padding around it -- is written as text, and so is every value of a text column.

- A call with an argument count no form of the function accepts is refused under the name the caller wrote ([#858](https://github.com/nao1215/filesql/issues/858)). A lowering renames a function to the helper that computes it without counting the arguments, so `SELECT TIME()` under the MySQL dialect translated and then failed from the caller's next Query with `wrong number of arguments to function mysql_time_of_day` -- a name that appears nowhere in the query they wrote. `LEFT` with one argument, `TIMEDIFF` with one and `WEEKOFYEAR` with none read the same way. The count is knowable while the caller's own spelling is still at hand, so `Translate` now refuses with `ErrUnsupportedSyntax` naming the function and how many arguments it takes. The count comes from the table of helpers the lowering layer keeps, which now records it, and the test that already held that table's names against the runtime's holds the counts too. A function whose count varies is registered as taking any number and is untouched by this, so `FORMAT`, `ROUND`, `SUBSTRING`, `LOG`, `LOCATE`, `WEEK` and `CHAR` still translate at every arity they accept.
- A workbook written back to itself keeps its rows where they were when the sheet holds a row with no cell in it ([#859](https://github.com/nao1215/filesql/issues/859)). A sheet may hold such a row under a title, or between two blocks of records, and the load passes over it, so the table's row N is not the sheet's row N+1. The save wrote the header at row 1 and the records under it, which landed every cell on a row belonging to a different record: the comparison that recognizes an untouched cell never matched, so every cell was written -- as a string, which is what this writer writes -- and a date cell nothing edited stopped being a date and took whichever number format the cell it landed on carried. The blank row disappeared and the table moved up with it. A save with no edit at all was enough to do it, and the save reported success. Each table now goes back to the rows it came from, so a sheet nothing edited comes back as it was and an edited cell is the only one that changes. Where the table sits is worked out by one function that both the load and the save call, since the load and the save disagreeing about it is what caused this.

- A numeric literal ending in its decimal point keeps the space between it and the word after it ([#854](https://github.com/nao1215/filesql/issues/854)). `SELECT a FROM t WHERE c > 1. AND a = 1` translated to `... WHERE c > 1.AND a = 1`, which SQLite reads as the single token `1.AND` and refuses; the same happened before any word that followed such a literal, under all three dialects. A trailing decimal point is legal in MySQL, PostgreSQL, GoogleSQL and SQLite alike, so a caller wrote a query every engine accepts and got back an error about a token they had never written. The renderer decided the separator by reading the last byte it had written and treating a dot as the dot that qualifies a name; the qualifying dot now holds the name after it through the writer's own `tight` flag instead, so a number's dot is just the end of a number.

- A qualified star written with whitespace around its dot is no longer given an alias ([#855](https://github.com/nao1215/filesql/issues/855)). `SELECT t. * FROM t` translated to `SELECT t.* AS "t. *" FROM t`, which SQLite refuses with a syntax error at the `AS`, and a star split across lines -- ordinary in formatted SQL -- did the same. An unaliased select item carries its original text as an alias when lowering rewrites it, so the caller's column keeps the name it had; the check was whether the source text differed from the rendering, and whitespace around the dot makes those differ for an expression that names no single column and can carry no alias. A star is now exempt, and an item that does name a column still gets its alias.

- A string literal or a quoted name holding a NUL byte is refused rather than written into a statement SQLite cannot read ([#856](https://github.com/nao1215/filesql/issues/856)). The MySQL and GoogleSQL escape `\0` decodes to that byte, and the byte was then written straight into the translated statement; SQLite reads a statement up to the first NUL, so `SELECT '\0' = b FROM t` came back as a successful translation that failed later with `unrecognized token: "'"` -- a message about the opening quote rather than about the escape. Rendering it as a cast from a blob would parse and then answer differently, since SQLite's `length` stops at a NUL where MySQL's `LENGTH('a\0b')` answers 3, so the translation is refused with `ErrUnsupportedSyntax` instead. Every other control character those escapes produce -- `\Z` is 0x1A, `\b` is 0x08 -- still goes through unchanged.

- A second transaction queues for the first rather than parking inside the driver ([#851](https://github.com/nao1215/filesql/issues/851)). SQLite runs one write transaction at a time and `modernc.org/sqlite` waits for its turn on a mutex of its own -- it registers an `sqlite3_unlock_notify` callback and blocks, with no deadline and no context anywhere in the path -- so a `BeginTx` carrying a 500ms deadline was still running ten seconds later and the caller had no way to fail the work. A busy timeout does not reach it either, because shared-cache table contention raises `SQLITE_LOCKED` rather than `SQLITE_BUSY`. Transactions now queue in this package instead, where waiting is a channel receive: the second one waits for the first and returns the context's error if the wait outlives the context, and runs normally as soon as the first commits or rolls back. Only transactions queue. Statements outside one are left alone, because this package cannot tell a transaction that has written from one that has only read, and making statements wait would deadlock the ordinary shape of holding a transaction open and querying the same database beside it. What that leaves is now written down in the package documentation: while a writing transaction or an unclosed rows iterator holds a lock, a statement touching the same table -- `DumpDatabase` included -- still waits inside SQLite, and no context ends that wait.

- A transaction begun with a `sql.TxOptions` this package cannot honor is refused rather than taken and dropped ([#849](https://github.com/nao1215/filesql/issues/849)). `db.BeginTx(ctx, &sql.TxOptions{ReadOnly: true})` returned a transaction that went on to accept an INSERT, so a caller who declared a transaction read-only as a guard rail had none, and with auto-save on, the rows it wrote reached the file at close. Every isolation level was accepted as well, up to `sql.LevelLinearizable`, though SQLite runs serializable transactions and has nothing else to give. `database/sql` refuses both on its own for a driver that implements only the legacy `Begin`, and that safety net disappears the moment a driver implements `ConnBeginTx`, which this one does. A read-only transaction is now held under SQLite's `query_only` pragma for as long as it runs, so it reads and refuses to write, and the permission comes back when it commits or rolls back -- including on a handle from `OpenReadOnly`, which was read-only already and stays so. A level other than `sql.LevelDefault` and `sql.LevelSerializable` is refused with `ErrDatabaseOperation` naming it. All four handle kinds -- plain, auto-save, dialect and read-only -- answer the same way, because every one of them now opens its connections through one wrapper.

- Auto-save no longer reports success and writes the stale file when the transaction was begun by a BEGIN statement ([#850](https://github.com/nao1215/filesql/issues/850)). A transaction begun with `db.BeginTx` and left open at close already stopped the save and said so; one begun by running BEGIN through `Exec` was invisible to that guard, so Close returned nil and wrote the rows the file already held. The caller's statements had run, a SELECT on the same handle read them back, and closing the pooled connection rolled them back before the save read the tables -- with nothing anywhere in the sequence reporting a problem. A BEGIN run as a statement, in any of SQLite's spellings and whether run directly or from a prepared statement, now counts the connection's transaction as open, and COMMIT, END and a bare ROLLBACK release it; `ROLLBACK [TRANSACTION] TO` a savepoint does not, since it leaves the transaction around it open. Savepoints themselves are not counted. The count is deliberately biased toward refusing: a save that was skipped is recoverable with `DumpDatabase`, and rows written over the caller's own are not.

- MySQL's NULLIF compares its two arguments the way MySQL compares them ([#810](https://github.com/nao1215/filesql/issues/810)). SQLite's own `nullif()` was answering, and it compares by storage class, so a string was never equal to a number: `NULLIF('abc', 0)` answered 'abc' where MySQL answers NULL, `NULLIF('', 0)` answered the empty string, and `NULLIF('1', 1)` answered '1'. A caller using NULLIF to blank out a sentinel in a text column therefore kept the sentinel, and nothing said so. The comparison now follows MySQL's "=": numerically as soon as either side is a number, where a string that spells no number reads as zero, and by a case-folding collation when both sides are strings, which is the reading FIELD here is already on. Checked against mysql:8.4 over every pair of a thirty-value matrix: the only remaining differences are the spelling of a decimal literal written in the query, which SQLite has no exact decimal to hold.

- A first column name that begins with a byte-order mark is refused by the text dump formats rather than written to a file that loads under a different name ([#847](https://github.com/nao1215/filesql/issues/847)). Such a name is what a file with a blank first line before a marked header loads as: the mark is not at the front of the file there, so it is a character the file wrote. Dumping that table as CSV, TSV or LTSV put the name at the front of the file, where the next load took the mark for the encoding mark and dropped it, and the dump reported no error. None of the three can escape it -- TSV and LTSV have no quoting, and Go's CSV writer does not quote a field for a mark -- so it is refused with `ErrUnsupportedFormat` naming the column, the way a tab in a TSV value already is, and the message points at XLSX and Parquet, which carry the name inside a container. The advice a refused dump gives is no longer always CSV: it was, which for a CSV dump read as advice to do what had just failed.

- A file carrying more than one byte-order mark names its first column the same as a file carrying one ([#846](https://github.com/nao1215/filesql/issues/846)). A mark was stripped once, so a doubly marked file -- what a program that marks text already carrying a mark writes, and what re-exporting a CSV a spreadsheet already marked produces -- left the second mark in the first column's name, which is the failure stripping exists to prevent: a caller matching the column by name found nothing, and the name printed the same as the one they asked for. Dumping such a table wrote the mark back at the front of the file, where the next load read it as the encoding mark and dropped it, so the same data named its first column two ways. The marks are dropped now for every text format, by `skipByteOrderMarks` in `internal/reader`, which is the one place the loading path and the parsing path both pass through; the single strips in front of it stay only where they still do something, and `parser.skipUTF8BOM` is gone. A U+FEFF anywhere else is a character the file wrote and is kept, in a later column's name and inside a value alike, and a file whose only content is marks and a newline is empty -- which is what the same file with one mark already was.

- A double dash opens a MySQL line comment only when a blank follows it, and a GoogleSQL backtick-quoted identifier takes the escapes BigQuery gives it ([#833](https://github.com/nao1215/filesql/issues/833)). The lexer applied one rule to all three dialects in both places. MySQL asks for a blank or a control character after the dashes, so `SELECT 1--1` there is one minus negative one and answers 2, where this read a comment and answered 1: everything after the dashes was dropped, nothing raised, and a query subtracting a negative number -- or `SELECT a--b FROM t` over two columns -- answered the left operand, which is the kind of answer that reads as correct. BigQuery lists the string escape sequences among what a backtick-quoted identifier accepts, an escaped backtick with them, so `` SELECT `a\`b` `` closed the name early under the GoogleSQL dialect. Both rules are per dialect now: PostgreSQL and GoogleSQL still open a comment on a double dash whatever follows, and a MySQL backtick still has no escapes, where a backtick is doubled and mysql:8.4 refuses the backslash form as a syntax error.

- FIELD compares its arguments the way MySQL compares them ([#839](https://github.com/nao1215/filesql/issues/839)). Every argument was read as text, so `FIELD(0, 'x')` answered 0 where MySQL answers 1 and `FIELD(0, 'x', '0')` answered 2 where MySQL answers 1: MySQL compares as strings only when every argument is one, and numerically otherwise, where a string that does not spell a number reads as zero. A caller ordering rows with `ORDER BY FIELD(col, 'a', 'b')` over a numeric column therefore got a different order than the same query gives on MySQL, and 0 for a miss is what a genuine miss looks like, so nothing said the answer had changed. The comparison now follows the arguments, two integers are compared as integers so a pair past what a double holds exactly does not match itself into the wrong position, and the string comparison folds case, which is what MySQL's default collation does and what STRCMP and LIKE here already do.

- FORMAT groups the decimal MySQL groups rather than the exact value of the double ([#841](https://github.com/nao1215/filesql/issues/841)). `FORMAT(1e100, 2)` printed the hundred digits the binary number actually holds -- 10,000,000,000,000,000,159,028,911,... -- where MySQL prints 1 followed by 100 zeros, and any value whose exact decimal runs longer than the one the caller wrote read the same way: `FORMAT(1e20 + 65536, 0)` was 100,000,000,000,000,065,536 where MySQL answers 100,000,000,000,000,070,000. The number is now the shortest decimal that reads back as the same double, so the places past it are filled with zeros rather than with the double's own digits. A half also goes to the even neighbor now, which is what MySQL does for a floating-point argument and the rule ROUND here is already on: `FORMAT(2.5e0, 0)` is 2 where it was 3, `FORMAT(1234.5e0, 0)` is 1,234 where it was 1,235, and `FORMAT(3.5e0, 0)` is 4 as before. Measured on the package's own benchmark, five runs: 1297ns and 592 B/op before, 1292ns and 609 B/op after.

- The MySQL string functions that stay on SQLite's own built-ins read their argument the way MySQL does ([#838](https://github.com/nao1215/filesql/issues/838)). TRIM, LTRIM, RTRIM, LOCATE, INSTR, CONCAT_WS, GROUP_CONCAT, LENGTH and CHAR_LENGTH are translated to the SQLite function of the same meaning rather than to a helper of this package, because SQLite trims, joins and counts the way MySQL does. What it does not do the same way is convert a REAL to text, which happens before any of them runs, so `TRIM(v)` over a column holding 1e15 answered "1000000000000000.0" where MySQL answers "1e15", `LENGTH` of it was 18 rather than 4, and `LOCATE('e', v)` answered 0 because SQLite's spelling has no "e" in it where MySQL answers 2. The argument is now wrapped in a conversion rather than the call being renamed, so the operation stays SQLite's and only the value it reads changes; a string literal is left as the caller wrote it, so `INSTR(v, 'e')` carries the wrap on one argument. With this, every MySQL string function reads a REAL the way MySQL does.

- A REAL is written the way MySQL writes one wherever a MySQL string function reads it ([#809](https://github.com/nao1215/filesql/issues/809)). Every non-integer SQLite holds is a double, so a numeric column reaching a string function goes through one conversion, and that conversion was PostgreSQL's for all three dialects: `CONCAT(v, '')` over a column holding 1e308 answered "1e+308" where MySQL answers "1e308", `TO_BASE64` and `MD5` encoded the longer text, `REVERSE` reversed it, and `LIKE '1e%'` matched a column holding 1e-5, which MySQL writes as 0.00001. The three engines spell the same double three ways and disagree in both directions -- for 1e-5 MySQL writes 0.00001, PostgreSQL writes 1e-05 and SQLite writes 1.0e-05 -- so this is a conversion per dialect rather than one to correct. MySQL's is now its own: no plus sign and no padding in an exponent, the plain spelling out to 1e14 and down to 1e-15, and an exponent past either bound or wherever the plain spelling would run past 24 characters.

- A JSON array element past the record bound is refused without reading the rest of the stream ([#836](https://github.com/nao1215/filesql/issues/836)). The reader counted the bytes after handing the source whatever slice the decoder asked for, and it kept serving the source after it had returned the refusal once, so the count bounded the record rather than the reading -- which is what the bound is for. `encoding/json`'s decoder sizes its own read-ahead buffer and does not stop at the first error a reader hands it, so a source that fills whatever it is given could hand over the whole remainder before the count was consulted, and how much that was belonged to the decoder's buffer growth rather than to this package: through Go 1.26 it stayed near the bound and the existing test passed, and on Go 1.27 a body forty times the bound was read to its end. The slice handed to the source is now capped at what the element is still allowed, plus the one byte that proves the limit was passed, and the refusal is sticky, so a decoder that asks again is told no without another byte leaving the source. The element's length is also measured from where the element begins -- the decoder's input offset at the boundary -- rather than from the last read, so the read-ahead that belongs to the elements after it is not charged to the one being decoded and a file of short records is not refused for the length of its neighbors. Go 1.27.0 joins the unit test matrix, since it is what surfaced this.

## [0.52.0] - 2026-08-29

### Breaking Changes

- a query outside the supported subset is now refused rather than forwarded to SQLite. The three refusals are `ErrInvalidSyntax` for a query that cannot be read, `ErrUnsupportedSyntax` for a construct SQLite cannot express, and the new `ErrUnsupportedFeature` for one this package does not model. A query that holds only comments, or nothing at all, is refused for the same reason: there is no statement in it, which is what MySQL and PostgreSQL answer for the same input. Callers that relied on an unrecognized statement reaching SQLite -- `SHOW TABLES`, `GRANT`, `CREATE SEQUENCE`, a `CREATE TABLE ... LIKE` -- now get an error naming the construct instead of one naming SQLite's parser.

- comments are dropped from the translated SQL. They carry nothing SQLite acts on, the syntax tree does not model them, and the previous renderer had to defuse a nested comment's delimiters to keep its own output parseable. A translated query is now the statement without them.

### Changed

- The dialect translation is a compiler frontend rather than a sequence of token rewrites. A query is now read by a lexer into a parser that builds a typed syntax tree, the tree is lowered into one SQLite can execute, and a renderer writes the SQLite SQL; the token-rewrite passes and everything that supported them are gone. The reason is that the passes had no tree to work on, so each one that needed an operand rediscovered it by walking tokens outward and guessing where the operand began and ended -- the package carried five separate functions for that -- and three kinds of defect followed from it. Operator precedence lived in the passes rather than in one place, so the order the passes ran in was part of the language's meaning: MOD had to be rewritten before DIV, "!" before "^", the typed-literal pass before the interval pass, and every new construct had to be inserted at the right point in a sequence whose constraints were recorded only in comments. An operand boundary found by scanning is a guess, and the guesses were wrong at the edges: SOUNDS LIKE, LIKE ANY, the PostgreSQL bit-string literal and the "#-" operator each produced SQL that could not parse. And anything no pass recognized was handed to SQLite unchanged, which made the supported language accidental -- a construct "worked" whenever SQLite happened to accept the same text, with whatever meaning SQLite gives it, so CREATE TABLE x LIKE y was accepted and built a table with no columns. Precedence is now one table per dialect inside the parser, the lowering rules read a node and its children rather than neighboring tokens, and a construct outside the supported subset is refused by name with the line and column it was written at. The subset is listed in the package documentation and is the contract.

- Translation is faster and allocates a third of what it did, because a tree is built once where the passes copied the whole token slice on every one of them. Measured on the package's own benchmark, three runs each: MySQL 19.1us and 91.6 KB per translation before, 13.4us and 19.6 KB after; PostgreSQL 14.9us and 74.5 KB before, 11.1us and 16.5 KB after; GoogleSQL 12.8us and 48.0 KB before, 11.2us and 16.9 KB after.

- The translated SQL is spelled the way a reader would spell it. An identifier keeps its bare form unless the caller quoted it or it collides with a SQLite keyword, where before every name was written in quotes; a sign stays against its operand; and the parentheses a rewrite used to add around every operand are gone, since the renderer parenthesizes from the tree instead. `SELECT a DIV b` is `SELECT CAST(mysql_divide(a, b) AS INTEGER)` rather than `SELECT CAST(mysql_divide((a), (b)) AS INTEGER)`.

- Review of the rewrite found six constructs the old passes translated into SQL that ran and answered something else, and they are fixed here rather than carried over. `SELECT 1 UNION SELECT 2 INTERSECT SELECT 3` grouped as SQLite groups it rather than as the source dialect does, because SQLite has no precedence among the compound operators and reads them left to right; the nested operation is written as a subquery now. PostgreSQL reads concatenation, the bitwise operators and the JSON operators below addition and SQLite reads them above, so `a || b * c` concatenated the wrong thing. `LIKE` with an `ESCAPE` clause stayed on SQLite's own `LIKE`, whose folding stops at ASCII and is on by default, so under PostgreSQL `'A' LIKE 'a' ESCAPE '!'` was true where PostgreSQL answers false; the helper takes the escape character now. `CONVERT(x USING binary)` and its `ucs2`, `utf16` and `utf32` siblings were dropped as though they asked for the encoding the value already had. A `FILTER` or a `DISTINCT` on one of the aggregates that expands into an expression was dropped, so `VAR_POP(x) FILTER (WHERE y > 0)` computed over every row; both are refused now. `FETCH FIRST ROW ONLY` lost its limit and returned every row. `ON CONFLICT (a) WHERE a > 0` lost the predicate that picks which partial index the upsert resolves against. `ON DELETE SET NULL` was truncated after the `SET` and the `NULL` became a column constraint the caller never wrote. And a short `U&'\+41424'` escape read past the end of the string and panicked, where a query is untrusted text and has to be an error.

- A second review round found four more, fixed with the same measurement against the engines. `CONVERT(x USING utf8)` was dropped as a no-op, but MySQL's `utf8` is `utf8mb3`, which holds three bytes at most and cannot carry a character outside the basic plane; only `utf8mb4` is dropped now. `U&'\D83D\DE00'` wrote each half of the surrogate pair on its own and produced two replacement characters instead of the emoji; the halves are read together now, and a half with no partner and the zero character are refused, which is what PostgreSQL does with them. A `LIKE` pattern that ends in its own escape character raises under PostgreSQL and reads the character as itself under MySQL, and both readings are implemented, reported where the walk reaches the escape so that `'ab' LIKE 'ab!' ESCAPE '!'` answers false rather than raising, as PostgreSQL answers. And `INSERT INTO t VALUE (1)`, MySQL's singular spelling, read `VALUE` as the table's alias; an insert target takes an alias only after `AS`, which is how every dialect writes one.

- An alias the caller left out is written in. A name standing against a quoted one is an alias in every dialect that allows it, so MySQL's `SELECT a`b`` comes out as `SELECT a AS "b"` rather than as two tokens whose relationship the reader has to work out. MySQL's `LIMIT 5, 10` comes out as `LIMIT 10 OFFSET 5` for the same reason: one spelling, and the one that says which number is which.

### Fixed

- A file wider than a table can hold is refused in this package's words ([#829](https://github.com/nao1215/filesql/issues/829)). SQLite holds 2000 columns and the limit is fixed when it is compiled, so a wider file cannot be loaded whatever this package does; what reached the caller was the failed `CREATE TABLE`, which answered `SQL logic error: too many columns on _filesql_stage_t` -- a driver message naming the staging table this package builds for a reader, with no limit, no count and no sentinel to match beyond the one every failed statement carries. It is `ErrUnsupportedFormat` now, naming both numbers, for every format that builds a header: the delimited readers, LTSV, whose columns come from the labels its records carry, the sheet reader and the Parquet reader.

- A record longer than the bound is refused whatever the format ([#827](https://github.com/nao1215/filesql/issues/827)). LTSV held to no bound at all, so a stream sending one record with no terminator was read however long it was: a 200 MiB record loaded and answered a table, allocating 954 MiB on the way, where CSV, TSV and JSONL refuse the same input at 64 MiB. A JSON document that is not an array had none either, and an ACH file was held whole by the library that reads it. All three are read against the bound now and refuse with `ErrRecordTooLong`, naming the record. The bound is on the record and not on the file, so a large LTSV log of short records still loads: a file cannot be longer than itself, which is what separates it from a stream. The same 200 MiB record now costs 233 MiB for LTSV, 233 MiB for the JSON document and 176 MiB for ACH.

- A header row holding blank cells loads, and a blank header takes the name of its position ([#824](https://github.com/nao1215/filesql/issues/824)). Two or more blank cells were refused with `ErrDuplicateColumn`, naming a column name the file never wrote: one blank header became a column whose name was the empty string and a second one collided with it, so a spreadsheet exported with spacer columns, or a CSV whose header row ends in two commas, could not be loaded at all. A blank header names nothing, so there was no name typed twice to refuse. It is now `column_2`, `column_4` and so on by position, moved along to `column_2_2` when the file wrote a column of that name itself, which also gives the caller something to write in a query where the name used to be `""`. `ErrDuplicateColumn` keeps the meaning the documentation gives it: a name the file wrote twice. A header cell holding a space is a name the file wrote and is unchanged.

- A row whose values are all empty survives a dump to XLSX and the load back ([#825](https://github.com/nao1215/filesql/issues/825)). A workbook stores no cell for an empty value, so such a row held no cell and the reader passed it over along with the space under a sheet's data -- the rows a workbook has by the million when one stray cell sits near the bottom. The two are different things and the file says which is which, so the sheet's own XML is read for the rows that hold a cell rather than asking the library, which drops a cell whose value is the empty string. A whitespace-only cell in a numeric column is stored as NULL and reached the same loss. The scan reads bytes rather than XML tokens and runs once per sheet: on the workbook benchmarks it costs 114.6ms against 113.7ms and 71.1 MB against 71.0 MB.

- A text value carrying a zero byte reaches a dialect helper whole ([#772](https://github.com/nao1215/filesql/issues/772)). The driver reads a TEXT argument as a C string unless the registration asks for volatile arguments, so a value SQLite holds as `a\x00b` reached a helper as `a`: `HEX` of a value beginning with a zero byte was the empty string where SQLite's own `hex()` answered `00`, and `LENGTH`, `UPPER`, `REVERSE`, `SUBSTRING` and `REPLACE` all answered about a shorter string than the one in the database. MySQL stores a zero byte inside a string and a CSV cell can carry one, so a query mixing this package's helpers with SQLite's built-ins got two answers about the same cell. The registration asks for volatile arguments now and copies every string and byte slice before the helper sees it, which is the allocation the driver would have made itself with them off.

- `to_date` and `to_timestamp` read their template rather than translating it into a Go layout, and refuse a value PostgreSQL refuses ([#777](https://github.com/nao1215/filesql/issues/777), [#779](https://github.com/nao1215/filesql/issues/779)). A Go layout cannot spell a day of the year beside a year, an ISO week date, a Julian day, a zone offset or a number written without its padding, and PostgreSQL reads all five; each answered NULL, which is also the answer for a value that does not match, so a caller could not tell an unimplemented pattern from a bad row -- and `to_char` formatted every one of them, so a value this package wrote could not be read back by it. `to_date('March 5, 2024', 'Month DD, YYYY')`, `to_date('2024-065', 'YYYY-DDD')`, `to_date('2024 W10 2', 'IYYY "W"IW ID')`, `to_date('2460375', 'J')`, `to_date('2,024-03-05', 'Y,YYY-MM-DD')` and `to_date('2024-3-5', 'YYYY-MM-DD')` all answer the fifth of March now, and every pattern `to_char` writes is read back by the pattern that wrote it. A template PostgreSQL itself refuses is refused here for the same reason it is there: a day of the year with no year beside it, and a template naming a field of the Gregorian calendar and one of the ISO week date at once. A value PostgreSQL refuses is an error rather than NULL: a thirteenth month, the thirtieth of February, a year that is not a number. `to_number` follows the same rule, and answers NULL for an empty template, which names no digit positions -- scraping the digits out of the value instead answered `to_number('2024-02-29', '')` as -20240229, the digits of a date run together with the hyphens read as a sign ([#811](https://github.com/nao1215/filesql/issues/811)).

- `STR_TO_DATE` reads `%j` and `%f`, the two specifiers `DATE_FORMAT` writes and the parser did not, so a value this package formatted could not be read back by it ([#778](https://github.com/nao1215/filesql/issues/778)). MySQL refuses an empty regular expression rather than matching everywhere with one, which is what Go's regexp does and what this answered: `REGEXP_LIKE(x, '')` was true for every row, including the rows the query was written to find, and the same for `REGEXP_SUBSTR`, `REGEXP_INSTR`, `REGEXP_REPLACE` and the `REGEXP` operator. `DATE_FORMAT` with an empty format answers NULL rather than the empty string ([#810](https://github.com/nao1215/filesql/issues/810)).

- A REAL is written into a text dump the way SQLite renders it ([#815](https://github.com/nao1215/filesql/issues/815)). Go's shortest `'g'` leaves the plain form as soon as the decimal exponent reaches six, so a CSV holding 2500000 came back as 2.5e+06 with nothing changed in between, and the same database disagreed with itself about the value, since `CAST(c AS TEXT)` answers SQLite's own rendering. The plain form now runs from a ten-thousandth up to a hundred quadrillion, which is where SQLite leaves it; outside that the engines disagree with each other and the shortest form is as good an answer as any.

- A decimal a float64 cannot hold keeps its digits instead of becoming an infinity ([#816](https://github.com/nao1215/filesql/issues/816)). A cell holding 1e400 was loaded as +Inf and the number in the file was gone, while an integer too large for an int64 stays TEXT and survives; the two overflows are the same situation and are handled the same way now. Underflow follows, since a stored zero cannot be told from a measured one. The one out-of-range spelling that stays REAL is `9e999`, which is what a dump writes for an infinity and the only text SQLite's REAL affinity reads back as one.

- `coerce=int` and `coerce=float` rewrite how a number is written and not what it says ([#793](https://github.com/nao1215/filesql/issues/793)). They read a value with Go's number syntax rather than the file's, so 0777 became 777, 007 became 7 and 1_000 became 1000 while 0x10, 0b11 and 0o17 were left alone. A zero-padded code -- a postal code, an account number, a permission mask -- lost its leading zeros and became a different number, and a column the loader would have typed TEXT for exactly that reason became an INTEGER one, so the type changed depending on whether the caller ran prep first. Those spellings are refused now, along with a literal past int64 and a decimal a float64 cannot hold, which came back as the smallest integer there is and as an exact zero. `coerce=bool` normalized on, yes, off and no -- which `strconv.ParseBool` does not accept -- and left t, T, f and F, which are exactly the spellings it does accept, unchanged; all of them are normalized now ([#792](https://github.com/nao1215/filesql/issues/792)).

- The PostgreSQL operators that are more than one character and are not spelled by SQLite ([#781](https://github.com/nao1215/filesql/issues/781), [#790](https://github.com/nao1215/filesql/issues/790), [#791](https://github.com/nao1215/filesql/issues/791), [#812](https://github.com/nao1215/filesql/issues/812)). The lexer did not know `#-`, `|/` and `||/`, so it split each one at the longest operator it did know and left the rest behind: `doc #- '{a}'` came out as a bitwise XOR beside a negation and answered NULL, and `|/ 25` came out as a bitwise OR beside a division, which is not a query. All three are one token now, and the two roots and the prefix `@` are the functions they mean -- `sqrt`, `cbrt` and `abs`. The `||` and `-` operators mean one thing beside a document and another beside a number or a string, and both reached SQLite's own operators and answered from the wrong operation: `'{"a":1}'::jsonb - 'a'` subtracted a key from an object as though both were numbers and answered 0, and two documents concatenated as text. A document is recognized the way the date arithmetic recognizes a date, by a cast to json or jsonb or a literal written as one, which is the form PostgreSQL itself needs for these operators to resolve; the deletions become `json_remove` with the key, the index or the `{a,b}` path written as the `$` path SQLite removes by, and `||` on a document is refused naming the operator, because it merges two objects, concatenates two arrays and wraps a scalar and SQLite's `json_patch` does none of the three. A `#-` path element that is a number is refused for a related reason: PostgreSQL reads it against the container it lands on, as a key on an object and an index on an array, and one `$` path cannot be both. `text || text` and `number - number` are untouched. The three prefix operators are PostgreSQL's "any other operators" and bind below addition, so `@ -3 + 1` is the absolute value of -2, and `@` is not a parameter prefix in that dialect, where a parameter is written `$1`. Separately, a bound parameter whose name holds a dollar sign or starts with a digit -- `@a$b`, `:1abc`, both of which SQLite reads as one name -- was split into two tokens, and the renderer's space between them made SQL that no longer parses; the name is read the way SQLite reads it now.

- The functions and aggregates each dialect has and SQLite does not are translated rather than left to fail as a missing name ([#746](https://github.com/nao1215/filesql/issues/746), [#748](https://github.com/nao1215/filesql/issues/748), [#750](https://github.com/nao1215/filesql/issues/750), [#751](https://github.com/nao1215/filesql/issues/751), [#752](https://github.com/nao1215/filesql/issues/752), [#754](https://github.com/nao1215/filesql/issues/754), [#755](https://github.com/nao1215/filesql/issues/755), [#760](https://github.com/nao1215/filesql/issues/760), [#761](https://github.com/nao1215/filesql/issues/761), [#808](https://github.com/nao1215/filesql/issues/808), [#813](https://github.com/nao1215/filesql/issues/813)). MySQL's `MAKE_SET`, `EXPORT_SET`, `INTERVAL()`, `JSON_LENGTH`, `JSON_CONTAINS`, `TO_SECONDS`, `TIMESTAMP()` and `CONVERT_TZ` and PostgreSQL's `scale`, `min_scale`, `trim_scale`, `age`, `pg_typeof`, `json_typeof`, `jsonb_typeof` and `EXTRACT(JULIAN FROM ...)` all reached SQLite under their own names and failed as unknown functions; each has a helper now, with the answers read off mysql:8.4 and postgres:17 rather than derived. `INTERVAL` is a call only when the parenthesis follows the name immediately, which is MySQL's own rule for telling it from the `INTERVAL 1 DAY` unit. `CONVERT_TZ` converts between fixed offsets and answers NULL for a named zone, which is what MySQL answers when its zone tables are not loaded. `age` borrows the days of the earlier timestamp's own month, as PostgreSQL does, so from 2024-01-31 to 2024-03-01 is a month and a day. `json_typeof` answers the names JSON defines rather than SQLite's storage classes, so a JSON string is "string" and a number is "number". `COALESCE(x)` with one argument was refused by SQLite, where both MySQL and PostgreSQL accept it as the value itself. The MySQL numeric functions -- `CEIL`, `CEILING`, `FLOOR`, `SIGN`, `SQRT`, `EXP`, `LN`, `LOG2` and `LOG10` -- answered NULL for a string MySQL reads as a number, which is what a cell loaded from a CSV file is, and `EXP` of a large argument answered +Inf, which no dump format can carry; they read the leading number of a string now and raise on overflow, and `LN` of a non-positive argument answers NULL as MySQL does. `JSON_ARRAYAGG`, `json_agg` and `jsonb_agg` build the same array SQLite's `json_group_array` builds, and PostgreSQL's `corr`, `covar_pop` and `covar_samp` reach the same expansion GoogleSQL's already had. The aggregates and functions with no SQLite form are refused by name instead: `JSON_OBJECTAGG` and `json_object_agg`, whose duplicate-key rule differs from SQLite's, the `BIT_AND`, `BIT_OR` and `BIT_XOR` family, `array_agg`, the nine `regr_*` aggregates, the ordered-set `percentile_cont`, `percentile_disc` and `mode`, the GoogleSQL functions whose result is an array such as `SPLIT` and `GENERATE_ARRAY`, and `FARM_FINGERPRINT`, whose value is a hash a different implementation would not agree on. Separately, every function that reads its argument as text wrote a REAL in exponent notation once its magnitude reached one million, because Go's shortest formatting switches there: `CONCAT(1234567.5, 'x')` answered `1.2345675e+06x`, `LPAD(1234567.5, 12, '0')` padded the wrong string, `LOCATE('5', 1234567.5)` answered a position that is not the one MySQL answers, and `SUBSTRING(1234567.5, 1, 3)` returned characters the number does not contain. Between 1e-4 and 1e15 SQLite, MySQL and PostgreSQL all write the plain form and so does filesql now; outside that band the three disagree with each other and the shortest form is kept.

- The PostgreSQL answers that were SQLite's rather than PostgreSQL's ([#736](https://github.com/nao1215/filesql/issues/736), [#738](https://github.com/nao1215/filesql/issues/738), [#739](https://github.com/nao1215/filesql/issues/739), [#740](https://github.com/nao1215/filesql/issues/740), [#741](https://github.com/nao1215/filesql/issues/741), [#742](https://github.com/nao1215/filesql/issues/742), [#743](https://github.com/nao1215/filesql/issues/743), [#744](https://github.com/nao1215/filesql/issues/744), [#774](https://github.com/nao1215/filesql/issues/774), [#775](https://github.com/nao1215/filesql/issues/775), [#776](https://github.com/nao1215/filesql/issues/776)). An `ORDER BY` put its NULLs at SQLite's end, which is the opposite of PostgreSQL's: NULLs sort last for an ascending order and first for a descending one there, and the difference is not only the order of the rows a caller reads -- `first_value`, `last_value`, `nth_value`, `lag` and `lead` all answer about a different row, and those answers travel on into the query. `random()` answered SQLite's pseudo-random 64-bit integer where PostgreSQL answers a double in [0, 1), so `WHERE random() < 0.1` selected about half the rows rather than a tenth. `quote_ident` left a non-ASCII name unquoted, which is the one answer that is not safe to paste back into SQL. `chr(0)` answered a space where PostgreSQL refuses the code point. `format()` was SQLite's printf, so `%I`, `%L` and the positional `%n$s` all answered NULL -- indistinguishable from a NULL argument -- and a printf verb PostgreSQL rejects was expanded instead; it now has PostgreSQL's own verbs. The SQL-standard `SUBSTRING(x SIMILAR p ESCAPE e)` was refused in one spelling and read as a position and a length in the other, answering NULL for every row; both spellings now return the part the pattern's escaped double quotes mark. Four `to_char` patterns copied their own letters into the result, where a caller cannot tell them from data: `TM` garbled the pattern after it so `TMMonth` answered "T03onrd", the timezone patterns `TZ`, `TZH`, `TZM` and `OF` printed their own names, `EEEE` printed itself instead of formatting in scientific notation, and the currency-code pattern `C` left a stray C in front of the number.

- MySQL's case-folding collation reaches GREATEST and LEAST, and JSON_TYPE spells its answer the way MySQL spells it ([#732](https://github.com/nao1215/filesql/issues/732), [#734](https://github.com/nao1215/filesql/issues/734)). `STRCMP`, `LIKE` and `REGEXP` already fold case under the MySQL dialect because MySQL's default collation does; `GREATEST` and `LEAST` compared byte by byte, so `GREATEST('a', 'B')` answered 'a' where MySQL answers 'B' and one dialect disagreed with itself about which of two strings is larger. `JSON_TYPE` answered SQLite's lower-case name where MySQL answers an upper-case one. `JSON_ARRAY` and `JSON_OBJECT` still print without MySQL's space after a comma and a colon: re-rendering their text would break the nesting SQLite gets right, and the values are the same, so the spelling is left as it is.

- The literals, predicates and operators each dialect spells and SQLite does not are translated rather than passed through ([#762](https://github.com/nao1215/filesql/issues/762), [#763](https://github.com/nao1215/filesql/issues/763), [#764](https://github.com/nao1215/filesql/issues/764), [#765](https://github.com/nao1215/filesql/issues/765), [#766](https://github.com/nao1215/filesql/issues/766), [#768](https://github.com/nao1215/filesql/issues/768), [#769](https://github.com/nao1215/filesql/issues/769)). MySQL's bit literal `0b1010` was split by the lexer into the number 0 and the identifier b1010, so `SELECT 0b1010` reached SQLite as a zero with a column alias and answered 0 -- while its hexadecimal sibling `0x41` was refused with a message explaining that MySQL reads it two ways. Both spellings of the bit literal are now one token and refused for the same reason the hexadecimal one is. A charset introducer -- `_utf8mb4'abc'`, `N'abc'` -- was read as a column name; one naming a UTF-8 spelling is now dropped, `_binary` becomes the literal's own bytes, and one whose bytes would genuinely differ is refused by name. PostgreSQL's `U&'...'` Unicode escape literal was read the same way and is now decoded, with its optional `UESCAPE` clause. `IS UNKNOWN` and `IS NOT UNKNOWN` become `IS NULL` and `IS NOT NULL`, which is what they mean; `= ANY (subquery)` and `= SOME (subquery)` become `IN`, `<> ALL (subquery)` becomes `NOT IN`, and a quantified comparison with no short SQLite form is refused naming the quantifier rather than failing as a parse error on the subquery's SELECT. PostgreSQL's four LIKE operator aliases translated into a doubled `REGEXP REGEXP` that could not parse, and would have applied regular-expression matching to a pattern written with LIKE wildcards if it had; they are LIKE, NOT LIKE, ILIKE and NOT ILIKE now. Its `#` bitwise XOR reached SQLite's lexer as an unknown token and its prefix `~` was read as the regular-expression match operator missing a left side. A `COLLATE` clause was passed through raw, so SQLite either raised naming a collation the caller never wrote or accepted the syntax and quietly applied nothing; a byte-order or case-insensitive name now reaches the SQLite collation that means the same, and a locale collation is refused by name.

- The cast targets are converted the way each dialect converts them ([#728](https://github.com/nao1215/filesql/issues/728), [#729](https://github.com/nao1215/filesql/issues/729), [#730](https://github.com/nao1215/filesql/issues/730), [#745](https://github.com/nao1215/filesql/issues/745), [#747](https://github.com/nao1215/filesql/issues/747), [#767](https://github.com/nao1215/filesql/issues/767)). `CAST(x AS YEAR)` mapped onto the text conversion, so the helper handed its argument straight back and a query casting a column to group by it grouped by the original values; YEAR now follows MySQL's rules, including its 1901..2155 range and the 1..69 and 70..99 shorthands. `CAST(x AS TIME)` formatted a value with no clock in it as midnight, which a caller cannot tell from a real midnight -- MySQL reads such a value as a number spelled right to left as seconds, minutes and hours, so `CAST('2024-03-05' AS TIME)` is 00:20:24, and PostgreSQL refuses it, which it now does here too. `CAST(x AS DECIMAL(p,s))` applied the scale and ignored the precision, so `CAST('2024-03-05' AS DECIMAL(4,1))` answered 2024 for a type that holds 999.9 at most; MySQL clamps to that bound and PostgreSQL raises, the same split the other conversions follow. `CONVERT(expr, type)` and the `BINARY` prefix are MySQL's other two spellings of the cast and reached SQLite unchanged, failing as a missing function and a missing column; both now reach the same helper as `CAST`, and `CONVERT(expr USING charset)` becomes the expression itself for a charset that spells UTF-8 and is refused for one whose bytes would differ, as is `CHAR(n USING charset)`. A cast to an array type is refused where both spellings pass: the `::` form wrote the brackets into the SQLite text and failed to parse while the `CAST` form dropped them and converted to the element type, so one unsupported construct failed two different ways and neither message said the word array.

- The time a value carries survives, and the date parts each dialect names are taken ([#727](https://github.com/nao1215/filesql/issues/727), [#731](https://github.com/nao1215/filesql/issues/731), [#735](https://github.com/nao1215/filesql/issues/735), [#749](https://github.com/nao1215/filesql/issues/749), [#757](https://github.com/nao1215/filesql/issues/757), [#759](https://github.com/nao1215/filesql/issues/759)). The fractional seconds a value carried were dropped on the way out, so `TIME('2024-03-05 01:02:03.123456')` answered 01:02:03, `DATE_ADD` on the same value moved the date and lost the microseconds with it, and every PostgreSQL `EXTRACT` and `to_char` template that reads a sub-second field answered zero for a timestamp that had one; a datetime that came from the caller's own data is now written with whatever fraction it had, while a clock reading still has none. `EXTRACT(MICROSECOND FROM ts)` answered the seconds and the microseconds run together, which is PostgreSQL's rule for its `MICROSECONDS` and a million-and-something times MySQL's answer. MySQL's eleven compound date parts are implemented, in `EXTRACT` and as `INTERVAL` units, where an interval value carries its fields in one string with any punctuation between them, read from the right when the value is shorter than the unit names. `MICROSECOND` and `MILLISECOND` are units wherever a unit is taken, and `DATE_TRUNC` takes `ISOYEAR` beside the `ISOWEEK` it already had. PostgreSQL's `date + integer` and `date - date` reached SQLite as arithmetic on the number the date text spells, so `'2024-03-05'::date + 1` answered 2025 and a difference of two dates answered 0; both are rewritten where the operand can be seen to be a date -- a cast to a date or timestamp type, or a literal spelling one -- which is the form PostgreSQL itself requires for the expression to compile, and which leaves ordinary arithmetic out of a helper call.

- The remainder, the integer quotient and the rounding tie are computed the way each dialect computes them ([#720](https://github.com/nao1215/filesql/issues/720), [#737](https://github.com/nao1215/filesql/issues/737), [#756](https://github.com/nao1215/filesql/issues/756)). SQLite truncates both operands to integers before taking a remainder, so `7 % 2.5` answered 1 where MySQL, PostgreSQL and GoogleSQL all answer 2.0, and `7.5 % 2` answered 1 rather than 1.5; MySQL spells the same operation three ways and only `MOD()` was right, because SQLite's own `mod()` happens to match, while the two operator spellings reached SQLite's `%`. All three MySQL spellings now reach one helper, the operands are kept as written, and a divisor counts as zero by its value rather than by the integer it truncates to, so `7 % 0.5` answers 0 instead of being refused. `div()` had the same fault from the other side -- it truncated its operands and then divided, so `div(7, 2.5)` answered 3 where PostgreSQL and BigQuery answer 2 -- and now divides first and truncates the quotient. Rounding was the third: MySQL and PostgreSQL break a tie toward the even neighbor for a floating-point argument, so `ROUND(2.5)` is 2 and `ROUND(3.5)` is 4, and the one-argument form was not rewritten at all, so every REAL column loaded from a file rounded the other way from the engine the query was written for. Both forms now reach a helper under those two dialects; BigQuery rounds away from zero as SQLite does and is unchanged. The helper rounds the shortest decimal that reads back as the value rather than the binary value itself, which is what makes `ROUND(2.675, 2)` answer 2.68 as MySQL does. What it cannot reproduce is a decimal literal written in the query, which MySQL reads as an exact decimal and rounds away from zero; SQLite has no type to tell that from a double, and the column loaded from a file is the case worth getting right.

- The string helpers answer what their dialect answers for a value at the edge of what they take ([#721](https://github.com/nao1215/filesql/issues/721), [#722](https://github.com/nao1215/filesql/issues/722), [#723](https://github.com/nao1215/filesql/issues/723), [#724](https://github.com/nao1215/filesql/issues/724), [#725](https://github.com/nao1215/filesql/issues/725), [#726](https://github.com/nao1215/filesql/issues/726), [#733](https://github.com/nao1215/filesql/issues/733), [#758](https://github.com/nao1215/filesql/issues/758)).
