A count argument now reads a string as the number its leading run spells and truncates it, which is MySQL's rule; requiring the whole string to parse as an integer made `SPACE`, `REPEAT`, `LEFT`, `RIGHT`, `LPAD`, `RPAD` and `SUBSTRING` answer NULL for the whole call whenever the count came from a text column, which is what a cell loaded from a CSV file is. `SOUNDEX` has a helper of its own, because SQLite's answers `?000` for NULL and for a value holding no letter and cuts the code to four characters where MySQL emits one digit per coded consonant; the coding rule was read off mysql:8.4 rather than from the textbook algorithm, which differs in two places. `UNHEX` reads an odd digit count as having a leading zero, so `UNHEX('ABC')` decodes `0ABC` instead of answering NULL, and it hands back bytes rather than text so a zero byte in them survives into the next function. `SUBSTRING` reads every argument before working out the range, so a NULL length makes the call NULL even when the position already put the range outside the string. `REPLACE` reaches a helper that looks at all three arguments, since SQLite short-circuits on an empty search string and answered the subject without noticing a NULL replacement. `CHAR` builds bytes under MySQL rather than the code points SQLite's `char()` builds, so `CHAR(0)` is one zero byte and `CHAR(256)` is `0x01 0x00`. `FORMAT` groups an integer from its own digits instead of widening it to a float64, which printed the wrong last digits for anything past 2^53. Under GoogleSQL a negative length is refused by all six functions that take one -- `SUBSTR`, `REPEAT`, `LPAD`, `RPAD`, `LEFT` and `RIGHT`. `LEFT` and `RIGHT` already raised, `SUBSTR` and `REPEAT` answered the empty string and `LPAD` and `RPAD` answered NULL, so a computed length that came out negative meant something different depending on which function read it. `SOUNDEX` also has a GoogleSQL helper of its own now, since BigQuery stops the code at three digits, keeps the first letter's case and sees no letter outside ASCII, none of which MySQL does.

## [0.51.0] - 2026-08-27

### Breaking Changes

- An LTSV dump of a table with no rows is refused rather than writing an empty file ([#716](https://github.com/nao1215/filesql/issues/716)). LTSV carries its labels on every record rather than in a header, so a table with no rows has nothing to write; the empty file that came of it did not only lose its own columns, it blocked the load of every file beside it, since an empty file is not a table. A database of two tables, one of them empty, dumped to LTSV wrote `empty.ltsv` at zero bytes beside a `full.ltsv` that held its rows, and `OpenContext` on that directory failed naming the empty file, so neither table could be read. The refusal carries `ErrUnsupportedFormat` and says to dump the table as CSV, which is the shape the other LTSV refusals have. CSV and TSV keep the header, Parquet keeps its schema and XLSX keeps the header row, so a table with no rows round-trips in every other format. Migration: dump such a table as CSV, or drop it before dumping. This overturns a decision recorded in a test -- that an empty file was a correct empty LTSV -- which was made without the directory load in view.

### Fixed

- `LoadInto` and `LoadIntoTx` refuse a dialect rather than dropping it ([#714](https://github.com/nao1215/filesql/issues/714)). `WithDialect` was accepted by both and then did nothing, because a dialect is a connector wrapping the database `Open` returns and cannot reach one the caller opened -- so a caller who configured PostgreSQL and loaded into their own database met SQLite's tokenizer error on their first `::` cast, about a token they had not written, with no sentinel of this package's to match and no mention of the option that had been dropped. Auto-save cannot reach that database either and has always been refused by name; the dialect is now refused the same way, carrying `ErrDatabaseOperation` and naming the dialect and the method. A builder configured with `dialect.SQLite`, or with no dialect at all, asks for no translation and is unaffected.

### Documentation

- The Memory and Streaming section says what an XLSX load costs and what the number depends on ([#712](https://github.com/nao1215/filesql/issues/712)). It read "Budget from the file size" and gave XLSX as "about 24 times the file", which is one workbook measured at one size: a workbook is read whole, so what it costs follows the cells it holds rather than the size of the file, which is a zip and shrinks with how well the sheet compressed. The same 200,000 rows cost about 26 times the file as a wide workbook and about 37 times as a workbook of one column, and against the file alone the ratio reaches 135 times for a small one-column workbook -- so a caller sizing a server for uploads from the old figure was wrong by more than a factor of four on a shape that is not unusual. The section now says to budget an XLSX load from the cells, and to bound the decompressed size before loading a workbook that came from somewhere else, since nothing here does. `TestLoadMemoryFootprintByFormat` grew a one-column workbook beside the wide one, so both shapes are printed by the command the section names and neither can drift alone.

## [0.50.0] - 2026-08-27

### Breaking Changes

- `DumpDatabase` refuses a table whose column names a load would refuse, and an XLSX dump refuses a table whose last column has no name ([#708](https://github.com/nao1215/filesql/issues/708), [#709](https://github.com/nao1215/filesql/issues/709)). Both wrote a file this package cannot read. SQLite tells `a` from `a ` and this package does not -- a table built from both would answer a query about `a` with a column the caller did not mean, so the load refuses it -- and the dump wrote the two names into one header and said nothing, leaving the caller to find out when they read their own dump: `filesql: duplicate column name: "a " (column 2)`. The check is now the load's own, `ValidateColumnNames`, asked of the same names before anything is written, and it carries `ErrDuplicateColumn`. The XLSX case is the same failure by another route: a worksheet stores cells rather than a rectangle and does not store a trailing empty value, so a header ending in a column with no name came back one cell short of the rows under it and the load refused the workbook with `row 2 has 2 cells where the header has 1`. That table is now refused with `ErrUnsupportedFormat` and the advice to dump it as CSV, which holds it; a column with no name anywhere but last is unaffected, since a worksheet keeps an empty cell that has a cell after it, and a last column named with a space is not empty and is kept. Migration: rename the columns before dumping, or dump to a format that holds them.

- `DumpDatabase` refuses a table whose name a load would spell differently, rather than writing a file that comes back under another name ([#700](https://github.com/nao1215/filesql/issues/700)). A table is written to a file named after it and a load names a table after the file, spelling a space, a hyphen and a dot as an underscore and dropping what is neither a letter, a digit, a mark nor an underscore -- so a table named `with space` was written to `with space.csv` and came back as `with_space`, and `a[b]` came back as `ab`, with nothing said about the rename and a caller's queries failing against the reloaded database. Two such tables were worse than silent: `a b` beside `a-b` dumped to two files and the directory could not be loaded at all, since both names become `a_b`, so the dump reported success and produced something this package refuses to read. The check is the loader's own rule, applied to the file name the dump is about to write, and the error names the table and the name a load would give it. Migration: rename the table before dumping it -- `ALTER TABLE "with space" RENAME TO with_space` -- or dump the rows another way. This is the same answer, and for the same reason, as the existing refusal of a name that cannot be a file name at all.

- `DBBuilder.Build` is removed ([#694](https://github.com/nao1215/filesql/issues/694)). v0.49.0 deprecated it, because `Open`, `OpenReadOnly`, `LoadInto` and `LoadIntoTx` validate the builder themselves; this release drops it. Migration: delete the `Build` call and the error check that followed it, and call the terminal method on the builder -- `filesql.NewBuilder().AddPath("users.csv").Open(ctx)` in place of `Build` then `Open`. The errors are the same ones `Build` reported, from the method that now reports them, and `SkippedRows` is read from the builder, which is what `Build` was handing back. A caller who wants the validation without loading anything has `OpenReadOnly` followed by a close, which validates the same inputs and reads nothing until a query asks for it.

### Fixed

- The blank-cell rule holds whichever route the bytes took ([#706](https://github.com/nao1215/filesql/issues/706)). A load has two paths: an input that can be read again is typed as it reads, and one that cannot -- an `io.Reader` -- is staged as text and typed once it is all there. The whitespace half of the rule was applied on the first and not the second, so the same CSV through `AddReader`, or through `LoadInto` from a reader, kept the whitespace in a column declared INTEGER while the same bytes through `AddPath` stored NULL; an upload is the route a server takes, which is where a caller can least inspect the file first. The staged path applies the rule in SQL, because it has every column as TEXT while it reads, and it asked `NULLIF(col, '')` -- the empty string alone, since SQLite's own `TRIM` strips spaces and nothing else. It now tests the cell against the characters `unicode.IsSpace` names, which is the set the typing asks about, and copies the cell rather than the trimmed text, so a value that is not blank is unchanged. This rule has now come apart between the two paths twice, so the set is written once and both sides read it.

- A cell holding only whitespace in a number column is a missing number and is stored as NULL ([#704](https://github.com/nao1215/filesql/issues/704)). It was stored as the whitespace, so a column of numbers with one padded blank answered every question wrongly: loading `v` over `" "`, `5` and `10` gave an INTEGER column whose `MAX` was the space rather than 10, since SQLite orders text above every number, whose `SUM` was 15.0 rather than 15, whose `COUNT(v)` was 3 rather than 2, and where `WHERE v IS NULL` found nothing. The same file with an empty cell answered all four correctly, which is what [#633](https://github.com/nao1215/filesql/issues/633) fixed -- and this is that defect still open for the spelling of blank a fixed-width export, a spreadsheet column and a hand-edited CSV produce. The two sides had drifted apart on what blank means: the typing asked whether the cell was empty after trimming, which is why a column holding one is a number column at all, and the load asked whether it was the empty string. They now ask one question, `infer.IsBlank`. A blank cell in a TEXT column is still what it is, a datetime column is still stored as text and left alone, and a number written with spaces around it still keeps the column TEXT, because the padding is data a fixed-width code column depends on. One visible consequence beyond the queries: a save writes such a cell as empty rather than as the whitespace, since that is what the database now holds -- an in-place save of a file whose number column carried a space after `south,` writes `south,` back with nothing after it. That is the same loss the empty cell has always taken, and the column it happens in is one whose padding the typing had already decided says nothing.

- An XLSX dump refuses a value XML cannot hold rather than changing it ([#702](https://github.com/nao1215/filesql/issues/702)). A worksheet is XML, and XML 1.0 has no way to spell a control character other than tab, line feed and carriage return, so the library writing the workbook replaced each of the others with U+FFFD: a cell holding a NUL was dumped and read back with the replacement character in place of the NUL, under a dump that reported success, and so was every character from 0x00 to 0x08, 0x0B, 0x0C and 0x0E to 0x1F. A caller had no way to detect it, which is what makes a silent substitution worse than a refusal -- and CSV, TSV, LTSV and Parquet all round-trip these values unchanged, so the loss was XLSX's alone. The refusal is the shape the delimited formats already use, naming the column and the character and pointing at CSV, which can hold what this cannot. Tab, line feed and carriage return still round-trip, since XML admits them, and so does U+007F and everything above it. This check comes out if excelize grows a way to report or refuse the substitution itself.

- A table whose name Excel cannot use as a worksheet name comes back from an XLSX dump under its own name ([#701](https://github.com/nao1215/filesql/issues/701)). A worksheet name is at most 31 characters and cannot hold `[`, `]`, `:`, `*`, `?`, `/` or `\`, so a dump writes the sheet in the spelling Excel allows; the loader appends the sheet name to the file's name whenever the two differ, and asked the question against the unadapted name, so Excel's own rules were what made them differ. A table named `quarterly_revenue_by_region_2026` -- 32 characters, an ordinary name -- was written to a sheet named `quarterly_revenue_by_region_202` and loaded back as `quarterly_revenue_by_region_2026_quarterly_revenue_by_region_202`, growing by 32 characters every round trip. A sheet is now recognized as named after the file in the spelling Excel allows as well as in the file's own, which is what the append rule already meant to say: its comment reads "It is not appended when it would only repeat the file name. That is the shape a dump produces." A workbook whose sheets are genuinely named something else keeps the suffix, so the multi-sheet naming is unchanged.

- A Parquet file costs no more than its own size, whatever its column chunks and page headers declare ([#526](https://github.com/nao1215/filesql/issues/526)). A page header is a thrift structure inside a column chunk, and the library decodes it by allocating what the structure declares before checking those bytes are there: a 473-byte file found by fuzzing declared a 98 MiB statistic, so it allocated 98 MiB and then failed with `unexpected EOF`, and paid that again on every open of the same file -- a long-running process opening it twenty times paid it twenty times. The failure was always fast and the error was always right; the price was not, and the reader's own comment says this library was chosen over the Arrow one because it refuses damaged files with time and memory bounded by the file's own size. That was the one place the second half did not hold. The footer's declared length was already checked before the library was handed anything, and now the column chunk metadata is too -- an offset or a length that leaves the file is refused naming the chunk -- and the page headers inside each chunk are walked first, refusing the first one that declares more than the chunk holds. The walk allocates nothing for what a field declares: it steps past a field by arithmetic where the format allows it, and reads only the header bytes themselves, so it reads a few hundred bytes per page and no page data. The two halves of the class are separate and both are covered: the 473-byte file is caught by the chunk bounds, and a file whose chunk metadata is consistent while a page header inside it declares 100 MiB -- which no metadata check can see -- is caught by the walk, costing 38 KiB where it cost 100 MiB. `BenchmarkOpenParquet` over 100,000 rows does not move. This check comes out if parquet-go grows an option bounding what a decoded page header may declare.

## [0.49.0] - 2026-08-27

### Breaking Changes

- The `prep.Stream` interface is removed, and `ProcessResult` carries an `OutputFormat` field in its place ([#690](https://github.com/nao1215/filesql/issues/690)). The interface existed so a caller could recover the format of the bytes `Processor.Process` returns by asserting the returned `io.Reader` to it, which is a protocol where a struct field says the same thing: `result.OutputFormat` is the format of the emitted bytes -- CSV, TSV and LTSV pass through, JSON becomes JSONL, XLSX and Parquet become CSV -- beside the `OriginalFormat` that was already there. The `Format`, `OriginalFormat` and `Len` methods are gone from the value the reader actually is, not only from the interface, since leaving them would let any caller rebuild the same protocol with an ad-hoc `interface{ Format() parser.FileType }` and nothing would have been removed. Migration: read `result.OutputFormat` instead of asserting to `prep.Stream`; a caller who wanted `Len` can read the bytes, and a caller who was rewinding still can, because the reader satisfies `io.Seeker` and `Process` now documents that rather than pointing at the interface.

- `DBBuilder.DisableAutoSave` is removed ([#691](https://github.com/nao1215/filesql/issues/691)). Auto-save is off until `EnableAutoSave` is called, so the method only expressed enabling it and changing your mind inside one chain, and no code outside this repository called it. Migration: delete the call, and delete the `EnableAutoSave` call it was undoing.

- The fields of `ach.TableSet` and `wire.TableSet` are unexported, and the three IAT getters that were missing are added ([#692](https://github.com/nao1215/filesql/issues/692)). `ach.TableSet` published seven fields beside getters that reach the same data, and `wire.TableSet` one, so the same table had two names and this package's own ACH loader used the fields while its Fedwire loader used the getter. The methods are now the only path: `GetFileHeaderTable`, `GetBatchesTable`, `GetEntriesTable`, `GetAddendaTable`, the new `GetIATBatchesTable`, `GetIATEntriesTable` and `GetIATAddendaTable`, and `GetMessageTable` for Fedwire, each returning nil for a nil receiver as the existing ones do. Migration: replace `ts.Entries` with `ts.GetEntriesTable()` and so on; a `TableSet` can only come from `ParseReader`, so nothing was building one by literal. What this does not change is what a getter returns: the same pointer the field held, which is what the get-mutate-update-write round trip through `Update*FromTableData` and `WriteToWriter` relies on, and the updaters still validate nothing. This buys surface, not integrity.

- Four members of the compression surface are unexported or removed ([#693](https://github.com/nao1215/filesql/issues/693)). `CompressionFactory.DetectCompressionType`, `CompressionFactory.CreateHandlerForFile` and `CompressionFactory.GetBaseFileType` are called from inside this package and by nothing outside it, so they keep their bodies under lowercase names; `CompressionHandler.Extension` had only tests behind it and duplicated `CompressionType.Extension`, so it leaves the interface and the implementation. Migration: `CompressionType.Extension()` answers what the handler method answered, and a caller who was detecting a codec from a path can hand the path to `CreateReaderForFile`, which detects it and opens the file in one call. `NewCompressionFactory`, `CreateReaderForFile`, `RemoveCompressionExtension`, `NewCompressionHandler`, `CreateReader`, `CreateWriter` and `CompressionType.Extension` are untouched.

### Deprecated

- `DBBuilder.Build` is deprecated: `Open`, `OpenReadOnly`, `LoadInto` and `LoadIntoTx` validate the builder themselves ([#694](https://github.com/nao1215/filesql/issues/694)). The two-step protocol was one no caller exploited -- every known user calls a terminal method immediately after `Build`, and `OpenContext` wrapped the pair internally -- so the terminal methods now run the same validation and report the same errors, and a builder can be opened in one call. `Build` stays and still works, so nothing has to change today; a call followed by a terminal method validates once, because the validation runs at most once per builder. That guard is the reason this could not simply move: the validation derives a reader from each file of each `AddFS` filesystem, and registering those twice would load every one of them a second time. Nothing it derives reaches the builder until every check has passed, so an open that failed and is tried again -- after the caller gave auto-save an output directory it can write to, say -- starts from the inputs the caller registered rather than from what the failed attempt left behind. Migration: drop the `Build` call and its error check, and call `Open`, `OpenReadOnly`, `LoadInto` or `LoadIntoTx` on the builder. `Build` is removed in a later release, once the code known to call it has moved.

- A builder with an unusable input now reports what is wrong with the input rather than asking whether `Build` was called. `Open` on a builder holding a path that does not exist reports `ErrFileNotFound` naming the file, where it reported `ErrNoFiles` with "did you call Build()?"; a builder holding nothing at all still reports `ErrNoFiles`. A caller matching on the sentinel of the second case is unaffected; a caller matching on the message text of the first should match `ErrFileNotFound` instead.

### Documentation

- The rules the package follows moved from README to godoc, where a caller meets them while writing the call ([#688](https://github.com/nao1215/filesql/issues/688)). README's `## Important Notes` had grown to about 19,500 characters across six subsections against about 4,400 of `## Quick Start`, so a reader who opened the file to learn how to load a CSV scrolled past the line-terminator policy, the memory figures and the ACH control-record semantics to reach the examples index; and two of those subsections were a second copy of a rule `doc.go` already stated, free to drift from it. `doc.go` now carries Column Types, Memory and Streaming, Concurrency, Saving Changes and ACH and Fedwire as sections of its own, its Excel Sheet Visibility section gained the two facts README had beside it, and the rule about a table whose name cannot be a file name sits on `DumpDatabase`, which is what refuses it. The prose was shortened on the way, since a godoc section states the rule and the reasoning behind each one is already in this file. README keeps what it is for and takes the shape atago and sqly already have: a runnable example first, before the reasons and the feature list, since a reader wants to run something before reading about it; a `Documentation:` line near the top pointing at pkg.go.dev; sections named for jobs rather than concepts, so `Quick Start` is now `Recipes` and the format table moved below them; and no bold, which atago uses none of and sqly one of. `doc_sync_test.go` moved with the prose rather than staying behind: it required the `## Important Notes` heading and pinned README markers such as `EnableAutoSave("")`, which is what had kept the rules there, and it now requires those markers in `doc.go` and refuses a rule that comes back to README.

### Added

- `DumpDatabaseContext` exports under a context, so an export can be canceled or given a deadline ([#685](https://github.com/nao1215/filesql/issues/685)). `DumpDatabase` took none and built `context.Background()` for the calls underneath that do take one, so a caller who could cancel `DumpACH` or `DumpFedWire` directly lost the ability by going through it, and an export ran to completion however long ago the request behind it was abandoned. That is the operation whose cost is set by the size of the tables rather than by anything the handler can bound, and the one that backs a download endpoint. The new function is the shape `Open` and `OpenContext` already establish here; `DumpDatabase` keeps its signature and calls it with a background context, so nothing existing changes. The export stops at the next table, row or write after the context ends: a table already written stays written, since the tables are separate files and nothing undoes one, and the table it was in the middle of leaves nothing, because each file is staged and put in place only once it is whole. The context also reaches the queries that read each table and the two write-back functions that had been receiving a manufactured one. A save at close still uses a background context, because `Close` has none to offer and the rows have to be written before the database goes.

### Fixed

- A load that ends because its context ended reports an error carrying that context's error, whatever the database said on the way out ([#683](https://github.com/nao1215/filesql/issues/683)). A load runs its statements under the caller's context, and when the context ends `database/sql` tears the transaction down from a goroutine of its own; which of the two a statement meets is a race, so reaching the insert after the teardown gave `sql: statement is closed`, about a statement the caller never held and with nothing of the context in the chain. `errors.Is(err, context.Canceled)` was therefore true most of the time and false the rest for the same cancellation of the same load, which is the distinction an HTTP handler branches on when it decides whether a failure is worth a log line and a 500. Measured over 60 cancellations per entry point before the fix: 4 losses for `OpenContext` with a deadline, 5 with a cancel, 3 for `LoadInto`, 1 for a load from `AddReader`, and 0 for `LoadIntoTx`, which runs the same statements under the same teardown and is the same class rather than a different one. Every per-input load now passes through one place that attaches the context's error when the context is done, wrapping rather than joining so the message stays one line for a log, and keeping the cause because it is the only thing that says where the load stopped. A failure that is not about the context, under a context that is still alive, gains nothing. The lock-wait loop, which had drawn this conclusion for itself, now uses the same helper. The cleanup that follows such a failure is quiet for the same reason: when the context is done the transaction has already been rolled back, by `database/sql` from a goroutine of its own or by the driver underneath, so the rollback this package then attempts loses a race it was never going to win -- and it loses to two different errors, `sql.ErrTxDone` from one and SQLite's "cannot rollback - no transaction is active" from the other. Only the first was recognized, so the second reached the caller as a second failure about a rollback that had already happened. Both are now read as what they are, in the transaction path and in the savepoint path `LoadIntoTx` uses.

- A load stops reading its source when its context ends ([#684](https://github.com/nao1215/filesql/issues/684)). Nothing between the caller's context and the source read that context: the reader package takes none, so the first thing that noticed a canceled load was the database call after the read. For a path that is a moment away, but for a source handed to `AddReader` it is however long the sender takes, and a body smaller than the 64 KiB the line-ending sniff peeks was read to its end whatever the caller's context said. A 688-byte body delivered one byte per millisecond under a context whose deadline was 10ms kept `Open` running for 728ms and consumed every byte of it; a client sending a byte per second would hold the goroutine and the buffered bytes of an abandoned request until it reached the sniff window, which is about eighteen hours. CSV, TSV, LTSV and JSONL all behaved this way, each stopping at a different point, so the answer depended on the format and the chunk size rather than on the caller. A source that is a stream is now read through a wrapper that refuses the next read once the context is done. A source that is an open file is left as it is: its length is its own and the operating system serves it, and Parquet reads a file at both ends and by column chunk, so wrapping it would hide that it is a file and turn a read where it lies into a copy of the whole of it. A read already blocked inside the source cannot be interrupted from here, which is what a deadline on the connection is for, and README now says so.

- Two of the load failures no longer name the package twice in one message ([#686](https://github.com/nao1215/filesql/issues/686)). A load canceled while inserting reported `filesql: database operation failed: failed to insert chunk data into table "t": filesql: database operation failed: failed to insert record: ...`, the same sentinel spelled twice because the insert and the caller that adds the table name each wrapped it; the inner one no longer does, and the sentinel a caller matches is unchanged. A file holding bytes that are not UTF-8 reported `filesql: parsing failed: failed to read CSV record: filesql: invalid UTF-8: ...`, because the text decoder sits in front of the reader and reports with this package's own error, which the reader then framed again as a parse failure; that frame is skipped for a cause that already carries a sentinel of this package's, and `ErrParsing` still reaches a caller through the `ParseError` the load wraps it in. The nine other failure modes a load can report already named the package once, and a test now holds all eleven to that.

- A JSON document whose array is followed by `]` or `}` is refused rather than loaded, and nothing after that byte is dropped in silence ([#679](https://github.com/nao1215/filesql/issues/679)). A file holding `[{"a":1}]}[{"a":2}]` loaded as a one-row table and reported success, so a caller had no way to tell it from a file that really holds one row, and the second array's rows were gone with nothing said about them. Every other trailing byte was already refused -- `[1] garbage`, `[1] 2`, `[1],` -- so the hole was exactly the two characters a container is closed with: the check was `decoder.More()`, and `encoding/json` defines `More` as "the byte ahead is not `]` and not `}`", which is the right question while a container is being iterated and the wrong one once the document is over. The read now asks the decoder for one more token instead, which a complete document answers with `io.EOF`, a stray bracket with a syntax error and a trailing value with that value, so all three are refused under the message the third already had. The non-array branch of the same read has always been strict, because it goes through `json.Unmarshal`, so this is the two readings of "these bytes are a JSON document" in one function coming back into agreement. `parser.Parse` refuses the same documents, since it reads through the same code.

- One element of a JSON array is held to the same 64 MiB bound a delimited record and a JSONL line are held to ([#680](https://github.com/nao1215/filesql/issues/680)). An element is held whole while it is decoded, so its length is what the read costs, and an array whose first element never closed asked for the whole of the stream: measured against a reader yielding `[{"a":"` and then filler, a 200 MiB body was read entirely and a 400 MiB body was read entirely, where the same shape in JSONL stopped after 64 MiB. A file cannot cost more than its own size, so what this is for is a source that is a stream, which is the reason the other chunked formats were bounded and the reason README states the rule -- and the array is one of the shapes README names as arriving in chunks, so it was the one place the rule was stated and not applied. The refusal now lands within one of the decoder's own reads of the bound rather than on it, because the decoder reads ahead into a buffer that doubles: an unterminated element reads about 127 MiB of the stream whatever the sender goes on to send, rather than all of it. A document that is not an array is still read whole, which is what README says it is.

- An LTSV record holding a field that names no label is handled by the malformed-row policy rather than dropped in silence ([#681](https://github.com/nao1215/filesql/issues/681)). A line holding no `label:value` pair at all vanished from the table with no error, no warning and no entry in `SkippedRows`, under every policy including the default `MalformedRowStop`, whose documentation gives the reason this is wrong: a ragged row usually signals a corrupt or misaligned file, and silently dropping data would hide the problem. A three-line file whose middle line was a stray log line or a line from another format loaded as a two-row table that reported success. The field is data the table has no column for, so the record carrying it is now one the policy decides about: `MalformedRowStop` refuses the load naming the row and quoting the field, `MalformedRowSkip` drops the record and counts it in `SkippedRows`, and `MalformedRowFill` refuses it for the reason it refuses a long delimited record -- there is no column to pad that would hold what the field says, so keeping the record would be discarding the field. What LTSV settles itself is unchanged and is the missing half of the same question: a record naming only some of the columns is padded, since LTSV writes its labels on every record and leaving one out is how the format says a value is absent. A blank line, a line of only tabs and a line ending in a tab are still not records and are still skipped without a word. `ErrColumnMismatch` is the sentinel in both halves and its documentation now covers both. `parser.Parse` refuses such a record, since it applies no policy of its own and is strict about a delimited record for the same reason.

## [0.48.0] - 2026-08-27

### Added

- The `validate` tag reads `iscolor`, `uuid_rfc4122`, `uuid3_rfc4122`, `uuid4_rfc4122`, `uuid5_rfc4122` and `dns_rfc1035_label` ([#650](https://github.com/nao1215/filesql/issues/650)). These are the last spellings the reference dialect defines whose checks prep already owned or could state in one regex, so a struct carrying tags copied from a go-playground one no longer fails to parse on any of them. `iscolor` is an OR over `hexcolor`, `rgb`, `rgba`, `hsl` and `hsla`, which is how the reference defines the alias, built the way `country_code` is built over the three code lookups, and its message names a color rather than the five spellings, since a cell that is none of them is not close to any one in particular. The four `_rfc4122` spellings name the checks the unsuffixed tags already perform and build the same validators, each carrying the spelling the caller wrote so an error about `uuid4_rfc4122` does not say `uuid4`; collapsing them meant the four near-identical UUID types became one carrying a pattern and a tag. `uuid3_rfc4122` inherits prep's existing divergence rather than opening a loophole beside it: prep's `uuid3` requires the variant nibble the reference leaves unconstrained, and the suffixed spelling of the same version requires it too. `dns_rfc1035_label` is the reference's reading of an RFC 1035 label, capped at 63 characters, which is the RFC's own limit and which the reference's pattern leaves out, so that pattern accepts a 100-character string no DNS server accepts as a label. The reading is lowercase-only although the RFC's grammar admits upper case and DNS compares labels without regard to it: that is the reference's rule and also the one Kubernetes enforces on resource names, which is where such columns come from, so a column holding mixed case wants `prep:"lowercase"` before the tag rather than a looser tag.

- The `validate` tag reads `iso4217_numeric`, and the currency table carries the numeric codes it needs ([#651](https://github.com/nao1215/filesql/issues/651)). The #642 batch brought `iso4217` but not its numeric sibling, and the table it added held only the alphabetic codes, so a currency column exported from a payments or accounting system carrying 392 rather than JPY had nothing to be checked against. `prep/codes.go`'s currency table is now rows the way the country table already was, one row per active currency carrying both of its published forms, rebuilt from the same published list with the source and retrieval date kept in the comment; every active currency the source lists carries a numeric code, so the two forms name the same 178 codes. The tag follows the decision `iso3166_1_alpha_numeric` pinned in #642: the reference defines the numeric tags on integer fields, so the string-cell form is the standard's three-digit print form, and `008` is the lek while `8` is nothing. `XXX`, the no-currency code the alphabetic set deliberately includes, is numeric 999 and passes.

- The `validate` tag reads `unique`, meaning uniqueness of a column across the rows of one processing run ([#645](https://github.com/nao1215/filesql/issues/645)). An ID or key column could not be checked for duplicates: SQL finds them after loading, but prep's job is to refuse bad rows before they reach SQLite, and a duplicated key is exactly the class of error a schema wants to refuse at the door. The tag changes meaning on the way over from the reference dialect, which defines it for a slice, array or map field, and says so in `prep/doc.go`: a row here is a flat struct, so the reading that carries over is the column. The first occurrence of a value is valid and every later one is reported, naming the value and the row it first appeared on. An empty cell never counts as a duplicate, since an empty cell is a missing value under this package's rule and two of them are two absences rather than two equal values. The comparison is exact string equality on the value preprocessing produced, so `prep:"trim,lowercase"` beside it gives case-insensitive deduplication without `unique` needing options of its own.

  Where the state lives is the whole design of it. The validator interface is stateless and parsed validators are cached process-wide in a `sync.Map` keyed by struct type and strict flag, with `Processor.Process` sharing those instances, so a seen set parked in a validator would carry values from one file into the next and race between concurrent processors sharing one struct type. Instead the tag marks the field at parse time and builds no validator, and the processor allocates one seen set per unique column per `Process` call, holding each value against the row it first appeared on so the message can name both. A struct with no unique column gets no slice at all and pays nothing; a struct with one grows with the number of distinct values in the column, which `prep/doc.go` states, and `BenchmarkProcessUniqueCSV` keeps the cost measured. The rows of one run are walked in order on one goroutine, so the map needs no lock, and a code comment says that this map is the first casualty if that ever changes.

- The `validate` tag reads `iso3166_1_alpha2`, `iso3166_1_alpha3`, `iso3166_1_alpha_numeric`, `country_code` and `iso4217` ([#642](https://github.com/nao1215/filesql/issues/642)). Country and currency columns are among the most common code-typed columns in tabular data and prep rejected all five spellings as unknown tags. The tables live in a new `prep/codes.go`: one row per ISO 3166-1 assignment carrying all three of its published forms, and the active ISO 4217 alphabetic codes, each with the source it was read from and the date in a comment above it, since the assignments are published facts rather than anything this package decides. Every lookup is exact, as it is in the reference dialect, so `JP` is a country code and `jp` is not, and a numeric code keeps the leading zeros the standard prints, so `032` is Argentina and `32` is nothing. What is deliberately absent is the user-assigned range: `XK`, which is widely used for Kosovo and has never been assigned, is not a country code here, and neither is a code ISO has withdrawn. `country_code` is a thin pass over the three lookups rather than a fourth table, and the tests bound each table from below rather than pinning an exact count, so a truncated table fails while an assignment ISO adds later does not.

- The `validate` tag reads `json`, `timezone`, `semver`, `base32`, `base64`, `base64url`, `base64rawurl` and `oneofci` ([#643](https://github.com/nao1215/filesql/issues/643)). Cells holding a JSON payload, a time zone name, a version or encoded bytes are everyday tabular content and every one of these spellings was an unknown tag. `json` and `timezone` come straight from the standard library, `json.Valid` and `time.LoadLocation`, and `semver` uses the regular expression published at https://semver.org, named as its source in a comment; two rules are pinned on top of what those give: `timezone` refuses `Local` in every casing, since `time.LoadLocation` reads it as the host's own zone and a cell that means a different offset on a different machine is not a zone name; and `semver` refuses a leading zero in a numeric pre-release identifier, which the published regex already encodes. The four encoding tags check the RFC 4648 alphabet before decoding, and that order is the point: Go's base32 and base64 decoders skip carriage returns and line feeds, so `Zm9v\nYmFy` decodes there and is still not a base64 value. `oneofci` is `oneof` compared with `strings.EqualFold`, sharing its candidate parsing so a single-quoted candidate holding a space works the same way. `timezone` needs a zone database and Windows ships none, so prep now imports `time/tzdata`; that adds a few hundred KB to a program importing prep, and neither filesql itself nor sqly imports it.

- The `validate` tag reads `credit_card`, `luhn_checksum`, `isbn`, `isbn10`, `isbn13`, `issn`, `md5`, `sha256`, `sha384` and `sha512` ([#644](https://github.com/nao1215/filesql/issues/644)). Checksummed identifiers are where validation earns its keep in tabular data, because a mistyped value still looks plausible and only the check digit betrays it, and prep could not check one. The checksum rules are the published ones: Luhn for `luhn_checksum` and `credit_card`, the weighted sums modulo eleven for `isbn10` and `issn`, and the alternating 1,3 weights for `isbn13`, with `isbn` accepting either width. Where the tags differ from each other is in what they will read: `credit_card` groups its digits with single spaces, as the reference dialect does and as a card is printed, so a number written with hyphens fails and is a column for `prep:"keep_digits"` first; `luhn_checksum` takes the digits alone; `isbn10` and `isbn13` remove hyphens and spaces themselves, at most three of each for the ten-digit width and four for the thirteen-digit one, which is how many an ISBN of that width is printed with -- bounding the removal is what refuses `0--13-110362-8`, whose remaining characters have the right shape and the right check digit and which is still not an ISBN; and `issn` requires the hyphen the standard prints. The four digest tags are one validator parameterized by width -- lowercase hexadecimal of exactly 32, 64, 96 or 128 characters -- so an uppercase spelling is refused and one column spells a digest one way.

- The `validate` tag reads `ip`, `ipv4`, `ipv6` and `port` ([#640](https://github.com/nao1215/filesql/issues/640)). prep answered only to `ip_addr`, `ip4_addr` and `ip6_addr`, and to no spelling of `port` at all, so a struct carrying tags copied from a go-playground one failed to parse on the spellings that dialect documents most prominently -- `validate:"ipv4"` was an unknown tag rather than a check. The first three build the validators the underscore spellings already built, since the semantics are the same and the distinction the reference draws there is between `net.ParseIP` and `net.ResolveIPAddr`, which drags in a resolver for nothing a cell needs; each names the spelling the caller wrote when it reports, so an error about `ipv4` does not say `ip4_addr`. An IPv4-mapped address such as `::ffff:192.0.2.1` satisfies `ipv4` and fails `ipv6`, which is what the reference answers and what `net.IP` carries. `port` is defined on a numeric field in the reference, so the form a cell may take is pinned here rather than inherited: ASCII digits alone naming a port from 1 to 65535, which admits `0080` as port 80 and refuses `+80`, `0x50`, `80 ` and `0`.

- The `excluded_if`, `excluded_unless`, `excluded_with`, `excluded_with_all`, `excluded_without` and `excluded_without_all` tags are implemented ([#641](https://github.com/nao1215/filesql/issues/641)). The `required_*` family they negate has been there all along, so a schema could demand a cell's presence under a condition and had no way to forbid it, although the reference dialect defines both halves symmetrically. Each of the six complains only about presence: it reports an error when its condition holds and the tagged cell carries a value, and says nothing on every other row, including the row where the cell is empty. That last part is what makes them decide presence rather than compare values, and it matters most for `excluded_without`, whose condition is that a named field is empty -- exactly the row a comparison is skipped on, so wiring it as a comparison would have skipped the only rows the tag exists for. `excluded_if` and `excluded_unless` take field and value pairs and read an odd parameter count the way `required_if` does, reporting it under `WithStrictTagParsing` and ignoring it otherwise; the other four take a list of field names. The four pair-taking tags now go through one registry rather than a switch, so adding the two new ones did not add two more cases to it.

- The GoogleSQL dialect gains the scalar functions it had no SQLite spelling for ([#618](https://github.com/nao1215/filesql/issues/618)), which is the third and last of the three dialects to get this pass: `INSTR` with a position and an occurrence, `CONTAINS_SUBSTR`, `NORMALIZE`, `NORMALIZE_AND_CASEFOLD`, `EDIT_DISTANCE`, `FROM_HEX`, `TO_BASE32`, `FROM_BASE32`, `TO_JSON_STRING`, `IEEE_DIVIDE`, `IS_INF`, `CSC`, `SEC`, `CSCH`, `SECH`, `COTH` and `ERROR`. Three of them needed syntax rather than a registration: `NORMALIZE` writes its form as a bare keyword, which would have reached SQLite as a column name, so the rewrite turns the word into an argument; `EDIT_DISTANCE` writes its cap as a named argument, `max_distance => 2`, which SQLite has no syntax for, so the name is dropped and the value kept, and `=>` is now one token rather than an `=` beside a `>`; and `INSTR` is registered by SQLite itself in its two-argument form, so the longer ones are rewritten onto a helper. Two answers are deliberately not the BigQuery emulator's, because the emulator contradicts the documented definition and, in one case, itself: `INSTR` with a negative position searches backwards and answers a position counted from the beginning, so `INSTR('abcabc', 'b', -1)` is 5, where the emulator answers 4, a position that letter does not occupy; and `LAST_DAY` of an ISO week is the Sunday that closes it, since an ISO week begins on Monday, where the emulator answers the Saturday while its own `DATE_TRUNC` starts the week on Monday. `FARM_FINGERPRINT` is deliberately left out: it is FarmHash Fingerprint64, and a reimplementation that is close but not identical would answer a plausible number that buckets rows differently from BigQuery, which is worse than not having it.

- The DATETIME and TIME function families are implemented under the GoogleSQL dialect ([#616](https://github.com/nao1215/filesql/issues/616)). BigQuery has one family per type and filesql had the DATE and TIMESTAMP ones, so a column holding a datetime -- which on a loaded file is the ordinary case -- had no arithmetic at all. `DATETIME_ADD`, `DATETIME_SUB`, `DATETIME_DIFF`, `TIME_ADD`, `TIME_SUB`, `TIME_DIFF` and `TIME_TRUNC` come with `UNIX_DATE`, `DATE_FROM_UNIX_DATE`, `CURRENT_DATETIME`, `STRING(timestamp)` and `LAST_DAY`'s date-part argument. The DATETIME family answers what the TIMESTAMP family answers, since both are a point in time and the zone BigQuery separates them by is not carried here; the TIME family needed arithmetic of its own, because a BigQuery TIME is a time of day, so `TIME_ADD(TIME '23:04:05', INTERVAL 2 HOUR)` wraps to `01:04:05` rather than moving to another day, and `TIME_TRUNC` truncates within the day. The forms that take a time zone are refused rather than answered unshifted, since answering them without the zone would be a different instant.

- `EXTRACT`, `DATE_TRUNC` and `DATE_DIFF` read BigQuery's `WEEK(<WEEKDAY>)` ([#615](https://github.com/nao1215/filesql/issues/615)). It used to reach SQLite as `near "FROM": syntax error` -- `EXTRACT(x FROM y)` is not SQLite syntax at all -- so a caller grouping by a week that starts on Monday had no way to say so and no error that explained why. All seven weekday names work, in all three functions, with the days before the year's first such weekday in week 0, which is the rule the plain `WEEK` already follows for Sunday.

- BigQuery's `SAFE.` prefix works with any helper this package computes ([#614](https://github.com/nao1215/filesql/issues/614)). It was accepted in front of five names and refused during translation everywhere else, so `SAFE.SUBSTR` and `SAFE.PARSE_DATE` did not translate -- and those five are the separate `SAFE_ADD` family, whose underscore spelling is a different set of functions, so the prefix had no implementation here at all. `SAFE.f(args)` now becomes `safe_call('f', args)`, which runs `f` and answers NULL where `f` could not compute a value from what it was given, wrapping whatever the call pass turned the call into. Two kinds of failure stay reported rather than answered with a NULL, because neither is about the data: a name nothing defines, and an argument count the function does not have. Two other things the prefix does not do: a function SQLite computes itself, such as `ABS` or `LENGTH`, is out of reach from a user-defined function, so the prefix is dropped there and the call runs as written -- those are the ones that do not raise in the first place, which is why a BigQuery caller reaches for the prefix on the parsing and casting functions; and `SAFE.CAST` stays refused, since it is not BigQuery, the safe cast is `SAFE_CAST`, and dropping the prefix would leave a plain `CAST` that raises where the caller asked for a NULL.

- `EXTRACT` answers BigQuery's `MILLISECOND` and `MICROSECOND`, and `DATE_DIFF` its `ISOWEEK`, `ISOYEAR` and `QUARTER` ([#617](https://github.com/nao1215/filesql/issues/617)). All five were refused, so a query naming one failed rather than answering. BigQuery's sub-second parts are the fraction of a second alone, where PostgreSQL's plurals are the seconds field with the fraction scaled into it and PostgreSQL answers to both spellings, so 13:04:05.123 is 123 milliseconds under one dialect and 5123 under the other.

- `APPROX_COUNT_DISTINCT`, `CORR`, `COVAR_POP` and `COVAR_SAMP` are translated under the GoogleSQL dialect ([#619](https://github.com/nao1215/filesql/issues/619)). The first becomes `COUNT(DISTINCT x)`, which answers exactly what BigQuery's estimates -- a difference worth stating rather than a reason to refuse, since an exact count is a correct answer to the question the approximation asks. The other three are expressed from sums the way the variance family already is, over rows where both sides are present: a row missing either takes no part in any of them, so the sums and the count are taken over the same rows.

- `CAST` of a hexadecimal string to INT64 answers a number under the GoogleSQL dialect ([#620](https://github.com/nao1215/filesql/issues/620)). `CAST('0x10' AS INT64)` failed with "invalid cast"; BigQuery reads the form -- an optional sign, then `0x` or `0X`, then hexadecimal digits -- and it is how a column of hexadecimal identifiers becomes numbers. The reading sits behind the dialect check the other per-dialect cast rules already sit behind, so PostgreSQL still raises for it and MySQL still reads the leading digits, which its prefix rule makes 0.

- The PostgreSQL dialect gains the scalar functions it had no SQLite spelling for ([#607](https://github.com/nao1215/filesql/issues/607)). A query using one of them was told "no such function" about a name the caller did write and PostgreSQL does define; the MySQL side of the same gap was filled in v0.47.0 and this is the PostgreSQL half. Added: `quote_ident`, `quote_literal`, `quote_nullable`, `regexp_count`, `cbrt`, `factorial`, `gcd`, `lcm`, `sind`, `cosd`, `tand`, `cotd`, `asind`, `acosd`, `atand`, `atan2d`, `isfinite`, `to_number`, `to_timestamp` in both its spellings, `make_date`, `make_time`, `date_bin`, `num_nulls`, `num_nonnulls`, `sha224`, `sha256`, `sha384`, `sha512`, `encode`, `decode`, `get_byte`, `set_byte`, `gen_random_uuid`, `clock_timestamp`, `statement_timestamp`, `transaction_timestamp` and `timeofday`. `LOCALTIMESTAMP` and `LOCALTIME` are keywords rather than calls, so they are rewritten; SQLite had read them as column names and reported no such column. The edges are where a reimplementation drifts and are pinned as tests: the degree functions answer the quadrant angles exactly, so `SIND(30)` is 0.5, `TAND(45)` is 1 and `COTD(0)` is an infinity rather than what converting through radians gives, which is the reason PostgreSQL has them at all, though away from those angles the answer is the conversion and can differ from PostgreSQL's in the last place; `FACTORIAL` answers in 64 bits and refuses an argument past 20, where PostgreSQL computes in arbitrary precision and does not; `GCD` and `LCM` read their arguments unsigned and `GCD(0, 0)` is 0; `MAKE_DATE` and `MAKE_TIME` refuse a field outside its range rather than carrying it into the next one; the `sha` family answers bytes, so a query prints one with `ENCODE(SHA256(x), 'hex')`, and the three encodings round-trip through `DECODE`; and `DATE_BIN` refuses a stride that is not a fixed length, since a month is 28 to 31 days and cannot divide a timeline into equal bins. The clock functions are registered as non-deterministic, or SQLite would fold each of them to one value per statement. Three answers are deliberately not PostgreSQL's, because SQLite has no type to carry them: `TO_TIMESTAMP` answers a timestamp with no zone suffix, `ISFINITE` answers 1 rather than true, and `DECODE` answers a blob rather than PostgreSQL's `\x`-prefixed text. Every other answer was read from PostgreSQL 17.10 rather than derived.

- `x + INTERVAL n unit`, the operator spelling of date arithmetic, is translated under the MySQL and GoogleSQL dialects ([#609](https://github.com/nao1215/filesql/issues/609)). Only the function form was: `DATE_ADD(d, INTERVAL 1 DAY)` worked and `d + INTERVAL 1 DAY` failed with `near "1": syntax error`, so the spelling MySQL's own documentation writes first was the one that did not work. Both now reach the same `interval_add` call. A `(` directly after the word is left alone, because MySQL's `INTERVAL(n, a, b, ...)` is an ordinary function that returns the index of the first argument larger than `n` and has nothing to do with dates.

- `BETWEEN SYMMETRIC`, PostgreSQL's way of writing a range whose two ends may be in either order, is translated ([#606](https://github.com/nao1215/filesql/issues/606)). It reached SQLite, which has no such form, as `near "7": syntax error` -- a message naming a number from the caller's own query with nothing in it about the word SYMMETRIC. It becomes `BETWEEN least(a, b) AND greatest(a, b)`, so each bound is still written once and a bound that is a function call is not evaluated twice. The shared helpers are named rather than PostgreSQL's own pair: a NULL bound makes the whole comparison NULL there, and PostgreSQL's `LEAST` and `GREATEST` skip their NULLs, which would answer false instead. `BETWEEN ASYMMETRIC`, the explicit spelling of the ordinary reading, drops the keyword.

- `DATE_PART`, `EXTRACT` and `DATE_TRUNC` answer PostgreSQL's `millennium`, `century`, `decade`, `isoyear`, `milliseconds` and `microseconds` ([#603](https://github.com/nao1215/filesql/issues/603)). All six were refused, so a query naming one failed rather than answering. `isoyear` is the one that matters most in practice, because it is the companion of the ISO week the package already had: without it the last week of December and the first week of January group together. The centuries and millennia count from 1, so the year 2000 is in century 20 and truncating to a century gives the year ending in 01, and a decade is the plain division. The two sub-second parts are the seconds field with its fraction, scaled, and that same fraction now reaches PostgreSQL's `second`, which is 12.5 for 10:11:12.5 where MySQL's `SECOND()` and BigQuery's `EXTRACT(SECOND)` stay whole.


- `parser.TableData` carries a `Nulls` mask, so a caller can tell a Parquet null from an empty string ([#675](https://github.com/nao1215/filesql/issues/675)). Parquet is the one format this package reads with a null of its own, and `Parse` rendered a null and an empty string as the same empty string: a column of missing values and a column of present empty ones came back identical, with nothing in the result or the error to say which was which, while a load of the same file stored one as SQL NULL and the other as text. The reader underneath produces the mask already -- it is how the loader knows -- and `Parse` was dropping it. No existing field could carry it: `Records` is `[][]string` and every spelling of "nothing" in a string is also a value a file can hold, and `Headers` and `ColumnTypes` are per column rather than per cell. `Nulls` is nil for every other format, which says "this format has no nulls" rather than "this file has none", and a caller that ignores the field reads exactly what it read before.

### Breaking Changes

- A workbook handed to `AddReader` or found in an `fs.FS` now loads one table per sheet that names a column, so the table it loads as is named for the sheet rather than being the bare name passed to `AddReader` ([#672](https://github.com/nao1215/filesql/issues/672)). A workbook added as `book` whose sheet is `Sheet1` was the table `book` and is now `book_Sheet1`, which is what the same workbook has always been called when it was named by a path; a sheet whose name matches the base still keeps the bare base, as it does there. Migration: read the names out of `sqlite_master` after loading, or ask `ExcelSheetTableNames` for them by handing it the table name in place of a path, which is what its documentation now says it takes; either way, stop assuming the name given to `AddReader`. The alternative -- keeping the bare name for a one-sheet workbook -- was rejected because it leaves a caller unable to write one query that works whichever way the bytes arrived, which is the state this fixes.


- The `frame` package is removed. Its transformations -- `Filter`, `Mutate`, `GroupBy`, `Join`, `Distinct`, `Sort` and the rest -- are questions SQL over a loaded database already answers, which is what this module exists to make easy; pkg.go.dev reports no known importers for the package, and sqly, the shell built on this core, never used it. What cost more than the code was the contract around it: `frame` had to mirror the loader's semantics -- the type each column is given, what a blank cell means, whether the two signs of zero are one value, what spellings count as a number -- and each place the mirror drifted was a defect a caller could hit, which is where [#477](https://github.com/nao1215/filesql/issues/477), [#478](https://github.com/nao1215/filesql/issues/478), [#479](https://github.com/nao1215/filesql/issues/479), [#480](https://github.com/nao1215/filesql/issues/480) and [#494](https://github.com/nao1215/filesql/issues/494) came from; the package also cost defects of its own, in [#466](https://github.com/nao1215/filesql/issues/466), [#467](https://github.com/nao1215/filesql/issues/467) and [#468](https://github.com/nao1215/filesql/issues/468). Maintaining one implementation of those rules leaves nothing that has to stay equal to it. To migrate, load the data and ask SQL: `Filter` is a `WHERE` clause, `Mutate` is an expression in the `SELECT` list, `GroupBy` with an aggregation is `GROUP BY`, `Join` is a `JOIN`, `Distinct` is `SELECT DISTINCT`, `Sort` is `ORDER BY`, `Concat` is `UNION ALL`, `FillNA` is `COALESCE`, `DropNA` is `IS NOT NULL`, `Head` and `Limit` are `LIMIT`, and `ToCSV` and `ToTSV` are `DumpDatabase`. Where `frame.Row(row).Int("qty")` read a cell at a known type, `sql.Rows.Scan` into an `int64` does the same and returns an error for a value that does not fit, where `Int` returned a `false` a caller could ignore. The last version that ships `frame` is v0.47.0, so `go get github.com/nao1215/filesql@v0.47.0` pins a build that still has it.

- `Build` refuses `EnableAutoSave("")` when a source cannot be written back to itself, so a configuration that built successfully before now fails at `Build`. The sources this applies to are the ones overwrite mode could never have saved: a format this package reads but does not write (JSON, JSONL) and a compression codec it reads but does not write (bzip2). The save failed on them either way; what changes is that it fails before the session rather than at the end of it. To keep loading such a source alongside auto-save, pass an output directory -- `EnableAutoSave("./out")` -- which writes every table in the format `DumpOptions` names and leaves the sources alone.

### Fixed

- A multi-sheet workbook loaded from an `io.Reader` or an `fs.FS` keeps every sheet that names a column ([#672](https://github.com/nao1215/filesql/issues/672)). It kept the first one. A workbook of four sheets loaded as one table holding the first, with the other three neither loaded nor mentioned and the load reporting success, so a caller learned nothing unless they knew how many sheets the file had; `ExcelSheetsInReader` on the same bytes reported all four, so the package would say the workbook had four and then load one. README says the opposite twice, in the format table -- "Every sheet is loaded by default" -- and in the sheet-visibility section, which says the policy "applies to every source -- a path, a directory, an embedded filesystem, a reader, and a compressed workbook alike". The two routes that lost the sheets are the two a server uses: an upload and an embedded filesystem. A sheet holding nothing a table can be made from -- no rows, or no row naming a column -- is passed over on every route, as it always was on a path. What a workbook holds has nothing to do with how its bytes arrived, so the sheet policy, the check that refuses two sheets mapping to one table, and the naming now run in one place for every route, with the base taken from the file name for a path and from the caller's table name for a reader. The codec is unwrapped before the workbook is opened, so a compressed workbook through `AddReader` with `WithCompression` loads its sheets too.

- A JSONL line is bounded at 64 MiB, the same as a delimited record ([#668](https://github.com/nao1215/filesql/issues/668)). A line has to be complete before it can be parsed, so its length is what the read costs, and one line with no terminator made that the whole stream: 96 MiB arriving as a single line was read whole and held 320 MiB before anything reported that it is not JSON. CSV and TSV were bounded in the previous entry and JSONL, which is line-oriented in exactly the same way, was the one left -- and it is the format a server is most likely to accept from a client. It now refuses past the limit in the same words the other two use, matching `ErrRecordTooLong` and `ErrParsing`, and the whole line reader is one function rather than a `ReadBytes` call that grows until the stream ends. LTSV and a JSON document that is not an array stay unbounded on purpose: README says both are read in full before they are turned into rows, which is a documented cost rather than an oversight, and bounding them would change what the package promises.

- A codec's failure names its sentinel once, and a codec with no writer is reported as an unsupported format rather than a compression failure ([#670](https://github.com/nao1215/filesql/issues/670)). Two layers wrapped a read failure and both added `ErrCompression`, so a broken compressed file reported `filesql: compression operation failed` twice and named the path twice in one line -- the load already puts the path in front, and the handler has already said which sentinel applies. The write side did worse than repeat itself: `CreateWriter` separates a codec that has no writer from a compressor that failed to start, its comment saying so in as many words, and both of its callers wrapped whatever came back in `ErrCompression` regardless. Asking for bzip2 output therefore reported a compression failure whose cause was that there is no compressor, and the error matched `ErrCompression` and `ErrUnsupportedFormat` at once, which tells the two apart no better than matching neither. `ErrCompression` is now applied in one place, and a bzip2 dump matches `ErrUnsupportedFormat` alone. A caller matching `ErrCompression` to mean "the compressor failed" has to stop matching it for the bzip2 case, which is the case it was never true of.

- The parser package says that a workbook contributes one sheet, and how Parquet values are spelled ([#676](https://github.com/nao1215/filesql/issues/676)). A `TableData` is one table, so `Parse` on a workbook of several sheets returns the first the policy admits and does not read the rest -- which is the shape of the API rather than a defect in it, and which nothing in the package said. `WithExcelSheetPolicy` said it "decides which sheets of a workbook Parse may take its table from", telling a reader that the policy narrows a choice without telling them a choice is being made or that everything unchosen is dropped. `Parse` now says it, names `filesql.OpenContext` and `filesql.ExcelSheetsInReader` as the ways to get the rest, and says the other thing a caller has to know: values are spelled the way the format reads them rather than the way SQLite stores them, so a Parquet whole float is `2` here and the real 2 after a load. Both statements are pinned by tests, since the documentation now promises them.

- The README says what a sheet's used range costs ([#673](https://github.com/nao1215/filesql/issues/673)). It said a workbook whose used range reaches far down the sheet "costs what it holds rather than what its range spans", and the skipping it describes is real: a row with no cell becomes no record and is not padded out to the header's width, which is the larger of the two costs. The smaller one is not saved, because the library hands a sheet's rows over as one slice covering the whole range before any of them is read, so two workbooks of 5,897 and 5,904 bytes, both loading a single row, cost 4 MiB and 27 MiB of Go heap depending on whether the stray cell sits in row 2 or row 1,048,576. The paragraph now says both halves. Avoiding the slice means reading the rows through the library's iterator instead, which the date normalization -- which applies a map of cell to ISO 8601 by index -- would have to be rewritten around, so the note goes when that is done.

- The README says which formats arrive in chunks ([#669](https://github.com/nao1215/filesql/issues/669)). It listed JSONL among those "read in full before they are turned into rows", and a JSONL file is read a line at a time: 96 MiB of it costs the same 5 MiB of Go heap that CSV does, against the 255 MiB that LTSV, which really is read whole, costs. The wording "non-array JSON/JSONL values" is where the two were joined, and they are different things read differently -- a JSON document that is not an array has to be read whole to be one value, and JSONL is a value per line. A reader sizing a process from that paragraph was budgeting a file's worth of memory for a format that needs a chunk. The corrected paragraph also says what a chunked format does hold at once, which is one record.

- A delimited record is bounded at 64 MiB whatever the format, so reading CSV from an `io.Reader` has a memory bound ([#664](https://github.com/nao1215/filesql/issues/664)). One record is held whole while it is split into fields, so its size is what the read costs however the rows are chunked. TSV bounded that and said why -- a file that is not TSV at all must not be able to ask for unbounded memory one line at a time -- and CSV, which is the format most callers reach for, had no bound: a record with no terminator, or a quote that is never closed, was buffered until the stream ended. A 96 MiB body arriving as one record cost 892 MiB of allocation before any error was reported. It matters wherever the source is not a file the caller chose: a request body handed to `AddReader` is as long as its sender decides, and README's promise that "CSV, TSV, JSON arrays, and Parquet arrive in chunks while loading" is about rows, so one record is all it takes to make the chunk the whole input. The limit now lives once in `internal/reader/delimited.go` and both readers hold to it, since it is one rule about one danger and two copies of it had already drifted. A file on disk is unaffected: it cannot hold a record longer than itself, and a file whose largest record is under the bound loads whatever its total size. A legitimate CSV holding a single record above 64 MiB stops loading, which is the trade TSV already made, and README now says so where it describes chunked loading.

- The record-too-long refusal names the line and the limit, and says so once ([#666](https://github.com/nao1215/filesql/issues/666)). TSV reported `failed to read TSV record: failed to read TSV record: bufio.Scanner: token too long` -- the same phrase twice, because the reader and its caller both added it, and then the standard library talking about its own buffer, which says nothing to someone holding a file this package declined. It now reads `failed to read TSV record: record too long: line 2 is longer than the 64 MiB a record may be`, and CSV answers in the same words, since the two are now the same rule. The error still matches `ErrParsing`, which is how a caller tells a malformed file from an unusable one, and `ErrRecordTooLong` distinguishes a file that is merely larger than this reader will hold from one that is not the format it claims.

- A failed load through `AddReader` names the table the caller asked for ([#665](https://github.com/nao1215/filesql/issues/665)). An input that cannot be read twice is staged as text in a table under this package's reserved prefix, and a failure there was reported under that name: `too many columns on _filesql_stage_t`, naming a table the caller never wrote, that is hidden from a dump and from the table listing, and that is dropped before the transaction ends. The same input loaded from a path named the caller's own table, so which name an operator read depended on how the bytes arrived rather than on what went wrong. The wording this package adds now names the caller's table in all three of the statements the staged path runs -- the create, the insert and the copy into the typed table -- so a log line connects the failure to the input it came from. What the database says underneath is still the database's own.

- A double quote no longer changes where a record ends in a carriage-return terminated TSV or LTSV file ([#661](https://github.com/nao1215/filesql/issues/661)). A file whose lines end with a lone carriage return is read as lines, which is what lets a classic Mac OS file load at all, and the reading that decides which carriage returns are terminators tracked double quotes for every format. TSV and LTSV have none -- `TSVReader`'s own documentation says a double quote in a field is an ordinary character, because IANA's text/tab-separated-values defines a field as the bytes between two tabs, and the LTSV reader takes a value as the bytes up to the next tab -- so a single `"` in a value made every carriage return after it data and joined the records from that point into one. In a one-column file nothing caught it: a three-row file loaded as one row whose value held the other two, carriage returns included, with no error. With more columns it failed about a column count, naming neither the quote nor the line ending. The save side already drew the line in the right place, its own comment saying that tracking quotes for those formats "would let one unmatched quote swallow the rest of the file's terminators", so the two sides of the same question disagreed and an in-place save read a terminator out of a file the load had split differently. The normalization now takes the format it is reading for and honors a quote for CSV alone; a carriage return inside a quoted CSV field is still data, and a quoted line feed still does not disqualify a carriage-return file. The delimiter the reader was given beside the format went with the change, since the format already implies it.

- An in-place XLSX save leaves a numeric cell a number, rather than rewriting the whole numbers of a REAL column as text ([#659](https://github.com/nao1215/filesql/issues/659)). Every cell of an XLSX save is written as text, so what keeps a workbook's numbers numeric is the check that a cell whose value did not change is not written at all -- and that check compared two spellings rather than two values. A REAL column is rendered with a decimal point, which is right for a text dump and is what makes a dumped CSV load as REAL again, while the sheet renders the number it stores: the cell holding `2` showed `2`, the loaded value spelled it `2.0`, and the cell was rewritten as the string `2.0`. One decimal anywhere in the column was enough to do this to every whole number in it, and a large integer came off worse -- `1000000000000000` was written back as the text `1e+15`. A spreadsheet that was summing or charting that column had text in it afterwards, from a save the caller made without editing anything. The comparison now reads both sides as numbers when both are numbers this package would store as one, and falls back to comparing text otherwise, so `2` and `2.0` are one value and the cell is left alone, while `007` and `7` stay two values because a zero-padded code is text here and not that number. A cell the caller did change is still written.

- An in-place XLSX save leaves a boolean cell a boolean when its number format renders a calendar day ([#658](https://github.com/nao1215/filesql/issues/658)). This package has two ways to find the date cells of a sheet, and `internal/reader/xlsx_date_stream.go` opens by saying they "differ only in how they reach a cell's style". They did not. The loader reads the sheet's own XML, where a cell whose type attribute says boolean or string is not a serial and is passed over; the save reads the prior sheet by asking the library cell by cell, which decided from the style alone and then parsed whatever the cell stored. A boolean is stored as `1`, so a `TRUE` under a date format became `1900-01-01` on the second reading, the two readings disagreed about that cell, and the save wrote the loaded text over a cell nothing had edited -- taking the cell's type with it. `FALSE` survived, being stored as `0`, which is before the 1900 date system starts and converts to nothing, so the damage looked arbitrary. The library reading now asks the same question the XML reading always asked: a serial carries no type attribute or carries `n`, and a boolean, a shared or inline string, a formula's string result and an error are not serials whatever their format renders. A test holds the two readings against each other over a sheet whose cells are one of each type under one date format, since the design rests on their agreeing and nothing checked it.

- A blank cell in a numeric column loads as NULL through every route, rather than as the empty string when the input arrives as an `io.Reader` ([#654](https://github.com/nao1215/filesql/issues/654)). The rule that a blank cell in an INTEGER or REAL column is a missing number was applied where a row is bound to its insert, which is reached under the column's final type only when the input can be read again. A reader cannot, so it is staged as text and typed once it has all been read, and on that path every column is TEXT while the rows go in; the rule was therefore left out of it, and the staged copy into the typed table relied on SQLite's affinity, which converts text that spells a number and leaves text that does not -- and the empty string does not. The result was the exact set of wrong answers the rule exists to prevent, on the one route that goes through `prep`, whose documented pipeline hands its `io.Reader` to `AddReader`: `MAX` answered the empty string rather than the largest value, `COUNT(column)` counted the blank rows, `AVG` divided by them, `ORDER BY DESC` put them first, a numeric filter passed them, and `WHERE column IS NULL` found none of them, all against a column the schema declares INTEGER and with nothing raised. It affected CSV, TSV, LTSV and XLSX read from a reader; Parquet was unaffected, since it carries a null of its own and its blank arrives marked rather than empty. The copy now names its columns and selects each one that ends up INTEGER or REAL as `NULLIF(column, '')`, which applies the same rule once under the final types, and naming them also stops the copy depending on the staging table's column order. A staged load whose columns are all TEXT still renames the staging table rather than copying it, so the case that pays nothing goes on paying nothing.

- An LTSV dump refuses a column name carrying leading or trailing whitespace instead of writing a label that reads back as a different name ([#656](https://github.com/nao1215/filesql/issues/656)). The reader trims the whitespace around a label -- the format's own grammar admits none there, so a space around one is malformed however it got there and the file is read rather than refused -- while the writer wrote the name as it stood: a table with the column `" a"` was written as `" a:1"` and reloaded as `"a"`, so a save and reload -- which is what auto-save over an LTSV source does on every close -- returned a schema differing from the one that was saved, and a query naming the original column then failed against a file this package had written. Such names are ordinary in spreadsheet exports, where a header cell reads `"name "`. The refusal joins the three the writer already had for a colon, a tab and a newline in a label, is made before any row is written, and names both the column and the name it would have come back under. A dump of such a table now fails with `ErrUnsupportedFormat` where it used to succeed; rename the column, or dump the table as CSV, which quotes its header and keeps the spelling. Names LTSV can hold are untouched, including one with an interior space, one that is not ASCII, and the empty name.

- The README says which formats a blank line is a record in ([#662](https://github.com/nao1215/filesql/issues/662)). It said a blank line is not a record "in a delimited file or in a sheet", which is true of CSV, LTSV and XLSX and false of a one-column TSV, where the blank line is that column's empty value. The behavior is what makes a TSV dump round-trip: CSV writes a lone empty field as `""`, since a bare blank line is not a CSV record, and TSV has no quote to write that with, so an empty line is the only spelling left and a reader that skipped it would drop the row. The paragraph now says that, along with the consequence a caller meets first -- a one-column TSV ending with an extra newline loads with a trailing row holding nothing, the file having no way to say whether that line was a record or a stray terminator. The dump test that pinned the two spellings now reloads them as well, so the round trip the rule exists for is checked rather than assumed.

- The README no longer says a datetime column is stored in ISO 8601 ([#655](https://github.com/nao1215/filesql/issues/655)).
