It said a column recognized as a datetime is "stored as TEXT in ISO 8601, which is the form SQLite's date functions read", and that is true only of a column the file already wrote that way. A text cell is stored as the file wrote it, here as everywhere else in this package, so a column written `1/2/2024`, `2024/01/02` or `02.01.2024` is recognized as a datetime and is still not something `date()` can answer about -- a reader who took the sentence at its word and wrote `WHERE date(d) >= '2024-01-01'` got an empty result set and no error. Rewriting those cells was considered and rejected: it would change the caller's own bytes on an in-place save, which is a larger promise than the inference here makes, and the whole of `infer.MustStayText` exists so that a type does not rewrite the value that lands in it. An XLSX date cell is the exception and the README now says why: it holds a serial number and a number format rather than text, so there is no spelling to keep and the reader renders it in ISO 8601. A test pins the stored value for every recognized layout and records which of them SQLite's date functions can read.

- prep's documentation states the places where its validators deliberately answer differently from the go-playground dialect, and stops claiming one difference that does not exist ([#650](https://github.com/nao1215/filesql/issues/650), [#651](https://github.com/nao1215/filesql/issues/651)). Every single-field tag the two vocabularies share was swept against go-playground/validator v10.22.1 over 301 values, and the ISO code tags were swept over their whole code space -- all 676 two-letter, 17,576 three-letter and 1,000 three-digit candidates -- which turned up no defect in prep and five differences doc.go did not mention. `doc.go` said that the dialect's hostname patterns refuse a label ending in a hyphen; they do not, and neither `hostname` nor `hostname_rfc1123` there refuses one, so that clause was wrong and is now stated the other way round. The four newly documented differences: the base32 and base64 family decode strictly, so a value whose trailing pad bits are not zero is refused although the dialect's patterns accept it, which RFC 4648 permits and which no conformant encoder can trip over; `e164` takes the number with or without its leading plus where the dialect requires the plus, and requires the first digit to be 1 through 9 where the dialect accepts `+0123456789`; `hostname` reads RFC 952, so a one-character label is a hostname and every label must begin with a letter, and all three name validators cap a label at 63 characters and a whole name at 253 as the RFCs do; and `uri` requires something after the scheme, so a bare `http://` is not a URI. The differences the sweep found in the code tables are the tables being current rather than a defect on either side: the dialect accepts `XK`, `UNK` and numeric 153, none of which ISO 3166-1 assigns, and six currency codes ISO has since withdrawn, while prep carries five the dialect predates. Each documented difference is now pinned by a test, and a second test requires the encoding validators to accept every value Go's own encoders produce, so the strictness cannot grow past what the standard allows.

- A dump refuses a table name no platform can hold, rather than writing one Linux accepts and Windows does not ([#637](https://github.com/nao1215/filesql/issues/637)). `dumpFilePath` refused a path separator and nothing else, although the point of refusing on every platform rather than only the running one is, as its own comment says, "so the same database dumped on Linux and on Windows agrees on which tables it can write". They did not agree: a table named `CON`, `NUL`, `a:b` or `a|b` was written on Linux under a name Windows reserves for a device or forbids outright, and a device name is the dangerous one, since Windows resolves it to the device with or without an extension. It is reachable through a plain public call, because `AddReader` keeps the table name the caller gives it, so a name that came from user input reached the dump unaltered; a name derived from a file name cannot carry the punctuation, since only letters, digits, marks and underscore survive `identifierRunes`, but it can be a device name, because a file called `CON.csv` becomes the table `CON`. The refused set is now the two separators, `< > : " | ? *`, a control byte, a name ending in a dot or a space, which Windows strips, and the reserved device names matched without regard to case, with or without an extension. A table whose name is merely unusual is untouched: a dot or a space inside the name, a name that is not ASCII, and a word such as `console` that contains a device name without being one.

- A table name holding a quoting character loads and dumps instead of failing with SQLite's tokenizer error ([#638](https://github.com/nao1215/filesql/issues/638)). A table name reached SQL by concatenation almost everywhere, so a name carrying the character that ends the quoting broke the statement: `a"b` failed the load and ``a`b`` loaded and then failed the dump, because the load quoted with a double quote and the dump with a backtick, and both times the caller was handed the driver's message about a token they never wrote. Both are legal SQLite identifiers. `quoteIdentifier` already existed and doubles an embedded quote, and `createTable` was the one place calling it, which is where the same defect was fixed for column names; the insert, the drop, the rename, the staging copy, the ACH insert and the dump's own reads were left as they were. All of them go through it now, and the dump quotes the way everything else does rather than with a backtick, which is MySQL's spelling that SQLite accepts as a quirk. Injection does not follow from any of this and did not before: a name written to close the identifier and append a statement fails in `Prepare` before anything runs, and the table the load was about to replace is left intact.

- The codec fuzz target no longer fails on a stream this package accepts ([#635](https://github.com/nao1215/filesql/issues/635)). `FuzzCodecReader` budgets what opening a stream may allocate before any of it is read, and that budget was a number of its own: 64 MiB, which sits below both ceilings the package enforces -- a zstd frame may declare a 128 MiB window and an xz stream a 256 MiB dictionary, and README states that ceiling as the intended price of a damaged file. A 29-byte frame declaring a 64 MiB window is therefore exactly what the package promises to spend, and the target called it a failure. It failed only sometimes, because the measurement is process-wide and klauspost/compress allocates the window in goroutines that outlive `zstd.NewReader`, so whether the allocation landed inside the measurement depended on the scheduler: the nightly went red on a schedule nobody could predict and for no defect, which is worse than no nightly, since the next real finding reads as noise. The budget is now derived from `maxXZDictionary` and `maxZstdWindow` so the two cannot drift apart again, and a test pins it between the two numbers that give it meaning -- above every declaration the package accepts, and below the smaller of the two allocations the target exists to catch, which were 4 GiB from a 28-byte xz stream and 512 MiB from a 14-byte zstd one. The frame the fuzzer found is now a seed, as are xz streams on either side of that ceiling, so the most expensive stream the package accepts is opened on every run rather than when the fuzzer rediscovers it.

- A blank cell in a numeric column is a missing number rather than the empty string ([#633](https://github.com/nao1215/filesql/issues/633)). SQLite converts text that spells a number to the column's type and leaves text that does not, and the empty string does not, so a blank cell sat in an INTEGER or REAL column as text -- where SQLite orders text above every number. `MAX` answered the empty string rather than the largest value, `AVG` divided by the rows that held nothing, `COUNT(column)` counted them although counting a column is how a caller asks how many values are present, `ORDER BY column DESC` put those rows first, `WHERE column > 5` passed them, and `WHERE column IS NULL` matched none of them. None of it raised, and a spreadsheet exported to CSV with an empty cell is the ordinary case rather than a corner one. The package also disagreed with itself: the Parquet exporter already wrote that cell as a null, so a load, dump and load again changed the value. Such a cell now reaches the database as NULL. A blank cell in a TEXT column is unchanged and stays the empty string, which is a value the file holds; a column recognized as DATETIME is stored as TEXT and follows that rule, where the empty string sorts below every date and produces no wrong maximum. Round trips are unaffected, since a NULL in a numeric column already dumps as a blank cell. Loading is not measurably slower: `BenchmarkOpenContext` and `BenchmarkOpenParquet` moved within noise and allocate the same.

- The clock functions read UTC and answer once per statement ([#622](https://github.com/nao1215/filesql/issues/622)). They called Go's `time.Now()` and formatted it in whatever zone the process was running in, so `NOW()` answered a different hour on every machine and disagreed with SQLite's own `CURRENT_TIMESTAMP` in the same row -- two callers comparing results across machines saw a difference the data did not have. This package carries no time zone: no column has one, no cast produces one, and a zone argument is refused, so UTC is the only reading that is the same everywhere, and it is what SQLite's clock and BigQuery's default already answer. Separately, each of these was registered as non-deterministic, which makes SQLite call it once per row: `SELECT NOW() FROM t` over a large table answered several times in one result and a row could be stamped after the row below it, where MySQL, PostgreSQL and BigQuery all fix the reading when the statement starts. `NOW`, `CURDATE`, `CURTIME`, `UNIX_TIMESTAMP`, PostgreSQL's `now`, `statement_timestamp` and `transaction_timestamp`, and BigQuery's `CURRENT_DATETIME` are now registered as deterministic, which is what takes them off the row: SQLite computes such a function once per execution and again for the next statement. That is not quite once per statement, because SQLite folds each occurrence of the call separately, so a query naming `NOW` twice read the clock twice, microseconds apart, and the two answers differed whenever those microseconds straddled a second; the fixed clock therefore shares one reading for a window of a millisecond, which is wider than the gap between the occurrences and far narrower than the second these functions are formatted to. A statement beginning inside that window sees the reading the one before it was given, which is at most a millisecond old and never earlier than what an earlier statement answered. PostgreSQL's `clock_timestamp` and `timeofday` stay per-row, since advancing during a statement is the whole of what separates them from `now`. `RAND`, `GENERATE_UUID` and `GEN_RANDOM_UUID` are unaffected and still answer per row. `TO_TIMESTAMP` was in the same non-deterministic table although it reads no clock, and has moved across with the rest of the pure functions. Both changes move an answer a caller may have been relying on.

- Closing a database with auto-save on and a transaction still open returns instead of hanging forever ([#628](https://github.com/nao1215/filesql/issues/628)). The save reads every table through the connector's own connection, an uncommitted write holds the lock on the table it touched, and the driver waits for that lock through `sqlite3_unlock_notify`, which has no deadline and answers to no context. The only goroutine that could release the lock was the one inside `Close`, so the wait never ended, where the same program without auto-save returned from `Close` immediately. The shape that reaches it is an everyday one -- a deferred `Close` and an error path that returns without rolling the transaction back -- so what would otherwise leak a connection hung the program instead. The connector now counts the transactions the caller has begun and not yet finished, and a close with any of them still open skips the save and reports that it did, because a database closed mid-transaction holds rows the caller has neither committed nor rolled back. A transaction that only read is treated the same way, since one rule is easier to rely on than a rule that depends on what the transaction happened to run, and an unclosed `*sql.Rows` does not stop the save: it holds a connection but no transaction and no lock the save waits on. A transaction whose commit failed stops counting as open, since database/sql does not call `Rollback` after that and the driver has already rolled the connection back. A transaction begun by running `BEGIN` through `Exec` is invisible to the count, and a close during one of those still waits forever.

- Overwrite auto-save replaces no file until every source is known to be writable ([#629](https://github.com/nao1215/filesql/issues/629)). It wrote the sources one at a time and stopped at the first it could not write, so a set holding a JSON, a JSONL or a bzip2 source came out of a failed save with its earlier files carrying the session's rows and the rest carrying the old ones, with nothing on disk saying which was which. Whether a source can be written back is decided by its name alone, so `Build` now answers it before the session rather than after the work it would discard, and the save keeps the same check ahead of its loop for a path `Build` did not see. A workbook holding more than one table is the failure this cannot see -- it takes opening the file to know -- and is still reported from the save. An output directory is unaffected: export mode writes what `DumpOptions` says and replaces no source.

- A write to a path that is a symbolic link follows it ([#630](https://github.com/nao1215/filesql/issues/630)). The staged file was renamed onto the link itself, so the link became a regular file holding the change while the file it named still held the old rows: an overwrite auto-save and a `DumpDatabase` into a directory of links both reported success over a data file that never moved, and the link that made the layout work was gone. The staging and the rename now both move to the file the link points at, which also keeps the rename inside the target's filesystem, and the file's mode is read from there as it always was. A chain of links resolves to its end, and a link with nothing on the other end is written at the link's own path, as before, since there is no file to follow and inventing one would put the contents somewhere the caller never named. Hard links are unchanged: a rename cannot preserve a link count and there is no path to follow, so a save through one leaves the other name on the old contents.

- Day and hour differences are exact for every date the calendar holds ([#625](https://github.com/nao1215/filesql/issues/625)). Every helper that measured the distance between two times did it by subtracting one `time.Time` from another, which gives a `time.Duration` -- an int64 of nanoseconds, so it saturates about 292 years out and answers the bound rather than the distance. `DATE_DIFF(DATE '9999-12-31', DATE '2024-03-05', DAY)` was 106751 where the answer is 2913109, and MySQL's `DATEDIFF` over the same pair answered the same wrong number, with nothing to say so. The date that makes this ordinary rather than exotic is 9999-12-31: it is the standard "no expiry" sentinel in a warehouse table, so counting the days to it is a query people write. The distance is now built from the civil date -- a day number computed from the year, month and day, with no bound short of the calendar -- and the finer units from that day count plus the time of day. `DATE_BIN` counts its stride-widths the same way, and `UNIX_DATE` is a day number and no longer touches a duration at all. The units that were already computed from calendar fields, `YEAR`, `QUARTER`, `MONTH` and `ISOYEAR`, are unchanged, and so is MySQL's `TIMESTAMPDIFF`, which is why those were right when the others were not.

- `GCD` never answers a negative number ([#624](https://github.com/nao1215/filesql/issues/624)). The magnitude of the smallest int64 is not an int64 -- negating it overflows back to itself -- so taking the sign off it left it negative and Euclid carried that sign into the answer: `GCD(-9223372036854775808, 6)` was -2, and a caller using the result as a divisor, a step or a bucket width got a negative one. The magnitudes are now unsigned, which is the only type that holds the one they could not, and the three pairs whose answer is that magnitude are reported rather than answered. `LCM` reads the same magnitudes and keeps the overflow check it already had.

- `TO_CHAR` reads its argument to tell a date from a number ([#623](https://github.com/nao1215/filesql/issues/623)). It decided by scanning the template for a digit, so a date template with a digit anywhere in its literal text answered NULL for a timestamp: `TO_CHAR(ts, 'YYYY-MM-DD 00:00:00')` -- a date with a fixed midnight -- and `TO_CHAR(ts, 'HH24:MI (UTC+0)')` were both NULL, which in a text column reads as missing data rather than as a template nothing could format. The template cannot answer this question: a digit in a date template is literal text and a letter in a numeric one is too, which is why PostgreSQL reads the argument's type and never the template. A number now takes the numeric template and text that parses as a date takes the date one; text that is neither has no type to read, and there the template stays the only signal, so `TO_CHAR('2024', '9999')` goes on answering `2024`. A numeric template with no digit position at all now prints its literal text and nothing else, with no field to pad and no column held for a sign, which makes `TO_CHAR(2024, 'YYYY')` the four characters PostgreSQL gives rather than five.

- GoogleSQL's `MD5` and `SHA1` answer bytes rather than hexadecimal text ([#612](https://github.com/nao1215/filesql/issues/612)). BigQuery's digests are bytes and the shared helpers were PostgreSQL's `MD5` and MySQL's `SHA1`, which answer hexadecimal text, so `TO_HEX(MD5(x))` -- the spelling BigQuery's own documentation uses to print a digest -- hexed the hex and answered 64 characters instead of 32, and `LENGTH(MD5(x))` was 32 where BigQuery says 16. Nothing reported it, because both are strings to SQLite. `SHA256` and `SHA512` were already right, so two spellings of one idea disagreed inside one dialect. The other two dialects are unchanged.

- BigQuery's `DATE`, `DATETIME` and `TIME` constructors build a value instead of answering NULL ([#613](https://github.com/nao1215/filesql/issues/613)). SQLite has functions of those names that read a time value and a list of modifiers, so `DATE(2024, 3, 5)` ran, found nothing it could read, and answered NULL -- a caller assembling a date from three integer columns got a NULL column and no error, which reads as missing data. All four constructors, `TIMESTAMP` included, are now rewritten onto helpers of this package's own, in every signature a file-backed query can reach, and a field outside its range is an error rather than a date carried into the next month.

- `DATE_DIFF` in weeks counts the week boundaries crossed rather than the seven-day spans between the two dates, so a Saturday and the Sunday after it are one week apart, as they are in BigQuery. It used to divide the day difference by seven, which answered 0 for that pair.

- An aggregate that expands into an expression is refused a window rather than reaching SQLite with one. `STDDEV`, `VARIANCE`, `COUNTIF` and the covariances become several aggregates inside arithmetic, and an `OVER` after the call belongs to none of them; left alone, SQLite reported on whichever generated function it happened to reach -- "sqrt() may not be used as a window function" for a standard deviation -- naming a function the query does not contain. All of them now fail translation with `ErrUnsupportedSyntax` saying so. The aggregates that translate to a single SQLite one, such as `LOGICAL_AND` and `ANY_VALUE`, carry a window as they always did.

- `TIME_TRUNC` to a millisecond or a microsecond keeps the fraction it truncated to, and `TIME_ADD` keeps the fraction of a second in its input; both used to spell a time of day with whole seconds only, so `TIME_TRUNC(TIME '13:04:05.123', MILLISECOND)` answered the second below it. `CURRENT_DATETIME` refuses a time-zone argument rather than accepting one and discarding it, which answered the local instant under another zone's name.

- BigQuery's array-returning aggregates are refused by name ([#619](https://github.com/nao1215/filesql/issues/619)). `ARRAY_AGG`, `ARRAY_CONCAT_AGG`, `APPROX_QUANTILES`, `APPROX_TOP_COUNT` and `APPROX_TOP_SUM` reached SQLite as "no such function", telling the caller that a name they did write does not exist rather than that the construct has no SQLite form -- which is what every other array-shaped construct in this package says. They now fail translation with `ErrUnsupportedSyntax` naming the array.

- `TO_CHAR` implements PostgreSQL's date/time template rather than a handful of its patterns ([#601](https://github.com/nao1215/filesql/issues/601)). The template was translated into a Go layout string, which cannot express what it says: a Go layout has one spelling per field, so `MONTH`, `Month` and `month` were one answer instead of three; it has no fixed-width form, so a day or month name was not padded to the nine columns PostgreSQL pads it to; and a pattern with no Go equivalent had to be copied out as literal text, which is how `TO_CHAR(d, 'DDD')` came to answer `05D` -- `DD` substituted and the remaining `D` passed along -- and `'IYYY'` to answer `I24Y`. A caller formatting a date got a plausible-looking string that was not the date, with nothing to report it. The template is now scanned into its own elements and rendered one at a time, covering `YYYY YYY YY Y Y,YYY IYYY IYY IY I BC/AD MM MONTH MON RM DD DDD IDDD D ID DAY DY W WW IW Q CC J HH HH12 HH24 MI SS SSSS MS US FF1-FF6 AM/PM` and the dotted `A.M./P.M.` in each of their case spellings, together with `TH`, the `FM` prefix and double-quoted literal text. `TO_DATE` reads its template through the same scanner, so there is one table where there were two.

- `TO_CHAR`'s numeric template implements the elements it used to ignore ([#602](https://github.com/nao1215/filesql/issues/602)). It read digit positions and a decimal point and silently dropped the rest: `FM` did not suppress the padding it exists to suppress, so `TO_CHAR(12345.678, 'FM999999.00')` answered the number with the two leading spaces a caller writes `FM` to remove; `G` and `D` produced no separator and no point, so `'999D99'` dropped the fraction it asked for and `'999G999G999'` grouped nothing; `S`, `MI`, `PL`, `SG` and `PR` produced no sign at all, so `TO_CHAR(-12, '999PR')` answered ` -12` where PostgreSQL answers ` <12>`; `RN` answered NULL; and a value too wide for its template printed in full where PostgreSQL fills the field with `#`. All of those are implemented, along with `V` and the rounding of a half away from zero that SQL does and Go's own formatting does not. Every answer was read from PostgreSQL 17.10 over a matrix of thirty-four templates against thirteen values.

- PostgreSQL's `GREATEST` and `LEAST` skip their NULL arguments ([#600](https://github.com/nao1215/filesql/issues/600)). Both answered NULL as soon as one argument was NULL, which is what MySQL and BigQuery do and not what PostgreSQL does: it ignores the NULLs and answers the extreme of what is left, and answers NULL only when every argument is NULL. An empty cell loads as NULL, so `GREATEST(q1, q2, q3)` over three quarterly columns reported no maximum for every row that was missing a quarter, and nothing said so. The MySQL and GoogleSQL answers are unchanged.

- `SUBSTRING(s FROM pattern)` returns the text the pattern matches ([#604](https://github.com/nao1215/filesql/issues/604)). PostgreSQL spells its regular-expression extraction exactly like the positional form and tells the two apart by the type of the second operand; both were translated to the positional helper, which read a pattern such as `'[0-9]+'` as a start position, failed to make a number of it and answered NULL. A caller pulling a year out of a free-text column got a column of NULLs and no error, which reads as no match anywhere in the data. The reading is chosen while rewriting, from whether the operand is a string literal, because PostgreSQL decides on the static type rather than on the value -- `SUBSTRING('abc123' FROM '2')` is the match of the pattern 2 and not the substring from position 2 -- and SQLite has no declared type to consult once the query is running. A number literal keeps the positional reading. An operand that is neither literal -- a column, a placeholder, an expression -- carries no kind into the translation at all, so it goes to a helper that reads it from the value: a number is a position and anything else is a pattern, which is PostgreSQL's answer for every integer column and for every text column that does not hold digits. `SUBSTRING(s SIMILAR p ESCAPE e)`, whose third operand is an escape character rather than a length, is refused with `ErrUnsupportedSyntax` instead of reaching SQLite as a syntax error.

- An `INTERVAL` literal that no date arithmetic consumed is refused with `ErrUnsupportedSyntax` naming INTERVAL ([#605](https://github.com/nao1215/filesql/issues/605)). SQLite has no interval type, so `SELECT INTERVAL '3 days'` and `JUSTIFY_DAYS(INTERVAL '35 days')` had nothing to become; what the caller saw was `near "AS": syntax error` and `near "'35 days'": syntax error`, neither of which mentions the construct at fault. The refusal covers all three dialects and runs after the arithmetic rewrites, so every form that does translate is untouched.

- A division by zero is reported as a division by zero ([#610](https://github.com/nao1215/filesql/issues/610)). `ErrDivideByZero` wrapped `ErrInvalidCast`, so `SELECT 1 / 0` under the PostgreSQL and GoogleSQL dialects failed with `dialect: invalid cast: division by zero`, naming a conversion the query does not contain and sending the reader to look at their CAST expressions. The message is now `dialect: division by zero`. A caller matching `errors.Is(err, dialect.ErrInvalidCast)` to catch a division by zero stops matching; an error raised inside a SQLite user-defined function reaches the caller as the driver's error with the text in it rather than as a wrapped sentinel, so the text is what a caller had to work with either way.

### Changed

- The packages below the root gain concurrency tests. The root package has had a suite of them -- concurrent queries, auto-save, `LoadInto`, close -- and `dialect` and `prep` had none, so the state they keep was only ever reached one goroutine at a time and `-race` had nothing to report on. `dialect` now exercises its two process-wide TO_CHAR template caches from eight goroutines writing the same keys, and its registered helpers from several connections at once; `prep` pins what its struct-tag cache was built for, one `Processor` read by several goroutines. Both pass, which is the point: they were written to find nothing and to keep finding nothing.

- `dialect`'s benchmarks gain TO_CHAR, whose template is scanned on every call and so costs per row rather than per statement, and the scan is cached. Against v0.47.0, a date template is 0.87 times its old cost at 1.04 times its allocation, and a numeric one 1.10 times at 0.96 times, even though both now implement the whole template language rather than a handful of patterns: the parsed form of a template is kept in a bounded map, since the format is nearly always a literal in the query and the same string arrives once per row. The map stops growing at 256 entries, because the format can be a column and a cache keyed by data is a way to run out of memory on a large file. Translating a PostgreSQL query is 1.19 times its old cost at 1.14 times its allocation, which is the four token passes this release adds -- the leftover-INTERVAL check, BETWEEN SYMMETRIC, and the two keyword rewrites -- and which a caller pays once per prepared statement rather than once per row. The GoogleSQL work that follows costs nothing measurable: translating a GoogleSQL query allocates the same bytes it did before, and every per-row helper allocates byte for byte what it allocated before, the new names being new registrations rather than changes to the existing paths. The ns/op figures across a run of that benchmark vary by a fifth in either direction on this machine, which is why the allocation counts are what the comparison rests on.

### Documentation

- `SECURITY.md` names the current release series ([#608](https://github.com/nao1215/filesql/issues/608)). The supported-versions table still said `0.46.x` after v0.47.0 was tagged, and told a reader on an older series to upgrade to the latest `0.46.x` release. A reporter reads that table to decide whether the version they found a problem in is still supported, and it is edited by hand at release time, so a test now keeps it true: it reads the newest released version out of `CHANGELOG.md` and fails when `SECURITY.md` does not mark that minor series supported.

- `dialect`'s package documentation says what the translation cannot reach, which is SQLite's type system rather than any particular function. SQLite has no boolean, no interval, no array and no arbitrary-precision numeric, so a comparison answers 1 or 0, an INTERVAL literal works only in date arithmetic, and the functions whose result would be one of those types -- `AGE`, `JUSTIFY_DAYS`, `REGEXP_MATCH`, `SCALE`, `TRIM_SCALE` -- are not implemented because there would be no value to return. One consequence has no error to report it and is now written down: subtracting one timestamp from another is an interval in PostgreSQL and an ordinary subtraction to SQLite, so `ts2 - ts1` answers a number rather than a span and for two dates in the same year answers 0. The operands are columns as often as literals and a token rewrite cannot see their types, so `DATE_PART` and `DATE_DIFF` are the ways to ask that question here. The README says the same in two sentences.

- `prep`'s package documentation is precise about what its memory holds. It said prep loads the entire file into memory and that `ProcessToWriter` reduces the peak; what is live at once is the parsed rows, one struct per row, and, for `Process`, the output buffer, and `ProcessToWriter` removes the third of those alone. The section now says which three and why the first two cannot go: the struct slice is what the caller asked for, and preprocessing and validation are separate passes over the same rows.

- `dialect`'s package documentation covers the GoogleSQL work: the two kinds of name its own file holds -- BigQuery functions SQLite has nothing like, and names another dialect here already means something else by -- and the SAFE. prefix, which is a wrapper rather than a name and is dropped for a function SQLite computes itself. The list of what the translation cannot reach gains BigQuery's array-returning aggregates and time zones, which there is no type for either: the forms that take a zone are refused rather than answered unshifted, since that would be a different instant. `ExampleTranslate_googleSQL` runs three BigQuery constructs SQLite has no form for, and the README example index gains its row.

## [0.47.0] - 2026-08-26

### Breaking Changes

- `frame.DataFrame`'s `DistinctBy` and `DropNASubset` return an error, and a column name the frame does not have is refused rather than acted on. Both took a list of key columns and read a missing one out of each row's map, where an absent key is nil — so a misspelled name was not a mistake to report but a column every row shares the same missing value in. `DistinctBy("typo")` keyed every row identically and kept one of them; `DropNASubset("typo")` found a missing value in every row and kept none. Two rows became one row and two rows became zero rows, and both answers are a frame the caller can go on using, so nothing anywhere said a name had gone unread. The two now check every name they are given before they read a single row, and report the first one the frame does not have as an error wrapping `frame.ErrColumnNotFound` — the same sentinel `GroupBy` and the aggregations already answer with, and the check runs in front so a call that refuses one name has not already dropped rows for another. Passing no columns still returns a copy of the frame, which is what both did before and what makes `Distinct` and `DropNA` expressible in terms of them. Migration: `unique := df.DistinctBy("email")` becomes `unique, err := df.DistinctBy("email")`, and the same for `DropNASubset`; a caller who knows the column is there can write `unique, _ := df.DistinctBy("email")`, though the point of the change is that a typo is exactly the case where nobody knows. `Distinct()` and `DropNA()` keep their signatures and return no error: they name every column of the frame, so there is no name for the frame not to have. Internally each now calls a helper the other half shares, rather than the exported method, so the whole-frame halves did not have to grow an error they can never return.

- Four more names leave the exported surface. Each was exported, so an outside caller could name it and one who did has to migrate; what they had in common is that nothing in this repository called them and none of them did anything a caller cannot do directly. `parser.ExcelSheetSource` is the one that was genuinely unreachable: it was an alias of the reader's own interface, and once `ExcelSheets` and `SelectExcelSheets` were removed it appeared in no exported signature, so it named a type a caller had nothing to pass it to; the three unexported helpers in the root package that took it now name `internal/reader`'s type directly, and `parser.ExcelSheet`, `parser.ExcelSheetPolicy` and its two constants stay, because those are what `Parse` and `WithExcelSheetPolicy` take. `prep.DetectFileType` and `prep.IsCompressed` each forwarded one line to the parser function of the same name and were called from nowhere inside prep; `prep.FileType` is an alias of `parser.FileType`, so a caller passes the same values to `parser.DetectFileType` and `parser.IsCompressed` and gets the same answers, and the `prep.FileType` alias with its `FileType*` constants is untouched. `CompressionFactory.CreateWriterForFile` was dead: nothing called it, not even this package's own dump paths, and its only callers were the tests written for it -- a caller who wants what it did writes `os.Create(path)` and hands the file to `NewCompressionHandler(compressionType).CreateWriter`, which is the two lines the method wrapped and which the round-trip test in this repository now uses. The rest of `CompressionFactory` -- `CreateHandlerForFile`, `CreateReaderForFile`, `DetectCompressionType`, `GetBaseFileType`, `RemoveCompressionExtension` -- and `NewCompressionHandler` are unchanged.

- `dialect.RegisterTranslator` and `dialect.TranslatorFunc` are removed, and a dialect this package does not implement is refused rather than made to work by a caller. The registry was an extension point with no user: it let a caller install a translation function under a dialect name of their own, which meant a query could be rewritten by code this package cannot test, against a dialect whose behavior it makes no claim about, and the difference between a supported dialect and an invented one stopped being visible from here. The four built-in dialects are what filesql translates, and a dialect it does not cover is worth adding properly rather than routing around: a request for one belongs in an issue against filesql, which is also how the translation gets tests, documentation and a place in `Dialects()`. `Translate` is correspondingly simpler -- SQLite is passed through, everything else goes to the built-in translation, and an unrecognized dialect is `ErrUnknownDialect` -- and the registry, its mutex and the lookup go with it. `RegisterFunctions`, `Parse`, `Dialects`, `DisplayName`, the four `Dialect` constants and the three sentinel errors are all unchanged, and so is `Translate`'s signature; what changes about `Translate` is the branch described above, so a dialect that had a translator registered for it now answers `ErrUnknownDialect` where it used to answer that translator's output. Migration: a caller who registered a translator has no replacement and should open an issue for the dialect they need; nothing else changes, and a build configured with `WithDialect` on an unknown dialect is still rejected by `Build` with `ErrDatabaseOperation` naming it, as it was before.

- `WithLogger` takes a `*slog.Logger`, and the `Logger` interface with its adapter is gone. This package defined a five-method `Logger` interface -- `Debug`, `Info`, `Warn`, `Error` and a `With` returning another `Logger` -- alongside `SlogAdapter` and `NewSlogAdapter` to turn a `*slog.Logger` into one. The interface was shaped after `slog.Logger` and its doc comment said so, `log/slog` has been in the standard library since Go 1.21 while this module requires 1.25, and the only implementation anyone was expected to pass was the adapter, so the abstraction had one real inhabitant and cost a wrapper allocation and an indirection per call to reach it. `Logger`, `SlogAdapter` and `NewSlogAdapter` are removed and `WithLogger` now takes the standard type directly; the default stays a logger that discards everything, built on `slog.DiscardHandler`, and `WithLogger(nil)` still leaves the current logger in place. Nothing about what is logged changes: the same messages at the same levels with the same attributes. Migration: a caller writing `WithLogger(filesql.NewSlogAdapter(logger))` passes `logger` itself, and a caller who implemented `Logger` writes the same logic as a `slog.Handler` and passes `slog.New(handler)` -- which is also what decides levels and destination now, rather than anything this package offers. The one thing the interface allowed that the standard type does not is a logger that is not slog at all; a caller in that position writes a `slog.Handler` that forwards to it, which is the adapter direction slog is designed for.

- Read-only mode is a `*sql.DB` whose connections refuse to write, rather than a wrapper type of filesql's own. `(*DBBuilder).OpenReadOnly` returned a `*ReadOnlyDB`, a hand-written stand-in for `*sql.DB` that classified each statement by reading its SQL text and answered `ErrReadOnly` for the ones it called writes. That surface -- `ReadOnlyDB`, `ReadOnlyTx`, `ReadOnlyStmt`, `NewReadOnlyDB`, `ErrReadOnly` and the internal `sqlguard` package behind them -- is gone, and `OpenReadOnly` now returns an ordinary `(*sql.DB, error)` whose every connection is opened with SQLite's `query_only` pragma. The refusal moves from this package into the engine, which makes it complete rather than best-effort: text classification had to be taught about every way a statement can write, and a write reached through `Query` rather than `Exec`, or through a `RETURNING` clause, was only refused because the classifier had been taught that particular shape. The pragma is on the connection, so a write is refused whichever entry point issues it, including ones a wrapper could not report an error from at all. Migration: a caller who wrote `rodb, err := b.OpenReadOnly(ctx)` keeps the same line and now holds a `*sql.DB`, with the full `database/sql` API rather than the subset the wrapper reimplemented, including the `Begin`/`Prepare`/`Stmt` types it had to shadow. A caller who wrapped their own database with `NewReadOnlyDB(db)` has no direct replacement and should open the database read-only through `OpenReadOnly` instead. A caller matching `errors.Is(err, filesql.ErrReadOnly)` should test that the statement failed, since the error is now the driver's: SQLite reports `attempt to write a readonly database` (`SQLITE_READONLY`). One thing the pragma does not do is bind a caller who does not want to be bound -- `PRAGMA query_only = 0` on the connection lifts it -- so the guarantee is against writing by accident, which is what the mode was for, rather than against a caller who sets out to write. `WithDialect` and `EnableAutoSave` compose with it unchanged: the translated handle and the auto-saving handle open their connections through the same read-only DSN, so a read-only database still translates its queries and still writes its tables out when it is closed.

- Three names leave the root package's exported surface. `DBTX`, the execution surface `*sql.DB` and `*sql.Tx` share, was named in no exported signature: every function taking one is unexported, so the interface was a name a caller could write down but never had a reason to, and it is now `dbtx`. `ExcelSheetSource` was an alias of the reader's own interface, used only by three unexported helpers in this package; `parser.ExcelSheetSource`, which it pointed at, is removed in this release too, so a caller has no exported name for the type at all -- and nothing to pass one to, since the two functions that took it, `parser.ExcelSheets` and `parser.SelectExcelSheets`, are gone as well. A caller who wants what those did has `ExcelSheetsInFile` and `ExcelSheetsInReader`, which take a path and an `io.Reader` and are unchanged. `DefaultChunkSize` was the constant `SetDefaultChunkSize` starts from; the number it held is stated in that method's godoc, which is where a caller reading about chunk size already is, so the constant becomes internal rather than a second place the default has to be kept true. The Excel policy names beside it -- `ExcelSheetPolicy`, `ExcelSheet`, `ExcelSheetPolicyAll` and `ExcelSheetPolicyVisibleOnly` -- are unchanged, as are `ExcelSheetsInFile`, `ExcelSheetsInReader` and `ExcelSheetTableNames`.

- The `parser` package stops re-exporting `internal/reader` and unexports its file extensions. `NormalizeXLSXDates`, `CSVReader`, `NewCSVReader`, `TSVReader`, `NewTSVReader` and `NormalizeLineEndings` were each a one-line forward to the reader that does the work, and none of them was reached from anywhere: the root package's XLSX dump already calls `internal/reader` directly, and `Parse` is the entry point this package exists for. Removing `NormalizeXLSXDates` also takes `*excelize.File` out of every exported signature filesql has, so the spreadsheet library it reads workbooks with is no longer part of what a caller compiles against. A caller who was reading CSV through `parser.NewCSVReader` should use `encoding/csv`, or `parser.Parse`, which is the path that keeps the CRLF-inside-quotes behavior the reader existed for. `ParseValue` goes with them, since the typed value it built is what a `database/sql` scan already produces from a loaded table, and `parser`'s doc.go no longer has a Type Conversion section pointing at it. `ExcelSheets` and `SelectExcelSheets` are removed, and so is `ExcelSheetSource`, the interface they took: with both functions gone it appeared in no exported signature, so it named a type a caller had nothing to pass it to. `ExcelSheet`, `ExcelSheetPolicy` and its two constants stay, because those name what `Parse` and `WithExcelSheetPolicy` take, and a caller who wants the listing rather than the selection has `filesql.ExcelSheetsInFile` and `filesql.ExcelSheetsInReader`, which are unchanged. The sixteen `Ext*` constants -- `ExtCSV` through `ExtLZ4` -- become internal: they spelled the suffixes this package's own detection compares against, and the root package, their only caller outside tests, now names the codec constants and the literals directly. `ErrCSVSyntax`, `ErrTSVSyntax` and `ErrTSVUnrepresentable` are untouched, since a `Parse` failure wraps the first two and the root package's TSV writer reports the third.

- The forty compressed `FileType` constants leave the `frame` package. `CSVGZ`, `TSVBZ2`, `LTSVXZ`, `ParquetZSTD`, `XLSXLZ4` and the rest of that five-by-eight grid were aliases of the `parser` constants of the same names, and `frame.FileType` is itself an alias of `parser.FileType`, so a caller who has to name a compressed form passes `parser.CSVGZ` and it is the same value the removed constant held. Nothing in this package or in the examples used them: `frame` decides a file's type from its path, and the only two functions that read a `FileType` here reduce it to its base type or its delimiter, both of which a compressed constant answers exactly as its uncompressed counterpart does. The uncompressed five -- `CSV`, `TSV`, `LTSV`, `Parquet` and `XLSX` -- stay, since those are what a caller writes when naming a format, and reading a compressed file is unchanged in every way: a path ending `.csv.gz` is still detected, decompressed and loaded without the caller naming anything.

- `FromFile` and `TableSet.ToFile` are unexported in both `parser/ach` and `parser/wire`, which takes the moov-io types out of filesql's exported surface entirely. `ParseReader` already did what `FromFile` did, from an `io.Reader` rather than from a `*ach.File` or `*wire.File` the caller had read themselves, and `WriteToWriter` already did what `ToFile` did, writing the rebuilt file rather than handing back the library value; both pairs existed side by side, so the packages whose stated purpose is that a caller need not import moov-io named moov-io types in four signatures anyway. A caller reading a file should use `ach.ParseReader` or `wire.ParseReader`, and one writing a modified `TableSet` back out should use `WriteToWriter`, which is the path the root package's `DumpACH`, `DumpFedWire`, `DumpACHWithTableSet` and `DumpFedWireWithTableSet` take and which is unchanged. Everything else on `TableSet` -- the `Get*` accessors and the `Update*FromTableData` setters -- is unchanged, and so is the round-trip they support. The `parser/ach` package documentation's usage example, which read a file with `moovach.ReadFile` and converted it with `FromFile`, now shows `ParseReader` and `WriteToWriter` instead.

### Added

- The MySQL dialect gains the TIME functions, completing [#595](https://github.com/nao1215/filesql/issues/595): `SEC_TO_TIME`, `TIME_TO_SEC`, `MAKETIME`, `TIME_FORMAT`, `ADDTIME`, `SUBTIME`, `MICROSECOND`, `TO_DAYS`, `FROM_DAYS`, `MAKEDATE`, `PERIOD_ADD` and `PERIOD_DIFF`. They needed a value of their own rather than the datetime helpers already here, which is why they came in a second round: a MySQL TIME is a signed span running from -838:59:59 to 838:59:59 and not a point on a clock, so `SEC_TO_TIME(360000)` is `100:00:00`, `TIME_TO_SEC('-01:01:01')` is -3661, a value past the range is clamped rather than refused, and `TIME_FORMAT` prints an hour field that can be three digits with the sign in front of the whole result rather than on each part -- `TIME_FORMAT('-01:30:45', '%k %p')` is `-1 AM`. Every answer was read from MySQL 8.4.11, including the whole `TIME_FORMAT` specifier table, where reading the documentation does not tell you that `%Y` prints `0000` while `%M` answers NULL and an unknown letter prints itself. The colonless form is read too, where the last two digits are the seconds and whatever is left is the hours, so `TIME_TO_SEC('1234')` is 754. `MAKETIME` refuses a minute or second outside 0..59 rather than carrying it, `MAKEDATE` refuses a day number below one and rolls one past the end of the year into the next, `FROM_DAYS` answers the zero date below the year one and NULL past 9999, and `PERIOD_ADD` and `PERIOD_DIFF` refuse a period whose month is not a month, which is what MySQL does with each of them. Three answers are deliberately not MySQL's and are pinned as tests so they cannot drift: an ISO timestamp with a `T` between the date and the time keeps its time of day, where MySQL coerces the whole string and answers a number built from its digits; a string that is not a TIME is NULL rather than a prefix salvaged from it; and a fraction of a second is printed only when there is one, with its trailing zeros removed, because MySQL takes the number of digits from the type of the argument and SQLite has no type to take it from.

- The MySQL dialect gains the scalar functions it had no SQLite spelling for ([#595](https://github.com/nao1215/filesql/issues/595)). A query that used one of them was told "no such function" about a name the caller did write and MySQL does define, and several of the missing names were plain spellings of something this package already had: `LCASE` and `UCASE` are `LOWER` and `UPPER`, `MID` is `SUBSTRING`, `DAYOFMONTH` is `DAY`, and `REGEXP_LIKE` is the `REGEXP` operator, so a query failed over which of two names for one function its author happened to type. Added, with the answer for every edge case read from MySQL 8.4 rather than derived: `LCASE`, `UCASE`, `MID`, `DAYOFMONTH`, `ISNULL`, `STRCMP`, `BIT_LENGTH`, `INSERT`, `TO_BASE64`, `FROM_BASE64`, `CONV`, `BIN`, `OCT`, `BIT_COUNT`, `COT`, the two-argument `ATAN`, `CRC32`, `REGEXP_LIKE`, `REGEXP_SUBSTR`, `REGEXP_INSTR`, `SHA1`, `SHA2`, `INET_ATON`, `INET_NTOA`, `IS_IPV4` and `IS_IPV6`. The edges are where these are easy to get wrong and are pinned as tests: `CONV` reads unsigned, stops at the first character its base does not have so `CONV('12abc', 10, 10)` is 12, saturates rather than wrapping, and takes a negative base as a request for the signed reading; `INSERT` leaves the string alone for a position outside it and replaces to the end for a negative length; `TO_BASE64` breaks its output into lines of 76 characters the way MySQL does, and `FROM_BASE64` ignores the whitespace that puts back; `INET_ATON` reads a short address by filling the top bytes from the left, so `1.2` is 1.0.0.2; and the three `REGEXP_` functions fold case by default and take the same match_type argument as `REGEXP_REPLACE`, counting positions in characters rather than bytes. `ISNULL`, `INSERT` and the two-argument `ATAN` are rewritten rather than registered, because `ISNULL` and `INSERT` are SQLite keywords and its `atan` takes one argument; everything else keeps the call text the query wrote, so the result column keeps its name. `STRCMP` is the one approximation: it folds case, which MySQL's default collation does, and not accents, which that collation also does, so `STRCMP('é', 'e')` is 0 in MySQL and a difference here. One other answer is deliberately not MySQL's: an occurrence argument at or above 2^32 wraps MySQL's own counter and makes `REGEXP_SUBSTR` answer the first match, where an occurrence past the number of matches has none and that is what this answers. The date and time functions from the same list are in the entry below.

- A field tagged `validate:"-"` is validated by nothing ([#578](https://github.com/nao1215/filesql/issues/578)). The go-playground dialect reserves `-` for a field it checks nothing on, and prep, whose doc.go names that dialect and whose own history includes adding `alphanum` because a caller arrives with tags copied from a go-playground struct, refused it: the tag was looked up as a validator name and the load failed with `unknown validate tag "-"`, so nothing loaded at all. Only the whole tag means a skip, since inside a list `-` is a tag name like any other and an unknown one is what a caller who wrote it there needs to be told; `validate:"-,required"` therefore still fails. The field is still read from its column and still preprocessed by its `prep` tag, because `-` speaks about validation alone, as it does in the dialect.

- The `dialect` package gains benchmarks, which it had none of. `BenchmarkTranslate` measures the rewrite of one query per dialect and `BenchmarkDialectFunctions` the helpers a translated query calls once per row, so the cost of a rewrite rule is measurable where it was not. They record what the fixes below cost. A query holding five calls this release started rewriting -- `LEFT`, `FORMAT`, `LOG`, `REGEXP_REPLACE` and a `CAST` -- translates in 14.0 us against 10.4 us before, allocating 70.0 KB against 49.8 KB, because each of those calls is now rewritten and given the alias that keeps its result column name, at the same per-call cost as every other rewrite rule in the package, and because two more passes walk the token stream for the shift and regular-expression rewrites below; a translation runs once per prepared statement, not once per row. The per-row helpers move less: `LEFT` and `to_hex` are within a twentieth of what they were, MySQL's `REGEXP_REPLACE` is 1.14 times its old cost for reading a position it used to ignore, and PostgreSQL's is slightly cheaper for replacing one match rather than all of them. GoogleSQL's `FORMAT` is 1.31 times the cost of the printf call it replaces, which is the price of printing a value where the old path answered NULL.

### Fixed

- MySQL's `REGEXP` and `RLIKE` now fold case, and so does `REGEXP_REPLACE` ([#589](https://github.com/nao1215/filesql/issues/589)). MySQL matches a regular expression under the collation of its operands, and its default collation folds case beyond ASCII: `'abc' REGEXP 'B'` is true there and was false here, and `REGEXP_REPLACE('aAa', 'a', 'X')` answered `XAX` where MySQL answers `XXX`. The translation succeeded either way, so a filter written against MySQL quietly selected fewer rows and a replacement quietly left some occurrences alone. This dialect had already made the same decision for LIKE, which is routed through a case-insensitive helper for exactly this reason, so the two halves of one dialect disagreed about the same letter. Both spellings of the operator now become a call to a MySQL-specific helper that compiles the pattern with Go's `(?i)` flag, which folds the whole of Unicode rather than ASCII alone, and the flag is written in front of the pattern so a caller's own `(?-i)` still turns it off for the rest of the pattern. `REGEXP_REPLACE`'s match_type argument keeps working in both directions: `c` asks for case-sensitive matching and `i` for case-insensitive. The shared `regexp` helper is untouched, because PostgreSQL's `~` and GoogleSQL's `REGEXP_CONTAINS` are correctly case-sensitive and reach it. Case folding stops at the calls this package owns: `=`, `IN`, `BETWEEN`, `ORDER BY`, `DISTINCT` and `GROUP BY` compare inside the engine, where a token rewrite cannot reach all of them, so `'abc' = 'ABC'` is still false here and true in MySQL. That limit is now written down in the package documentation rather than left to be discovered.

- `QUOTE` under the MySQL dialect now produces a literal MySQL can read back ([#590](https://github.com/nao1215/filesql/issues/590)). The call reached SQLite's own `quote()`, which escapes a single quote by doubling it, gives a backslash no special meaning and leaves a number unquoted -- all correct for SQLite and none of it what MySQL does. `QUOTE('O''Reilly')` came back as `'O''Reilly'` instead of `'O\'Reilly'`, `QUOTE(1.5)` as the bare number, and a value holding a backslash came back as text that is no longer valid MySQL at all. Producing a pasteable literal is the whole point of the function, so a caller building SQL from it got either a different literal than the one they meant or a broken statement, with nothing to say so. MySQL's `QUOTE` now has a helper of its own: the value is wrapped in single quotes and each single quote, backslash, NUL and Control-Z is preceded by a backslash, a number is converted to text and quoted like anything else, and NULL answers the four-character word `NULL` without quotes. PostgreSQL and GoogleSQL keep SQLite's `quote()`.

- `ASCII` under the MySQL dialect now answers the first byte ([#591](https://github.com/nao1215/filesql/issues/591)). MySQL documents `ASCII` as returning a value in 0..255 and points a caller who wants the whole character at `ORD`, which this package already routes to a MySQL-specific helper. `ASCII` did not have one, so it reached the shared helper that answers a code point -- PostgreSQL's meaning for the name -- and `ASCII('日')` was 26085 where MySQL says 230. The two functions had become the same function, and any comparison against a literal byte value silently failed to match. `ASCII` is now rewritten onto its own helper that takes the leading byte of the value's UTF-8 form, 0 for an empty string and NULL for NULL. PostgreSQL's `ascii()` is unchanged.

- `HEX` under the MySQL dialect now writes hexadecimal for a negative number, and reads its argument's type rather than its contents ([#592](https://github.com/nao1215/filesql/issues/592)). A negative number was formatted as a signed decimal, so `HEX(-1)` answered `-1` and `HEX(-255)` answered `-FF`. Neither holds hexadecimal digits, so `UNHEX(HEX(n))` -- the round trip the pair exists for -- answered NULL for every negative n, and a caller hexing an integer column that happened to contain one got output that was silently unusable rather than an error. A fraction was truncated where MySQL rounds, so `HEX(1.9)` was `1` rather than `2`. Separately, a string whose digits spell a number was hexed as that number: `HEX('255')` answered `FF` where MySQL answers `323535`, the bytes of the three characters, because MySQL chooses between its two forms by the argument's type and not by its contents -- and a column filesql typed as TEXT is a string to that rule. A number is now rounded half away from zero, reinterpreted as unsigned and printed in uppercase without a sign, and anything arriving as a string is hexed byte for byte.

- MySQL's `<<` and `>>` now shift the way MySQL shifts ([#593](https://github.com/nao1215/filesql/issues/593)). Both operators reached SQLite unchanged, and SQLite's shifts are signed where MySQL's are unsigned: `>>` copied the sign bit instead of bringing in zeros, so `-1 >> 1` stayed `-1` where MySQL answers 9223372036854775807, a shift by 64 or more left a negative value untouched where MySQL clears it, and a negative count was read as a shift in the other direction where MySQL reads the count as unsigned and so shifts past the width. These are different bits, not a different way of printing the same bits, and the query answered without a word about it. Both operators are now rewritten onto helpers that shift the unsigned 64-bit value. The operands are taken across the arithmetic rather than as the single primary beside the operator, because MySQL and SQLite agree that `*`, `/`, `%`, `+` and `-` all bind tighter than a shift: `1 + 2 >> 1` is `(1 + 2) >> 1` to both, and reading only the primary would have answered 2 where both engines answer 1. They stop at `&` and `|`, which MySQL puts below the shifts, so `1 | 4 >> 1` groups MySQL's way. A leading `~` or sign belongs to the operand, and a sign is told from the binary operator by what stands before it, where an unquoted word is a keyword or a column depending on which of the two keyword tables lists it. What remains different is only how a result with its top bit set is spelled: SQLite has no unsigned 64-bit integer, so MySQL's 18446744073709551615 for `~0` comes back here as `-1`, the same bits under the only reading SQLite has for them. That ceiling now sits in the package documentation.

- `IF` no longer takes the true branch for a string with no number in it ([#594](https://github.com/nao1215/filesql/issues/594)). The condition was read as true for any non-empty string, so `IF('abc', a, b)` answered a where MySQL and SQLite both answer b: both read a string in boolean context as the number its leading digits spell, and `'abc'` spells none. The helper's own comment said it applied SQLite-like truthiness, which it did not. A text column is exactly where this lands, since filesql types a column as TEXT as soon as one value in it would break a numeric column, so a flag column holding `0` and `1` alongside a stray `no` took the true branch for every `no`. A string is now read by its longest numeric prefix, ignoring leading space, and is false when that prefix is empty or zero, so `'abc'`, `'true'`, `''`, `'0.0'` and `'0abc'` are false and `'1abc'`, `' 1 '`, `'-1'` and `'1e2'` are true. A byte slice is read the same way rather than by its length. The non-strict `CAST(x AS BOOLEAN)` shares the helper and follows the same rule, which is what a cast that is not strict is asking for.

- Editing an ACH row's `batch_index` or `entry_index` no longer overwrites a different entry ([#587](https://github.com/nao1215/filesql/issues/587)). Those columns, and `addenda_index` beside them, say which record a row's other columns belong to; they are the position of a record, not a value it stores. Nothing stopped a caller from changing one and nothing reported it when they did: the update was applied to whatever record the new coordinate landed on, so a row pointed at another row's coordinates overwrote that entry's account number, amount and trace number, while the row that was edited came back unchanged. The table then read as if the edit had been ignored and a different entry had silently become a copy of this one, which for a payment file is the worst way to be wrong -- the file is still valid ACH, it balances, and it now contains one payment twice. Whether a caller was told anything depended only on whether the new coordinate happened to fall outside the file, and the tables disagreed about even that: entries and batches reported an out-of-range coordinate as an error while addenda and all three IAT tables skipped the row in silence, so the rows most likely to be edited by hand were the ones that said nothing. A write now holds every row against the coordinate it was read from and refuses one that has moved, with the error giving the row, the position it names and the position it came from. Comparing against the parsed coordinate is what catches the case a uniqueness check alone would not: two rows that exchange positions keep every coordinate unique and in range while each one points at the other's record, so the two payments trade account numbers and amounts. Out of range and named twice are refused too, in every table rather than in the two that used to report them, and so is a row the file did not have, since adding rows was never supported and used to be applied anyway. The addenda tables get the same guard, which also settles a second way they were loose: the singleton addenda types ignore `addenda_index` when they apply a row, so an out-of-range one used to update the record it happened to land beside. Editing a value column is untouched, which is what these tables are for: only the coordinates are now off limits, and they were never something a caller could usefully change, because moving a record between batches is a structural edit this package has always said it does not do.

- A Fedwire write-back can no longer replace one field with the value of another ([#586](https://github.com/nao1215/filesql/issues/586)). `github.com/moov-io/wire@v0.16.1` writes the remittance originator's fourth address line from its first -- `FormatAddressLineFour` reads `AddressLineOne` -- so a file that was read and written straight back came out with line four replaced by a copy of line one and the original gone, with nothing to say so and no edit required to trigger it. The repository's own fixture hid it by repeating "Address Line One" in the line-four position, so the wrong value happened to equal the right one and a round-trip test passed. filesql cannot make that write correct without patching another package's output, so it refuses it instead: a write now stages the message, reads it back, and compares it column by column against the table it was written from, and a field that did not survive fails the whole write before a single byte reaches the caller's writer. The caller keeps the file they started with and is told which field could not be written, which is the only safe answer here -- a silently wrong payment file is worse than a failed export, because the failure is visible at once and the corruption is not. The check is not specific to that field: it covers all 326 columns, so the next defect of this shape in the same dependency is caught the same way, and when the current one is fixed upstream the affected messages simply start writing again with no change here. A message whose fourth address line differs from its first cannot be exported until then; every other message is unaffected, and reading is unaffected in all cases.

- `frame.DataFrame`'s `ToCSV` and `ToTSV` cannot destroy the file they fail to write. Both began by calling `os.Create`, which truncates the destination before a byte has been produced, and both can fail after that: TSV has no quoting, so the writer refuses a value holding a tab or a newline, and it refuses it partway through the rows. A frame with one tab in one cell therefore returned an error and left an empty file behind — and for the caller these methods are for, one who read a file, changed it and is writing it back, that file is the data. The same shape could hit `ToCSV`, whose writer refuses nothing but whose disk can still fill up. Both now write to a temporary file in the destination's directory and move it onto the destination only after the last row is written, the writer is flushed and the staged file is closed, so a refusal costs nothing and the destination is either its old contents or its new ones. The close is checked rather than deferred and dropped, since a buffered write can fail there and nowhere else. Neither a success nor a failure leaves the staged file behind. This is the staging `DumpDatabase` and auto-save have used since they had the same fault, and it is now one implementation in `internal/atomicwrite` that all of them call: `frame` cannot import the root package, so the machinery moved down rather than being written twice. The root package keeps its own wording for a staging failure — `ErrIOOperation`, with a staged file it could not remove afterwards joined on as `ErrCleanup` — and nothing about the errors `DumpDatabase` reports changes.

- A validator that needs a parameter is no longer dropped in silence when it has none ([#577](https://github.com/nao1215/filesql/issues/577)). Thirteen tags built no validator at all for an empty parameter -- startswith, startsnotwith, endswith, endsnotwith, contains, containsany, containsrune, excludes, excludesall, excludesrune, eq_ignore_case, ne_ignore_case and datetime -- and they did it whether or not `WithStrictTagParsing` was on, since their builders discarded the strict flag. The tag was accepted, nothing ran, and every value passed. The one most easily reached is datetime: `validate:"datetime"` with no layout is the natural way to write it wrong, and the column it was meant to guard was then unchecked, with a row holding "abc" in a date column reported valid. A parameter these tags cannot work without is now a malformed tag argument, which is what strict mode exists to report and what its godoc promises -- an error naming the tag, where non-strict keeps ignoring it. Following the dialect literally was the other option and is worse: go-playground compares against the empty parameter, so `excludes=` would refuse every value and `eq_ignore_case=` every non-empty one, turning a typo into a file where nothing is valid. The tags whose empty parameter means something are untouched: `eq=` and `ne=` still compare against the empty string, and `contains=`, `startswith=` and `endswith=` still find it in every value.

- The pad preprocessors are documented with the separator that works, and every prep tag is written down ([#579](https://github.com/nao1215/filesql/issues/579)). Two comments in this repository -- on the tag constant in prep/tag.go and inside the parser -- said `pad_left=N,char`, a form that cannot work: the prep tag is split on commas before any tag is looked at, so the pad character was read as a tag of its own and the load failed with `unknown prep tag "0"`, naming something the caller never wrote and saying nothing about padding. The separator the parser reads is a colon, which is what every test, example and benchmark in the repository writes, and the comments now say so. The second half is that a caller had nowhere else to look: doc.go listed six prep tags out of the twenty-five the parser accepts, and unlike the validator section it had no pointer to the rest, so pad_left, replace, truncate, regex_replace, coerce, nullify, fix_scheme, trim_set and the others were not written down anywhere. The list is now complete, with the parameter each tag takes, and a test holds the parser's vocabulary against it so it cannot drift again.

- `LOG` answers the logarithm each dialect means by it ([#568](https://github.com/nao1215/filesql/issues/568)). SQLite spells the base-ten logarithm `log(x)` and writes the base first in `log(base, x)`; MySQL and GoogleSQL mean the natural logarithm by the one-argument form, and GoogleSQL writes the base second. The call was passed through untranslated, so `LOG(10)` answered 1 under both dialects where the engines answer 2.302585092994046, and `LOG(8, 2)` answered 0.3333333333333334 under GoogleSQL where BigQuery answers 3 -- a plausible number in both cases, with nothing to say the query had been read as a different function. The one-argument form is now rewritten to `ln` for MySQL and GoogleSQL, and GoogleSQL's two-argument form has its arguments swapped. PostgreSQL is unchanged, because its `log` means what SQLite's means in both arities, and so are `LOG2` and `LOG10`, which every dialect here agrees on.

- `to_hex` under the PostgreSQL dialect converts an integer instead of hexing the text of one ([#569](https://github.com/nao1215/filesql/issues/569)). The helper functions are registered on the driver under bare names shared by every dialect, and `to_hex` was bound to the GoogleSQL helper, which hexes the bytes of its argument: `to_hex(255)` answered `323535`, the hex of the three characters `255`, where PostgreSQL answers `ff`. Both answers are hex digits, so only the length gives the wrong one away. PostgreSQL's call now goes to a helper of its own, which reads a negative as a 64-bit two's complement value the way the engine does -- `to_hex(-1)` is sixteen `f`s -- and refuses a value that names no integer, since PostgreSQL has no string form of the function. GoogleSQL's `TO_HEX` keeps hexing bytes, which is the only form BigQuery has.

- `REGEXP_REPLACE` reads its arguments the way the source dialect writes them ([#570](https://github.com/nao1215/filesql/issues/570)). One helper served all three dialects and implemented PostgreSQL's signature only in part. MySQL's fourth argument is a 1-based character position to start from and its fifth selects one match to replace, but the helper read the fourth as PostgreSQL's flag string, so `REGEXP_REPLACE('aaa', 'a', 'X', 2)` answered `Xaa` where MySQL answers `aXX`, and the five-argument form MySQL documents was rejected outright. PostgreSQL's three-argument form replaces the first match alone and needs a `g` flag for the rest, but the shared helper replaced every match, so `regexp_replace('aaa', 'a', 'X')` answered `XXX` where PostgreSQL answers `Xaa`. Each dialect now has its own helper: MySQL's takes three to six arguments, counts the start position in characters, treats an occurrence of 0 as every match from there, refuses a position outside the subject the way the engine does, honors the `c`, `i`, `m` and `n` match types and refuses `u`, which has no Go equivalent; PostgreSQL's replaces one match unless the flags say otherwise. GoogleSQL keeps the three-argument replace-all form, which is what BigQuery means, and so does a query written in the SQLite dialect.

- `FORMAT(x, d)` under the MySQL dialect rounds and groups a number ([#571](https://github.com/nao1215/filesql/issues/571)). SQLite has a `format()` of its own, an alias of printf, so the untranslated call read the number as a format string with no verbs in it and answered it unchanged: `FORMAT(1234.5678, 2)` came back as `1234.5678` where MySQL answers `1,234.57`, with the second argument dropped and no error to say the request had not been honored. The call now goes to a helper that rounds half away from zero, pads to the number of decimal places asked for, groups the integer part in threes, and reads a negative count as zero, all of which is what mysql:8.4 answers. The locale argument MySQL also accepts is refused with `ErrUnsupportedSyntax` rather than ignored, since ignoring it would answer en_US formatting for a query that asked for something else. GoogleSQL's `FORMAT` and PostgreSQL's `format()` are printf-style and keep their printf behavior.

- `LEFT` and `RIGHT` answer what the source dialect answers for a negative length ([#572](https://github.com/nao1215/filesql/issues/572)). One helper implemented PostgreSQL's rule, which is to drop that many characters from the far end, so `LEFT('abcd', -1)` answered `abc` under every dialect -- where MySQL answers the empty string and BigQuery raises. A negative length is rarely written by hand; it arrives from arithmetic on a column, which is exactly when a nearly-complete string reads as a correct answer. MySQL's calls now clamp to the empty string and GoogleSQL's raise, while PostgreSQL keeps the trimming it documents. A length of zero, a length past the end of the string, and counting in characters rather than bytes are unchanged in all three.

- A fractional or exponent string cast to an integer type stops rounding ([#573](https://github.com/nao1215/filesql/issues/573)). The cast helper fell back to parsing the string as a float when it did not parse as an integer, and rounded the result for every dialect, which is not what any of the three engines does. PostgreSQL raises `invalid input syntax for type integer` for `CAST('1.5' AS integer)` and BigQuery raises for `CAST('1.5' AS INT64)`, with `SAFE_CAST` answering NULL; both now do the same here, and the reported error is `ErrInvalidCast` naming the text. MySQL reads the leading run of an optional sign and digits and stops at the first character that is neither, so `CAST('1.5' AS SIGNED)` is 1 and `CAST('1e3' AS SIGNED)` is 1, where this package answered 2 and 1000. MySQL's coercion in a numeric context does read the whole number -- `'1e3' + 0` is 1000 -- and that rule, which other conversions here use, is unchanged; a cast to an integer type is simply not that context. A well-formed number past the int64 range is still clamped for MySQL and still reported as out of range for the other two.

- `FORMAT('%t', x)` and `FORMAT('%T', x)` under the GoogleSQL dialect print the value ([#574](https://github.com/nao1215/filesql/issues/574)). `FORMAT` was rewritten to SQLite's printf, which covers the verbs the two share but knows neither of BigQuery's own, and printf answers NULL for a format string holding a verb it does not know -- so the whole call came back NULL, and the NULL traveled through whatever concatenated it. The call now goes to a helper that prints `%t` as the value's compact form and `%T` as the literal that would produce it, handing every other verb to the same formatting the printf path performed. A NULL prints as `NULL` for both verbs. A boolean reaches the helper as the integer SQLite stores it as, so it prints as 0 or 1 rather than as `true` or `false`, which is a limit of storing GoogleSQL's types in SQLite rather than of the verb.

### Changed

- ACH and Fedwire stop being described as experimental. The label had outlived what it was saying: both formats have had round-trip tests, control-record recalculation and a documented write-back contract for several releases, and what a caller actually needs to know about them is specific rather than general -- that control records are derived and an edit to one is overwritten, that a write rebuilds the whole file so untouched records can come back formatted differently, that writing needs the source file the tables were loaded from, and now that the columns naming a record's position are not editable and that a Fedwire write is verified before it lands. Those are in README's ACH and Fedwire section, where a caller deciding whether to use them will read them, and "experimental" was standing in for all of it while saying none of it. `doc.go` and `SECURITY.md` follow, the latter replacing "not recommended for untrusted input" with what is actually true of these two formats: both are parsed by libraries that validate as they read, neither is compressed and neither declares a size to allocate against, so a malformed file costs what the file costs; and a Fedwire export is verified while an ACH export cannot be, because ACH control records are recalculated by the write and so cannot be compared against what was read. Nothing about either loader changes here beyond the two fixes above.

- Fedwire files must be the delimiter-separated form, and that is now written down. The reader parses a message's variable-length fields by looking for the `*` that ends each one, so a file written in the fixed-width form is refused, naming every record it could not read; there is no option in the underlying library to read the fixed-width form, and files written by filesql are always the delimiter-separated one. A fixture of the fixed-width form had been sitting in `parser/wire/testdata` since the format was added, referenced by no test and unreadable by this package, so it is removed and the round-trip test over that directory now requires every fixture to parse rather than skipping the ones that do not.

- The Fedwire and ACH write paths gain benchmarks, which they had none of, and the Fedwire write costs more than it did. `BenchmarkWriteToWriter` for a customer transfer is 249 us against 171 us before, allocating 217 KB against 116 KB, which is 1.45 times the time and 1.86 times the memory: the verification reads the staged message back, so a write now costs a write plus a parse. That is the whole point of it and the price is paid once per file rather than once per row -- a quarter of a millisecond for a message -- against a defect that silently replaces one field of a payment instruction with another. The ACH write is 115 us against 108 us, allocating 44.7 KB against 44.1 KB, which is a sixteenth more time for the coordinate check; the parse it is compared against gains 531 bytes and eight allocations for the coordinates it now records, at 17.3 us against 18.6 us, which is within noise.

- The minimum Go version is 1.25.13, and 1.26.6 on the 1.26 line. Those are the patch releases carrying the standard library fixes for [GO-2026-6088](https://pkg.go.dev/vuln/GO-2026-6088), a missing recursion depth guard in `encoding/xml`, and [GO-2026-5972](https://pkg.go.dev/vuln/GO-2026-5972), the same in `encoding/asn1`. The first is not hypothetical here: filesql reads every XLSX through `encoding/xml`, so a workbook is input to the affected decoder on a path any caller loading a spreadsheet takes. The `go` directive moves from 1.25.8 to 1.25.13, the CI matrix pins 1.25.13 and 1.26.6 rather than floating on "1.25" and "1.26" — a floating minor happily tests on a patch release the directive already refuses to vouch for, and 1.26.0 through 1.26.5 are later releases than 1.25.13 while carrying neither fix — and README, CONTRIBUTING and SECURITY.md say the same thing. No dependency changed: `govulncheck` reports nothing reachable in this module beyond those two, and the one module-level advisory it does report, `golang.org/x/crypto`'s unmaintained `openpgp` package, has no fixed version and is not imported.

- `govulncheck` runs in CI, on pull requests and on `main`. It was not run anywhere, which is how a standard library advisory against a decoder this module calls on every XLSX went unnoticed until somebody looked by hand.
