Unlike staticcheck, which is kept off the pull request path because a cold run takes tens of minutes, govulncheck finishes in under a minute here, so it stands in front of a merge rather than reporting after one. The version is pinned to v1.7.0 rather than `@latest`, so a scanner that changes under the project cannot turn an unrelated pull request red for a reason nobody in it can act on, and a run is reproducible from the commit. It runs on both supported Go lines, 1.25.13 and 1.26.6, because what it reports for the standard library is whatever the Go building the module carries: a single-version scan would say nothing about an advisory affecting only the other line, which is a Go this module supports and which some callers will be building it with. The examples are scanned too, in their own step: each is a separate module pinned to a released filesql, so `./...` never reaches them, and they are what the README sends people to copy.

- The release workflow refuses a tag it cannot release properly, and runs the checks before it publishes anything. Three things were wrong with it. A tag was taken at face value, so anything matching `v*` — `vX.Y.Z`, `v1.2`, `v1.2.3.4` — became a release named after it; the tag is now held against the published SemVer grammar and refused if it does not match. A version with no changelog entry was published with the body "No changelog entry found for version v1.2.3", which turns a missing entry into the release notes people read instead of into a failure somebody fixes, and by then the tag exists; that fallback is gone and a missing or empty entry fails the workflow. And nothing was verified before the release was created: a tag can be pushed onto any commit at all, including one that was never in a pull request, so `go vet`, `go test`, the lint and the examples now all run first, in a job the release waits on. Those are two jobs rather than one on purpose: a job-level `contents: write` applies to every step in it, and the verification steps run third-party actions and this module's own tests, so putting them beside the release would put a token that can write to the repository within reach of all of them. Verification keeps the workflow's read-only token; the job that declares `contents: write` does nothing but create the release, and re-runs the tag check itself rather than trusting an output handed to it. Two smaller things go with it: the prerelease flag is now whether the tag carries a SemVer prerelease identifier rather than whether it contains the substrings `-alpha`, `-beta` or `-rc`, which called `v1.0.0-pre.1` a full release — and the full release is the one pkg.go.dev and this project's README point at — and the extraction no longer ends an entry at the next heading of any depth, which had been cutting every release note off at its first `### Added`. The logic is a script, `.github/scripts/release-metadata.sh`, rather than inline YAML, because a release workflow is otherwise only ever exercised by a tag, which is the one moment it must not be wrong; `release_metadata_test.go` runs it against changelogs written for the test.

- The package documentation says what the package does. `doc.go` named CSV, TSV, LTSV, Parquet and XLSX as the formats and left out JSON and JSONL, which are stable and have been in README's format table for releases; it named gzip, bzip2, xz and zstandard as the compression and left out zlib, snappy, s2 and LZ4, which are read exactly as the other four are. Its Column Name Handling section contradicted itself in consecutive sentences, opening with "case-sensitive comparison for duplicate detection" and then saying names identical after trimming are duplicates "regardless of case differences" — and neither statement is the rule. The rule is two comparisons that are deliberately kept apart: ASCII letter case is folded, because SQLite is what ends up holding the columns and that is where its own folding stops, so `ID` and `id` are one column while `ä` and `Ä` are two; and surrounding whitespace is trimmed, which is filesql's own rule, so `name` and `" name "` are one column. Applying them together would refuse `" A"` beside `a`, which neither refuses alone and which SQLite keeps as two columns, so they are never combined. README gains the same paragraph, and a test holds `doc.go`'s claims against what the loader actually does with each of those four headers. `SECURITY.md` is brought up to date in the same pass: it still named 0.17.x as the supported series, it says 0.46.x now, it does not claim v1.0.0 is published, and it states the known limits of feeding this package files from somewhere you do not control — chiefly that a damaged 473-byte Parquet file allocates about 98 MiB before it is refused, which is [#526](https://github.com/nao1215/filesql/issues/526) and is open.

- The prep tag parser reads a table rather than a switch. `parsePrepTag` carried a 130-line switch with one arm per preprocessor, each arm repeating the same shape: read the parameter, decide whether it can be used, append the preprocessor or report `ErrInvalidTagFormat` when strict parsing is on. The validators next door were already a map of builders, so the package had two ways of saying the same thing, and the switch had a third cost: the vocabulary existed only as case labels, which is why the documentation could fall nineteen tags behind without anything noticing. The tags are now a `map[string]func(value string, strict bool) (preprocessor, error)`, with three helpers covering the shapes -- no parameter, a parameter that is the value itself, and the pad tags' length-and-character -- and the vocabulary is the table's keys, which is what the new documentation test reads. No tag changes what it does, and the parse of a tag with no parameters costs one map lookup where it cost a walk down a switch.

## [0.46.0] - 2026-08-25

### Added

- `required_with_all` and `required_without_all` are accepted ([#563](https://github.com/nao1215/filesql/issues/563)). They complete the four-way grid the go-playground dialect defines over a list of fields: `required_with` and `required_without` fire when any named field carries a value or is empty, and the two new tags fire only when every one of them is. A struct that asked for the all-of-them rule had to spell it as a chain of `required_if` tags, which is a different rule -- `required_if` compares a cell against a value, not against emptiness.

- The `parser` package's exported surface is documented and tested where a caller meets it ([#522](https://github.com/nao1215/filesql/issues/522)). `NewCSVReader`, `NormalizeLineEndings`, `NormalizeXLSXDates`, `ExcelSheets` and `SelectExcelSheets` were reached only through the root package's tests of `Open`, so nothing said what they answer for an empty input, a quote that never closes, a workbook with no sheets, or a sheet name the workbook does not have — and the two that give this package its shape were the least pinned of all: `NewCSVReader` exists because `encoding/csv` turns a CRLF inside a quoted field into an LF, and `NormalizeLineEndings` exists because a file written the classic Mac OS way parses as one very long line. All five now have direct tests over those boundaries and a godoc example that runs under `go test`, and README's API example index has a `parser` section, which it had for `filesql`, `prep` and `frame` but not for this one. Statement coverage of the package goes from 96.0% to 98.9%, the remainder being two error paths a test cannot reach without a decompressor whose close fails.

- A nightly job fuzzes each target for ten minutes ([#521](https://github.com/nao1215/filesql/issues/521)). `go test` walks a fuzz target's seed corpus once and stops, so the four targets this module carries had never looked for an input nobody had already written down — which is what a fuzz target is for, and what this module's own history says pays: the comment on `FuzzParseBinary` records that it found a panic in the Parquet path in under three seconds, and the Parquet reader's comment records a worker dying within thirty seconds without the magic-number check in front of it. The workflow runs on a schedule and on demand, never on a pull request, because a fuzz run either finds something or runs out the clock and neither answer is worth holding a merge for, and it uploads whatever `testdata/fuzz/` holds when a run fails, since that input is the finding and a person commits it as a seed beside the fix. A fifth target, `FuzzCodecReader`, covers the decompressors, which is where the two most recent memory bugs were: it holds that reading never panics and that opening a stream allocates no more than 64 MiB before any of it is read, which is the shape both of those bugs had and which a later version of either library could reopen without any existing test noticing. Ten minutes of each target locally found the XLSX blank-row cost recorded above and nothing else, at 20.3 million executions for the CSV reader, 48.9 million for the dialect translation, and 9.0 million for the codecs.

- The dialect's spelling of the letters-and-digits tag is accepted, and its space-tolerant sibling exists ([#543](https://github.com/nao1215/filesql/issues/543)). go-playground/validator calls that validator `alphanum`, and prep registered it as `alphanumeric` alone, so `validate:"alphanum"` was not merely refused a value — `Process` returned `unknown validate tag "alphanum"` and nothing loaded at all. The rename was not consistent inside prep either: the Unicode sibling kept the dialect's spelling as `alphanumunicode`, so a struct copied from a go-playground one had its `alphanumunicode` field accepted and its `alphanum` field refused, and prep's doc.go invites exactly the caller who arrives with tags copied from a go-playground struct. Both spellings now build the same validator, and a reported error names the one the caller wrote, so a caller filtering on `ValidationError.Tag` sees no change. `alphanumspace` is added at the same time, since prep already had `alphaspace` under the dialect's own name and its alphanumeric counterpart was the only member of that four-way grid missing.

### Changed


- Five copies of one implementation come down to one each, with nothing about the behavior changed. The ACH and Fedwire loads were the same forty lines twice: check whether the table already exists folding ASCII case the way SQLite does, drop it when the load is replacing, create it, insert its records, and record the file source, with only the parse in front and the source format at the end differing, so the wording of "table already exists" and the case-folding rule that makes the check correct each existed in two places and a fix to one would have left the other as it was. Both loads now hand their parsed tables to `loadParsedTablesIntoDatabase`. In `dialect`, `topLevelComma` walked the token stream with its own depth counter to find the first comma at depth 1, which is what `topLevelCommas` does before returning all of them; the first is now the first element of the second's answer, so the parenthesis-depth machine that decides which comma belongs to which call exists once. `parser` declared `ExtGZ` through `ExtLZ4` with the same eight literals `internal/codec` declares for the codecs that read and write them, so an extension added or corrected had two homes; the parser constants alias the codec's, their doc comment spells the values out, since the aliased expression is what godoc renders and `internal/codec` is not a package a caller can follow, and a test holds every exported extension against its literal so the aliasing cannot change what a caller sees. In `frame`, `Rename` and `RenameColumns` each rebuilt the column list and then every row's map, and `FillNA` and `FillNAByColumn` each walked every column of every row deciding what a missing cell becomes; those two rebuilds are now `renamed`, which takes the map of old name to new, and `filledNA`, which asks a function per column and leaves the cell alone when it has no answer. The validation stays with each caller, because that is where the pairs genuinely differ, and the degenerate cases where they answer differently are pinned rather than tidied: `Rename` compares its two names before it looks for the column, so `Rename("unknown", "unknown")` is a no-op success, while `RenameColumns({"unknown": "unknown"})` checks every old name first and reports it as not found. One check is dropped as redundant rather than kept, `FillNA`'s own test for a row that has no key for a column: a map read of an absent key is nil, which `isNA` already calls missing, which is how `FillNAByColumn` had always spelled it, and a test now holds that such a cell is filled by both. `DropNA` and `DropNASubset` are the same shape and were already folded, the first calling the second with every column. Three similarities are deliberately left alone: the three `substr` implementations in `dialect/functions.go`, whose tails resemble each other while keeping their dialects' semantics apart is what the file exists for; the parallel `Error` types in `internal/reader` and `internal/writer`; and the validator boilerplate in `prep/validate.go`.

- Thirty-five runnable examples are added, and two godocs stop carrying the same forty-line query. The `dialect` package had none at all, so nothing showed what a MySQL query comes back as, how a construct with no SQLite equivalent is told apart from one SQLite will simply refuse, or how a translator of one's own is installed and taken out again; it now has five, and README's API example index gains the `dialect` section it already had for every other package. In the root package, `ExcelSheetsInReader`, `ExcelSheetTableNames`, `(*DBBuilder).SkippedRows` and `NewReadOnlyDB` gain one each: the reader-shaped half of the workbook helpers, the check that refuses a workbook whose sheets would share a table, the counts a skip policy reports, and wrapping a database the caller already owns. So do the four ACH and Fedwire export entry points, whose shape a caller has to get right and which README described only in prose: `DumpACH` and `DumpFedWire` rebuild the file from the source it was loaded from, and a database loaded from an `io.Reader` has no such source, which is what `DumpACHWithTableSet` and `DumpFedWireWithTableSet` take the structure for. Those two examples show the refusal first, so the error a caller meets and the way past it sit next to each other, and each edits one field and reads it back out of the file it wrote, printing only that field because the surrounding bytes are rewritten by the format. The five remaining `String` methods follow `MalformedRowPolicy`, which was the only enum with an example. `parser` gains `NewTSVReader`'s, the reader whose behavior surprises, since it exists because TSV has no quoting and a value as plain as 5'9" tall fails a CSV reader outright. `parser/ach` and `parser/wire` gain two each, since what a file becomes was visible only from the root package's tables: an ACH file is several tables, one per record kind, and a Fedwire message is one row of one table 326 columns wide. `frame` gains twelve, which completes every pair where one half had an example and the other did not, `Head` without `Tail`, `DistinctBy` without `Distinct`, `RenameColumns` without `Rename`, `FillNAByColumn` without `FillNA`, and `Drop` and `Rename` show the error they report as well, since both refuse a column name rather than skipping it. Every example runs under `go test` with its output checked, and README's index has a row for each. Two godocs shrink while this is being done: `Open` and `OpenContext` each carried the same window-function query with its error handling and its scan loop, which is a page of documentation that says nothing about either function and said it twice, while `ExampleOpen` and `ExampleOpenContext` already showed a call that runs. `Open` keeps what only its own godoc says, what a path may be, how a file becomes a table name, and that the files are not modified, and `OpenContext` says it is `Open` with a context and shows the four lines that make one. One test gap closes alongside: `prep`'s `getStructType` returns `ErrStructSlicePointer` from six places and its table asserted only that an error came back, so each of the five refused shapes now has to wrap the sentinel.

- The Parquet and XLSX writers move out of `filesql.go`, and a local variable stops shadowing the `writer` package. `filesql.go` was 1,220 lines holding five things: the four exported entry points — `Open`, `OpenContext`, `LoadInto` and `DumpDatabase` — the dump machinery they share, which decides what path a table name becomes, which columns are read as text, what the compressed writer is and how the write is staged, and three writers, one per output shape. The two that write a typed container format move to `dump_parquet.go` and `dump_xlsx.go`, 281 and 270 lines, leaving `filesql.go` at 687: the entry points, the shared machinery, and the 125 lines that write the three text formats, which stay because picking between them is what the dump machinery does. Not a line of the moved code changed — concatenating the three files' bodies back together reproduces the original 43,799 bytes exactly, and the only difference is that each file now names the imports it uses — and nothing is visible from outside, `go doc -all` being identical for every package. The move also takes away one of the two local variables that had been shadowing `internal/writer` inside the only file that imports it; the other, the compressed writer in `writeSQLiteTableDataTo`, is renamed, so the file that spells the package `writer` in five places no longer spells a local variable that way as well. Two things this is not. XLSX writing is still spread over two files, because `save.go` holds the compressed-workbook path an in-place save goes through, and moving that would be a change to the write-back machinery rather than a move of it. And `filesql.go` was not the only file in this package holding more than one concern: `stream_processor.go` at 978 lines and `save.go` at 853 are both larger than what is left here, and both are still owed the same treatment.

- The three write paths share one encoder. The root package wrote a dump's rows, prep wrote a processed file's rows and frame wrote a data frame's rows, and each carried its own copy of the same encoding: CSV existed three times, LTSV twice, and JSONL once. Only TSV was already shared, through `parser.WriteTSVRecord`. The copies had drifted in the way copies do, in the rules that are not evident from the code performing them. A CSV record of one empty field is a blank line written plainly, and a blank line is not a CSV record — a reader skips it — so a one-column table's empty rows disappeared on reload and the write reported success; the root dump wrote `""` for that shape and frame wrote it too, and prep did not, which is a row silently lost from a one-column file of free text, the kind of file prep is most likely to be handed. LTSV separates fields with a tab and records with a newline and defines no escape for either, so a value holding one produces a file that parses as something else; the root dump refused such a value and named the column, and prep wrote it. That one looked unreachable — prep writes LTSV only for LTSV input, and a tab cannot be in an LTSV value read from a file — but a preprocessor puts it there: a struct tag's value is unquoted by reflect.StructTag.Get, so `prep:"prefix=A\tB"` carries a real tab, and the tag parser trims whitespace only at the ends of a tag part. A row `note:v` preprocessed that way was written as `note:A\tBv`, which reads back as `A` — the rest became a second field with no label, which the reader drops without a word. Both rules now live in `internal/writer`, which is the one implementation of CSV, TSV, LTSV and JSONL: a caller hands it the header, then each record, then calls Flush. `parser.WriteTSVRecord` and its line-ending sibling were left in place, delegating there rather than keeping a copy; delegating turned out to be all they did, and they are removed under Breaking Changes below. Where the records come from is not shared, because it differs: the root package reads them from `*sql.Rows`, prep from a `[][]string` and frame from a map per row. Nor are the sentinels — the writer returns an `*Error` carrying a `Kind`, and a caller that has one puts it in front, since the same refused value is `ErrUnsupportedFormat` to a dump and `parser.ErrTSVUnrepresentable` to a frame; prep has none for this and passes the writer's error up as it stands. Three things about the output change. A one-column empty record written by prep — a row, or a header whose only column is named with the empty string — is now `""` rather than a blank line, and a value prep cannot hold in LTSV is now reported rather than written; those are the two bugs above. And a dump refusing a value it cannot write reports `ErrUnsupportedFormat` for TSV as it already did for LTSV, where TSV had carried only `parser.ErrTSVUnrepresentable`, so the two formats answered the same fault in two ways. That sentinel is still on a TSV failure and its wording is still in the message, because a caller may have been matching it since before this package had one of its own, so `errors.Is` answers as it did and now answers for `ErrUnsupportedFormat` too. The advice at the end of the message is corrected while it is being touched: it names CSV in every case, where LTSV used to answer "CSV or TSV". That was wrong for all three of the characters LTSV forbids in a value — a tab, a line feed and a carriage return — because TSV forbids the same three, having no quoting to hold them in. CSV does, which is why it is the answer. Everything else is byte-identical, including the carriage-return and CRLF terminators, which are still staged one record at a time because `encoding/csv` offers only `UseCRLF` and that rewrites line feeds inside quoted fields as well as between records. The consolidation is faster, because three things that were true in one place or none are now true everywhere. The destination is buffered: prep's and frame's CSV had that from `encoding/csv`, and no path that wrote TSV, LTSV or JSONL had it at all, so those wrote a record at a time to whatever they were given — for frame, a write per row to an unbuffered file. One record slice is reused across rows, which only frame's TSV path did; the dump and frame's CSV allocated one per row. And an LTSV line is built in a byte slice, which nothing did: the dump joined a fresh slice of fields per row, and prep used a `strings.Builder` whose `Reset` sets its buffer to nil, so reusing one reallocates per record anyway. `BenchmarkDumpText`, added here, goes from 270 ms and 92.6 MB/op to 216 ms and 37.5 MB/op for CSV, from 277 ms and 92.4 MB/op to 246 ms and 73.2 MB/op for TSV, and from 303 ms and 176.9 MB/op to 215 ms and 37.5 MB/op for LTSV. `BenchmarkToCSV`, also added here, goes from 2.94 ms and 1105 KB/op to 2.56 ms and 305 KB/op at ten thousand rows, and `BenchmarkToTSV` from 5.96 ms to 3.30 ms, that one being the write-per-row a frame made to an unbuffered file, for 4.3 KB more per operation and three more allocations, which is the buffer that bought it. `BenchmarkOpenWithAutoSave` goes from 574 ms and 257 MB/op to 548 ms and 202 MB/op, and `BenchmarkDumpParquet`, whose path is not touched, is unchanged. One message changes on a path nothing reaches: a JSON value prep cannot compact is now reported as "the value is not JSON" rather than "failed to compact JSON at output", which `ErrInvalidJSONAfterPrep` gets to first in every case. One measurement goes the other way: `BenchmarkCSVOutput` writes 1,000 records through prep and its allocation count per call goes from 3 to 6, which is the writer and its buffer allocated once per file rather than per record, with bytes per operation up 0.1% and time unchanged.

- `prep` maps a parse failure onto its sentinel by matching an error rather than a message. `wrapParseError` compared the text `parser.Parse` returned: two exact matches, a five-entry table of empty-input wordings, and two prefix-and-suffix patterns. Its own comment already said the right fix was for the parser to export sentinels and for this to use `errors.Is`, and the cost of not having done so was on the floor in two forms. The first is that the table did not list every wording the read side has for input holding no table. `empty XLSX file` was never on it, so a workbook with nothing in it reached the caller with no `ErrEmptyFile` on it while a workbook whose sheet was empty, three lines away in the same reader, was recognized; and `no visible sheets found in XLSX file`, which the reader gained after the table was written, is not on it either, so it would go unrecognized the day a caller reaches it — which prep cannot today, since it never asks for the visible sheets alone. The second is that one of the patterns matched nothing at all: nothing in the module says `empty ... array` any more, since a JSON document holding `[]` parses to a table with no rows rather than to an error, so that branch had been dead for as long as it took nobody to notice, and the test covering it passed the whole time because it handed `wrapParseError` a message it had written itself. The parser now exports `ErrNilReader`, `ErrUnsupportedFileType` and `ErrEmptyData`, and maps the reader's `KindEmpty` onto the last of them at its own boundary, where it already mapped `KindDuplicateColumn`. No message changes: two of the three sentinels are what `Parse` returns for their condition, so the message a caller reads is the sentinel's own, and the third is attached by a wrapper whose `Error` returns what the read side wrote and whose `Unwrap` returns the sentinel and the cause together, so `errors.Is` reaches both while `empty parquet file` still reads as `empty parquet file` instead of gaining a prefix that says the same thing twice. `wrapParseError` is three `errors.Is` arms, and its test now produces the errors it maps by calling the parser rather than by writing the messages out, so it fails when the mapping stops reaching a real failure rather than when a message is reworded.

- The six readers share one chunk accumulator. Every format read its rows its own way and then did the same things with them, in six copies across five files: flush when the chunk is full, flush what is left at the end, and, in the three formats that infer their types, fold each record into the column evidence first. The final flush carries a rule that is not evident from the code performing it, that a read which emitted nothing still emits one empty chunk, so a file that is nothing but a header, or whose records a `MalformedRowPolicy` all skipped, makes a table with the columns the header names rather than no table at all. Five of the six copies said so in a comment, each in its own words, and the LTSV one did not. The rule and the two evidence helpers now sit in one file around one type, built with `newChunker` by a format that infers its types and with `newTypedChunker` by one that states them; the Parquet reader, which was the copy furthest from the others, adds its rows through `addWithNulls` so the null mask it carries travels with them, and asks for the same room per chunk it always did. Nothing about what is emitted changes: a chunk still carries the types every row up to and including it requires, so the last chunk's are final, and the records are still not reused after a flush, since the callback may keep them. There is one difference, and it is the inconsistency the copies had: a Parquet file whose footer counts rows that its row groups then do not produce emitted no chunk at all, where a file whose footer counts none emitted an empty one two lines above, and both now emit the empty chunk. `BenchmarkOpenContext`, `BenchmarkOpenParquet`, `BenchmarkDumpParquet` and the three codecs of `BenchmarkOpenCompressed` are unchanged, with allocation counts inside the spread the runs already show between themselves.

- The three dialect call passes walk the token stream through one implementation. `mysqlCallPass`, `pgCallPass` and `googlesqlCallPass` were the same twenty-six lines with one word changed: find a word followed by an open parenthesis, match the parenthesis, hand the call to the dialect's rewriter, and keep whatever it does not recognize. The walk is not what distinguishes the dialects — the rules are — and keeping three copies of it meant the error for an unbalanced parenthesis after a function name existed three times, where a fix to one would have left the other two as they were. Each pass is now one line over a shared `walkCalls` taking the rewriter as its argument. No rewrite rule moves and no translation changes; `FuzzTranslate` ran 14.1 million executions clean over the change.

### Fixed

- The `datauri` validator accepts a data URI that carries a media type parameter ([#539](https://github.com/nao1215/filesql/issues/539)). Its pattern was `^data:[^;]+;base64,...`, which demands a non-empty media type and cannot cross a semicolon, so the literal `;base64,` had to follow the media type immediately: `data:text/plain;charset=utf-8;base64,aGVsbG8=` and `data:image/svg+xml;charset=UTF-8;base64,PHN2Zz48L3N2Zz4=` were both refused, and so was `data:;base64,aGVsbG8=`, which RFC 2397 defines as the form that omits the media type and means `text/plain;charset=US-ASCII`. A `charset` parameter is what browsers and SVG toolchains emit, so a column of real data URIs failed validation wholesale, and under `WithValidRowsOnly` those rows never reached filesql. The media type is now optional and may carry any number of `;name=value` parameters, while the payload is still decoded rather than merely matched, so `data:text/plain,hello` stays refused for not being base64 and a payload that does not decode stays refused for that.

- The `email` validator accepts an internationalized address and the rest of the RFC 5322 local part ([#540](https://github.com/nao1215/filesql/issues/540)). Its pattern was ASCII on both sides of the `@` and required at least two letters in the last label, so `user@日本.jp` and `山田@example.com` were invalid, `o'brien@example.com` was invalid, and `a@b.c` was invalid — while `user@xn--wgv71a.jp`, the punycode spelling of the same domain as the first, was accepted, so which of two equivalent spellings the exporting tool chose decided whether the address was data or an error. That matters most in the files this package is aimed at: an internationalized address is what turns up in a Japanese CSV. The local part now admits the atext set the dialect admits and the quoted-string form, both sides admit the dialect's Unicode ranges, and the last label may be one character but must still start with a letter, so a numeric top-level domain is still not an address. The ranges stop at the BMP, as the dialect's do, so a character above U+FFFF is still not a letter, while one below it is. The wider pattern costs what a wider pattern costs: `BenchmarkEmailValidation` went from 1344 ns/op to 1462 ns/op with allocations unchanged, and `BenchmarkProcessCSV_Medium`, where an address is one field among many, did not move.

- The `fqdn` validator accepts the trailing dot that makes a name fully qualified ([#541](https://github.com/nao1215/filesql/issues/541)). The check treated the two ends of a name alike and refused either, although only a leading dot is malformed: `example.com.` was invalid while `example.com` passed, which is backwards for a tag named after the absolute form. The trailing dot anchors a name at the DNS root and is what zone files, `dig` output and resolver logs write, so a column taken from any of them had every row rejected. One trailing dot is now stripped before the labels are walked, so the two spellings of a name always agree; `example.com..` still fails, because the strip leaves an empty label behind, and the 253-byte limit is measured on the name without the root dot, which is the form the limit is usually quoted in.

- The `e164` validator accepts a number whose leading plus a spreadsheet stripped ([#542](https://github.com/nao1215/filesql/issues/542)). The plus was required, so `819012345678` was invalid where `+819012345678` passed, and the plus is precisely what does not survive a trip through a spreadsheet: Excel and Google Sheets read a leading `+` as the start of a formula and drop it on export. The tag therefore refused the shape a CSV of phone numbers actually arrives in, which is the opposite of what a package that exists to take files as they are found should do, and the dialect makes the plus optional in any case. It is optional here now. The rest is unchanged: a number starting with zero, one shorter than eight digits and one longer than fifteen all stay refused.

- README says what an untrusted Parquet file can cost ([#526](https://github.com/nao1215/filesql/issues/526)). A Parquet page header sits inside a column chunk and is decoded as the rows are read, and it states the size of the page's statistics, so a damaged 473-byte file allocates 98 MiB and then fails with `unexpected EOF`. The failure is fast, under a millisecond warm, so this is not the unbounded decode the Parquet reader was changed to avoid; what is out of proportion is the price. filesql cannot check that number before the allocation happens — `encoding/thrift` reads the declared length and allocates it before consulting the reader, and `parquet.OpenFile` takes no option that bounds it — so what changes is that a caller reading files they did not write is told to keep them away from a memory-constrained process.

- A load never asks the library about one cell at a time, so a workbook costs what its rows cost ([#531](https://github.com/nao1215/filesql/issues/531)). Loading an 18.5 MB workbook of 200,000 rows cost 1849 MB of peak resident memory, against 101 MB for the same rows as a CSV, and reading the rows was not what cost it: opening the workbook is 24 MB and the streaming row read is 267 MB, while asking about one cell's style is 1470 MB, because that is when excelize builds the whole sheet as objects. `NormalizeXLSXDates` asked once per non-empty cell — 2.4 million times for that sheet — so the first question bought the entire cost. Two things answer it now. A cell is a date because of its style, so a workbook whose style table holds no date format has no date cells at all, and the style table is a part of the file on its own that costs nothing measurable to read whole. When it does hold one, the date cells are found by walking the sheet's own XML — located through the workbook's relationships rather than guessed at, since a workbook whose sheets were reordered or deleted does not name its parts in order — which costs what the date cells cost and nothing for the rest. The same load is now 422 MB with dates and without them alike, where it was 1849 MB and 1939 MB, and `BenchmarkOverwriteWorkbook` went from 553 ms and 302 MB/op to 208 ms and 180 MB/op. Nothing about which cells become dates changed: the built-in and custom date formats, the color brackets that are not elapsed units, the 1904 epoch and the serials below 61 all answer as they did, and the exported `parser.NormalizeXLSXDates`, which is handed an open workbook and no bytes, keeps asking cell by cell because that is all it can do.

- A Parquet file named by path is read where it lies instead of being copied first ([#532](https://github.com/nao1215/filesql/issues/532)). The reader buffered the whole file with `io.ReadAll` before opening it, even when the input was a file the operating system was already holding pages of, so a load carried a second copy of the compressed bytes for its whole length: 200,000 rows cost 177 MB of peak resident memory as a 32.2 MB Parquet file against 101 MB as a 32.8 MB CSV, and one file's worth of the difference was that copy. `parquet.OpenFile` takes an `io.ReaderAt` and a size, which an open file already is, so a source that can serve a read at an offset and has not been read from is now used as it stands, and the magic and footer-length checks in front read their four and eight bytes through the same interface. The reader is no longer wrapped in a buffer on the way down either, which is what had been hiding that it was a file; a text parser still gets its buffer, since it reads in small pieces. The same load is now 111 MB, and costs 2.1 extra bytes per extra file byte, which is what CSV costs. A reader passed to `AddReader` is still buffered whole, because a stream cannot go back. `BenchmarkOpenParquet` went from 321/318/323 ms and 211 MB/op to 319/305/309 ms and 177 MB/op.

- A `.z` file holding more than one zlib stream reads as all of them ([#527](https://github.com/nao1215/filesql/issues/527)). Only the first was read, and silently: ten streams of the same sixteen bytes were 280 bytes in and sixteen bytes out with a nil error, so nine tenths of a file made with `cat a.z b.z` went missing with nothing to say so. Every other codec here already read the whole of such a file — gzip's members are part of that format, and xz's streams are read one after another — so zlib was the one that did not. The source is buffered through a `*bufio.Reader`, which is an `io.ByteReader` and so keeps the flate decoder from reading past the end of a stream, and each stream's end is followed by `Reset` onto the next; `Reset` reads and checks the two header bytes, so bytes behind the last stream that are not a stream now come back as an error rather than being dropped. A test over all seven codecs holds both halves of that answer in one place.

- README's memory figure is given per format, because one number was wrong for two of them ([#531](https://github.com/nao1215/filesql/issues/531), [#532](https://github.com/nao1215/filesql/issues/532)). "About twice the file's size" was measured on CSV and stated for everything, and the same 200,000 rows cost 100 MB as a 32.8 MB CSV, 177 MB as a 32.2 MB Parquet file, and 1849 MB as a 17.6 MB workbook — fifty times what a caller was told to budget, which is the difference between a load that fits and a machine that does not. `TestLoadMemoryFootprintByFormat` measures each format in a process of its own and reports peak resident memory, because several loads in one process cannot be told apart: the allocator does not give pages back, so the second load reads as free and the third as negative. Breaking the workbook read into stages showed where its cost is, and it is not the reading: opening is 24 MB, the streaming row read is 267 MB, and one random-access call into the sheet is 1619 MB, because it makes the library underneath unmarshal the whole worksheet. `NormalizeXLSXDates` makes one such call per non-empty cell, so the first buys the entire cost. Both that and the Parquet reader's whole-file buffer were filed from this measurement and are fixed in the entries above; README carries the figures each of them left behind.

- Loading into one database from several goroutines works instead of failing ([#529](https://github.com/nao1215/filesql/issues/529)). Creating a table takes a write lock and SQLite refuses a second holder rather than queueing it, so a load that met another load's lock came back at once with the database's own message and its table not created: sixteen concurrent `LoadInto` calls against a file database left the database empty and sixteen errors behind, and eleven of sixteen failed against the database `Open` returns. The message was not filesql's either, and there were two of them — `database is locked (5) (SQLITE_BUSY)` from a file database and `database schema is locked: main (262)` from a shared-cache one — while README named only the second and told the caller to hold their own lock around every load. A load now waits the lock out and starts over, with a doubling wait and jitter, for up to five seconds before it gives the database's error back; the wait is decided by the driver's result code rather than by the message, so both spellings are covered and nothing else is. All sixteen loads land, on a pinned `:memory:` pool, a file database, a shared-cache one, and the database this package opens. A reader passed to `AddReader` is the one input that cannot be started over, since its bytes are gone once read, so only the steps before the reading are tried again for it; README says so. An error that is not a lock still comes back at once, which a test pins, so a bad input does not become a slow bad input. `BenchmarkOpenContext` is unchanged.

- Every xz stream in a file is held to the dictionary limit, not only the first ([#511](https://github.com/nao1215/filesql/issues/511)). An xz file may hold several streams one after another — what `cat a.xz b.xz` produces, and what a rotated log archive looks like — and the decoder opened the later ones itself, past anything this package had looked at, so the check made in front of the first stream said nothing about the second: an ordinary xz with a 28-byte stream declaring a 4 GiB dictionary appended to it, 100 bytes in all, decoded to seventeen bytes and allocated 4104 MiB on the way, with a nil error where the refusal should have been. The streams are now opened one at a time, each checked before its decoder is built, and that same file is refused for 8 MiB with the error naming both numbers. Splitting them rests on the decoder reading a single stream on request: asked for one, it reads past the end to see whether anything follows, and the bytes it took are handed back and taken as the next stream only when they really are a stream header, so a decoder that read past the end for its own reasons still surfaces its own error. Files that were already read correctly are unchanged — two genuine streams still read as one file, with or without the padding the format allows between them, a file that ends in that padding still ends there, and a truncated stream is still an error rather than a short read — and bytes behind the last stream that are not a stream are now reported as such rather than as an invalid header. What remains outside the check is a later block within a stream, whose header is not reachable without decoding the blocks before it; closing that still needs a decoder that takes a dictionary ceiling, which remains the condition for removing this check. Loading a 100,000-row xz measured 733 ms against 716 ms on main, with allocations unchanged.

- A Parquet file costs no more than its own size, whatever its footer declares ([#524](https://github.com/nao1215/filesql/issues/524)). The four bytes before a Parquet file's trailing magic state the length of its footer, and `parquet.OpenFile` allocates that length before checking the file is that big, so what a caller paid was set by a number the file asserted: eight bytes reading `PAR1PAR1`, whose second magic reads as a little-endian 826364240, allocated 789 MiB and then failed with `bytes.Reader.ReadAt: negative offset`. The error was always right; its price was not, and the reader's own comment says this library was chosen because it bounds time and memory by the file's own size. `TestReadParquetRefusesWhatIsNotParquet` had been feeding it exactly those eight bytes and asserting only that an error came back, which is how the cost went unseen. The declared length is now read before the library is handed anything and refused when the file cannot hold it, with an error naming both numbers, the way the xz and zstd checks do; the same eight bytes now cost nothing, a footer that fits is unaffected, and the new test asserts on allocation rather than on the error.

- A sheet's blank rows are not records, so a workbook costs what it holds ([#519](https://github.com/nao1215/filesql/issues/519)). `GetRows` returns a row for every row of a sheet's used range, and the reader padded each of them to the header's width, so the range between the last row a sheet was written to and a stray cell further down became rows of the table: an XLSX with a header of 200 columns in row 1 and one cell in row 1048576 — the last row Excel has, and one a user leaves behind by pressing Ctrl+End and typing — is 7,417 bytes, and loading it took 7.84 GB of peak resident memory and 88 seconds to produce 1,048,575 rows of which 1,048,574 held nothing. Nothing reported a problem, and README tells the caller to budget about twice the file's size. The cost was this package's rather than the library's: `GetRows` on that file allocates 24 MiB and hands back 1,048,574 rows with no cells at all, and the padding is what turned each into 200 empty strings. A row holding no cell is now skipped, which is what a blank line already means to every other reader here — `encoding/csv` skips one, and so do the LTSV and JSONL readers — and the same file loads one row. A row that reaches any column stays a record, so a row whose first cells are empty and whose second column holds a value still loads as an empty field beside that value. The property is pinned by allocation rather than by row count, since a fix that built the rows and then dropped them would pass a count: reading a 6 KiB workbook with a cell 200,000 rows down allocated 119 MiB before and 20 MiB after.

- The examples build and run against the library they document ([#514](https://github.com/nao1215/filesql/issues/514)). Each directory under `examples/` is its own module, all eight of them required filesql v0.12.0 — thirty-three minor versions back — and no workflow or `Makefile` target named `examples` existed: the integrations the README's table sends readers to were last compiled against an API from before most of what this file records, and the rule this repository shrinks public symbols by — check `examples/` and sqly for uses first — was reading a thirty-three-version-old snapshot, so it could not report anything. The pins are current, and `examples/verify.sh`, run by a workflow with one job per example and by `make examples`, points each module at the checkout through a temporary `go.work` instead of editing its `go.mod`, so the checked-in file still shows a reader the released version they would import while a rename or an unexport fails the pull request that makes it. Unexporting `OpenContext` was tried against this and the basic example failed to vet, which is the property the job exists for.

- Two example READMEs document what the example prints ([#515](https://github.com/nao1215/filesql/issues/515)). The `Expected Output` blocks were written by hand and never compared with a run, so `multi-format` gave the Keyboard row's JOIN total as 239.97 where the REAL column's arithmetic gives 239.96999999999997, and `squirrel` ordered the `age >= 30` rows Bob before Diana against its own `OrderBy("age")` and left Alice out of a `LIKE '%a%'` that matches her, since SQLite's LIKE is case-insensitive for ASCII. Both blocks taught something about the library that is not true — that a CSV number column stays exact through arithmetic, and that LIKE here is case-sensitive — and both now hold the real output, which `examples/verify.sh` diffs on every pull request, making the README the golden file rather than a separate fixture that can drift from it. The multi-format example's format list and the README's row for it now name the three formats it opens instead of the ones the library supports.

- The race detector runs on every pull request ([#517](https://github.com/nao1215/filesql/issues/517)). README's Concurrency section promises that the `*sql.DB` from `Open` is safe to share across goroutines, that `EnableAutoSave` saves once when `Close` returns, and that `EnableAutoSaveOnCommit` serializes its saves, and `concurrency_test.go` was written to hold those promises to account — five of its comments say to run it with `-race` — but no workflow ever passed the flag, so the checks ran with the instrument they name switched off. Both bugs those tests came from were invisible without it: pooled connections wrapping one shared `driver.Conn`, and two commits driving statements into one SQLite connection at once. A `race_test` job now runs `go test -race ./...`, on one operating system and one Go version because the detector watches Go code that does not vary by platform and the module takes about 25 seconds under it. The suite is clean as it stands, and two promises it did not reach are now covered: writing from several goroutines to a shared database, since README promises the database rather than a read-only view of it, and calling `Close` from several goroutines at once, which is where the auto-save connector's rule that a save runs once is decided.

- An xz stream costs no more working memory than this package agrees to hold for it ([#511](https://github.com/nao1215/filesql/issues/511)). An xz file states the size of the dictionary its decoder must allocate in one byte of the LZMA2 filter's properties, and `github.com/ulikunitz/xz` allocates exactly what the byte asks for before reading any compressed data, so what a caller paid was set by a number the file asserted rather than by anything it contained: a 28-byte stream whose property byte says 40 — the format's maximum, a 4 GiB dictionary — reserved 4 GiB and then failed with `unexpected EOF`. Once is mostly virtual; repeating it is not, because the runtime reuses and zeroes the spans, so twenty `Open` calls on that same 28-byte file took peak resident memory from 17 MiB to 8224 MiB and 2.44 seconds, which is the shape a long-running process such as a shell has. The bound is filesql's own because the library offers none to pass: `xz.ReaderConfig.DictCap` is a floor rather than a limit — the code raises it to whatever the file declares — and the same 28-byte stream through `DictCap: 1 MiB` still allocated 4096 MiB. The stream header and the first block header are now read before the decoder is built, the LZMA2 property byte is decoded, and a dictionary past 256 MiB is refused with an error naming both numbers; the same twenty opens now cost 19 MiB and 2.9 ms. The limit clears what real files declare — the CLI's default `-6` declares 8 MiB, `-9` declares 64 MiB, and this package's own writer declares 8 MiB — and files from all three still load. What is covered is the first block of every stream, which is where a damaged file and a plain hostile one declare their size; the streams behind the first are reached because they are now opened one at a time. A later block within a stream is not covered, because its header is not reachable without decoding the blocks before it. So the bound is a fixed ceiling on the common shape rather than the Parquet reader's rule that a file costs no more than its own size; closing the rest needs a decoder that takes a dictionary ceiling, which is also the condition for removing this check. Reading an xz file also got faster, by a wider margin than the check costs: the decoder now reads through the buffer the check leaves in front of it rather than straight from the file, and loading a 100,000-row xz went from 1836 ms to 729 ms with allocations unchanged.

- A zstd frame costs no more working memory than this package agrees to hold for it ([#512](https://github.com/nao1215/filesql/issues/512)). A zstd frame states its window size in one byte of the frame header and `github.com/klauspost/compress/zstd` allocates it up front, and filesql passed no decoder options, so it took the library's default ceiling of 512 MiB: a 14-byte `.zst` file asking for a 512 MiB window cost a 513 MiB allocation — and, unlike the xz case, nothing was damaged: it loaded, `SELECT` returned its one row, and twenty opens took peak resident memory to 1050 MiB and 397 ms, against 30 MiB and 17 ms for the same file declaring a 1 MiB window. The window is now capped at 128 MiB through `zstd.WithDecoderMaxWindow`, which is the enforcement because it holds for every frame in a file rather than only the first, and the first frame's header is read beforehand so the case a caller actually meets answers with the file's number and this package's limit instead of a bare `window size exceeded` from the library. The limit sits exactly on the largest window the CLI reaches on its own: 2 MiB at `-3`, 8 MiB at `-19`, and 128 MiB at `--ultra -22` and `--long=27`, which passes only because the comparison is strict, so what it refuses is a deliberately raised `--long=28` or above. The descriptor appears at all only when zstd does not know the content size, which is what a pipe gives it; a file whose size it knows becomes a single-segment frame carrying no window descriptor, and the ceiling bounds that through the content size the frame declares. `WithDecoderConcurrency` was measured at 1, 4 and 8 and left the 513 MiB untouched, and `WithDecoderLowmem` is already true by default, with false doubling the allocation to 1024 MiB, so neither is a substitute for the ceiling.

- A directory load reads every supported file in it, whatever the files are named ([#503](https://github.com/nao1215/filesql/issues/503)). The directory walk carried a filter written for this repository's own fixtures — any file whose base name contained `duplicate_columns` was skipped — and it shipped, so an ordinary CSV named `duplicate_columns.csv` or `report_duplicate_columns_2026.csv` vanished from a directory load with no table, no error and no log at all to say it had happened. The same file named directly loaded fine, and so did the same file reached through `AddFS`, whose collector never had the filter, so which of three routes carried the data decided whether the caller saw it. A file name is the caller's, not this package's to read meaning out of: the filter is gone, and nothing else in the walk looks at a name except to find the format and the compression codec.

- A symbolic link to a directory loads what the directory holds ([#505](https://github.com/nao1215/filesql/issues/505)). The two halves of the same decision disagreed about links: `os.Stat` follows one and reported the input as a directory, and `filepath.WalkDir` lstats its root and saw the link itself, so the walk visited one entry that was not a supported file and the load failed with "no supported files found in directory" for a directory full of them. A link standing in for a data directory is ordinary — a `current` link beside dated snapshots, a home-directory link to a mounted volume — and a link to a file was already followed, so the caller's tree decided whether the input worked at all. The root the caller names is now resolved before the walk, and what is found under it is reported back under the name the caller gave, which is the path an in-place save writes to and the one an error message names. Links found inside a walk are still not followed, which is what keeps a link cycle from running forever; a link to one of its own ancestors named as the input terminates, because resolving it lands on a real directory.

- A save works on a destination whose name is legal but close to the filesystem's length limit ([#504](https://github.com/nao1215/filesql/issues/504)). Every write goes through a staged file named after the destination, with a dot in front and `.tmp` plus up to ten random digits behind, so the staged name was up to fifteen bytes longer than a name the filesystem had already accepted: a CSV with a 250-byte name loaded and queried, and its dump failed with `file name too long`, naming a path the caller had never chosen. Under auto-save that failure comes out of `db.Close()`, after the edit is in the database and with nowhere left for it to go. No portable constant gives the limit, so the descriptive name is tried first and a short fixed one takes over when the filesystem refuses it, which needs no length calculation. The backup the copy fallback takes is created the same way, since it had the same shape and would have failed the same way.

- A staged file that cannot be removed is reported ([#506](https://github.com/nao1215/filesql/issues/506)). `writeFileAtomically`'s deferred cleanup joined a failed removal onto the operation's error with `joinCleanup`, which is what `ErrCleanup` exists for, but the function's result was unnamed: the assignment inside the closure wrote to a local that nothing read afterwards, so a save that left a hidden `.tmp` file beside the caller's data returned no sign of it and `errors.Is(err, ErrCleanup)` was false where the package documents it should be true. This is the one site that silently opted out of a contract the rollback and statement-close sites already keep. Naming the result is the whole fix; a save that succeeds still returns nil, because the staged file is gone by then and its absence is not a failure.

- `commitStagedFile` documents the fallback it takes ([#507](https://github.com/nao1215/filesql/issues/507)). Its comment still described the strategy the copy fallback replaced in v0.24.0 — renaming the destination out of the way and putting it back on failure — and claimed the destination is "never left missing or half-written", while `commitByCopy` in the same file says of the same code path that a reader watching during the copy can see a partial file. Anyone reasoning about the durability of an in-place save on Windows, which is the only platform that reaches the fallback on its own and the question the comment exists to answer, was told the wrong thing by the comment they met first. The behavior is unchanged and is the one deliberately chosen when the copy replaced the rename: the replacement is not atomic, and the destination's bytes are copied aside first so a failure partway can be undone — best effort, since putting the backup back is itself a copy.

- A string with a second point in it keeps the number in front of it ([#549](https://github.com/nao1215/filesql/issues/549)). `numericPrefix`, which is how a string reaches a number where the dialect coerces instead of raising, took every character that could belong to a number and parsed the lot once at the end. `1.2.3` therefore arrived at the parser whole, no parser accepts it, and the failure was answered with 0 — the answer for a string with no number in it at all — so `CAST('1.2.3' AS SIGNED)` was 0 where MySQL says 1 and a version-like column compared as if every row were zero, silently and without an error to show for it. The same greedy scan stopped at the `e` of `1e5` and answered 1 for a number MySQL reads whole as 100000. The run is now measured as it is scanned, following the grammar a number is written in: an optional sign, digits around at most one point, and an exponent only when a digit follows it. So a broken tail ends the number rather than voiding it — `1.2.3` is 1.2, `10.5.2` is 10.5, `1.` is 1, `1e` and `1e+` are both 1 — and `..`, `--3` and `-` are still nothing, since none of them has a digit anywhere a number could start. A run past the range of a float64 answers the bound of the type, as MySQL does, rather than 0. Reported by [@krishna3554](https://github.com/krishna3554). The integer cast of such a string still rounds the number it finds where MySQL truncates at the point, which is this package's existing shape for every string-to-integer cast and unchanged here.

- Auto-save keeps a workbook's sheets apart from those of a source whose name it prefixes ([#550](https://github.com/nao1215/filesql/issues/550)). A workbook is written back sheet by sheet, and the sheets belonging to it were found by name: a table called after the file, or starting with the file's name and an underscore. That is also the shape of the tables of any other source loaded beside it whose own name starts that way, so loading `book.xlsx` and `book_v2.xlsx` together made `book_v2_Orders` look like one of `book.xlsx`'s tables. Saving then looked in `book.xlsx` for a sheet `v2_Orders` it never had, and the whole save stopped there with the error that sheet gave: no file was written, not even the ones whose own tables were fine, and the session's edits went with the database. Sibling stems of that shape are ordinary — `sales.xlsx` beside `sales_backup.xlsx`, `data_2024.xlsx` beside `data_2025.xlsx` — and a source of any other format is enough to trigger it, since `book_v2.csv` loads under a name inside the same prefix. A save now leaves out the tables another source of the same session claims more precisely, which is the source whose own name the table starts with, so each file is written from its own tables. A workbook loaded on its own is unaffected, tables the caller creates during the session still go back into the workbook they are named after, and the refusal of two tables that would become one sheet is unchanged. Reported by [@krishna3554](https://github.com/krishna3554).

- A load waits for a lock as long as it was promised, whatever its input costs to parse ([#554](https://github.com/nao1215/filesql/issues/554)). The five seconds that bound how long one load waits for another to let go were wall clock over the whole attempt, and an attempt is a whole input. A CSV pays nothing for that, since the drop and the create come before its rows are read -- about 140 attempts fit in the five seconds. A workbook is the opposite: it has to be opened and its sheets listed before the load knows what tables to make, so three attempts at a 40000-row XLSX used the budget up and the load reported `database is locked` having waited a fraction of a second. The same workbook against a lock held for eight seconds now gets in when the lock is free, because what is counted down is the sleeping and not the clock. Retrying is not waiting, though, and each attempt at an expensive input pays for its parse again, so the whole retry is bounded at half a minute of wall clock as well: long enough for any queue of loads worth waiting for, short enough to be an answer rather than a hang. None of this makes an arbitrarily long queue succeed -- thirty-two goroutines loading a 200000-row CSV each into one file database is about ten seconds of serialized writing, and the ones at the back still exhaust five seconds of honest waiting -- so `(*DBBuilder).LoadInto`'s godoc now says the wait is bounded instead of saying a load "waits it out".

- A load that ends because its context ended says so ([#555](https://github.com/nao1215/filesql/issues/555)). While waiting out another connection's lock, the retry returned the lock error and nothing else when the context was done, so `errors.Is(err, context.DeadlineExceeded)` and `errors.Is(err, context.Canceled)` were both false for an operation that ended for exactly that reason, and a caller who gave the load a deadline could not tell their own deadline from a bad file. Every load path is affected, `OpenContext` included, since the retry sits under all of them. The context error is now joined to the lock error rather than replacing it, because the lock is what the load was waiting for and the only thing that says why the deadline was not enough.

- `STR_TO_DATE` reads a month, day or time written without a leading zero ([#557](https://github.com/nao1215/filesql/issues/557)). MySQL's numeric specifiers are padded on output only; on input each reads one digit or two, which is why `%c` and `%e` exist for the unpadded output rather than for the input. The format was turned into one Go layout for both directions, and a Go layout element means both at once -- `01` formats a month as two digits and refuses one -- so `STR_TO_DATE('2026-1-5', '%Y-%m-%d')` was NULL where MySQL answers `2026-01-05`. The answer being NULL rather than an error is what made it worth finding: a column of dates written the way a spreadsheet or a hand-edited CSV writes them turned into NULLs, and every filter over it quietly matched nothing. Parsing now uses its own layout for the numeric specifiers while formatting keeps the padded one, so `%m` still writes `01` and now reads `1` as well. What was refused stays refused: an impossible date, a value that is not a date, and a format that names part of a date without completing it are all still NULL. One leniency is deliberately not copied: MySQL answers `2026-01-05 00:00:00` for `STR_TO_DATE('2026-01-05', '%Y-%m-%d %H:%i:%s')` by matching a prefix of the format and warning about the rest, and this package still answers NULL.

- `ADDDATE` and `SUBDATE` are translated ([#558](https://github.com/nao1215/filesql/issues/558)). They are MySQL's synonyms of `DATE_ADD` and `DATE_SUB`, and the rewrite that turns `INTERVAL 1 MONTH` into this package's helper was keyed to the function name, so the same interval expression was understood inside `DATE_ADD` and was raw SQLite syntax inside `ADDDATE`, where it failed as `near "1": syntax error` -- an error naming a token the caller did not write, which is the confusing engine message this package exists to avoid. Both spellings now go through the same helper, month-end clamping included, and the two-argument shorthand `ADDDATE(d, 1)` counts days the way MySQL defines it. The interval form and the shorthand were both read from mysql:8.4.

- MySQL's `WEEK`, `WEEKOFYEAR`, `YEARWEEK` and `QUARTER` answer instead of failing as `no such function` ([#559](https://github.com/nao1215/filesql/issues/559)). Every one of those numbers was already computed here and reachable by the other spelling: `EXTRACT(WEEK FROM x)` and `EXTRACT(QUARTER FROM x)` both worked, and `DATE_FORMAT`'s `%U`, `%u`, `%V` and `%v` reach all four of MySQL's week numberings. So the same value answered or failed depending on which of two MySQL spellings the caller used. `WEEK` takes MySQL's optional mode and defaults to 0, `WEEKOFYEAR` is `WEEK(x, 3)`, and `YEARWEEK` returns the year the week belongs to times 100 plus the week, which is why `YEARWEEK('2026-01-01')` is `202552` and not a 2026 number. The week functions carry MySQL's name in the translated SQL, because MySQL's week is not everyone's: PostgreSQL has only the ISO week and BigQuery has both under separate names. The rest of MySQL's date vocabulary -- `TO_DAYS`, `FROM_DAYS`, `MAKEDATE`, `PERIOD_ADD`, `ADDTIME`, `SUBTIME`, `SEC_TO_TIME`, `TIME_TO_SEC`, `CONVERT_TZ` -- is still absent, in the ordinary way: nothing here computes them.

- Under the PostgreSQL dialect, adding an interval to a date answers a timestamp ([#560](https://github.com/nao1215/filesql/issues/560)). `DATE '2026-01-31' + INTERVAL '1 month'` was `2026-02-28` and is now `2026-02-28 00:00:00`, which is what postgres:17 answers and what its `pg_typeof` calls `timestamp without time zone`. The old shape came from a rule shared with MySQL and GoogleSQL, where a date plus a date-grained interval really does stay a date; PostgreSQL is the dialect that disagrees, and the shared rule followed the wrong one of the three. The difference was silent, and it decides a comparison: `WHERE d + INTERVAL '1 day' = '2026-01-01 00:00:00'` matched in PostgreSQL and not here. The month-end clamping is unchanged and still shared, since all three dialects clamp; MySQL and GoogleSQL keep answering a date for the same operation.

- `required_if` and `required_unless` read every field and value pair the tag names ([#562](https://github.com/nao1215/filesql/issues/562)). Both kept the first token as the field name and folded everything after it into the expected value, so `validate:"required_if=Kind paid Tier gold"` compared the `Kind` cell against the literal string `paid Tier gold`, which no cell ever holds, and the field was silently optional in every row. Nothing reported it: the row passed, `WithValidRowsOnly` kept it, and a file whose required column was empty loaded as if it were complete. `WithStrictTagParsing` did not catch it either, because both halves of the split were non-empty. The parameter is now tokenized the way `oneof` already was, so a value holding a space is written in single quotes -- `required_if=Status 'on hold'` compares against `on hold`, where before it compared against the quotes as well and never matched. `required_if` asks for a value when every pair matches and `required_unless` asks for one unless any pair matches, as the dialect defines them, and a parameter holding an odd number of tokens is refused with `ErrInvalidTagFormat` under `WithStrictTagParsing` and dropped without it, the way other malformed arguments already are.

- `required_with` and `required_without` read their parameter as the list of field names it is ([#563](https://github.com/nao1215/filesql/issues/563)). Both took the whole parameter as one field name, so `validate:"required_with=Street Zip"` named a field no struct has and every row was reported invalid with `target field Street Zip not found`, whatever the row held. The failure landed on the data rather than on the tag: one validation error per row, the whole file dropped under `WithValidRowsOnly`, and nothing pointing at the struct tag as the thing to fix. A misspelled field name is still reported that way, since that report is the only one it gets.

- The cross-field comparisons let an empty cell pass, as every other validator here does ([#564](https://github.com/nao1215/filesql/issues/564)). An empty cell is how a CSV spells a missing value, so this package holds a column optional unless `required` says otherwise, and `validators.Validate` has always returned early on one. The cross-field path did not: `eqfield`, `nefield`, `gtfield`, `gtefield`, `ltfield`, `ltefield`, `fieldcontains` and `fieldexcludes` compared an empty cell against a filled one and rejected it, so a struct describing an optional end date with `gtefield=Start` marked every row that left it blank invalid, and under `WithValidRowsOnly` those rows never reached the caller. A comparison is now skipped as soon as either side is empty, since a value has no order against a value that is not there; the tags that decide whether an empty cell is allowed are the exception, because that is what they are for. This widens the divergence from the reference dialect that doc.go already records for the single-field validators, and doc.go now records it here too.

### Breaking Changes

- `parser.WriteTSVRecord` and `parser.WriteTSVRecordLineEnding` are removed. They were exported when this package became the one that defines what TSV means here, so that a caller writing tab-separated records had something that refused a value the format cannot hold rather than quoting it, TSV having no quoting to quote with. What they guard has since moved to `internal/writer`, the single encoder every write path goes through, and the two were left as a wrapper over it that nothing called: the root dump, `prep` and `frame` each reach that encoder directly, and neither sqly, the examples, README nor this package's own examples referred to them. TSV output is what the output paths produce — `DumpDatabase` with `OutputFormatTSV`, `frame.DataFrame.ToTSV` and `prep`'s own output — and each of those refuses the same values for the same reason. `parser.TSVReader`, `parser.NewTSVReader`, `parser.ErrTSVSyntax` and `parser.ErrTSVUnrepresentable` are unchanged, and the sentinel is still what a refused value carries out of a dump and a frame, so a caller matching it sees no difference. The behavior the removed functions were tested for is tested where it now lives: a double quote written as data, a record that round trips through the TSV reader, and a field holding a tab or a line break refused with nothing written.

- `ContextLogger`, `SlogContextAdapter` and `NewSlogContextAdapter` are removed. The interface extended `Logger` with the four context-taking `slog` methods and the adapter implemented them, but nothing could ask for one: `WithLogger` takes a `Logger`, every internal call site logs through the four methods `Logger` declares, and no code in this module calls `DebugContext`, `InfoContext`, `WarnContext` or `ErrorContext`. Handing a builder a `SlogContextAdapter` therefore produced output identical to a `SlogAdapter`, with the context argument never reaching `slog` at all, which is a context-aware logging path in name only. A caller who built one passes `WithLogger(NewSlogAdapter(l))` for the same `*slog.Logger` and the same records; a caller who wants request-scoped fields on those records attaches them through their own `slog.Handler` or through `slog.Logger.With` before the logger is handed over. `Logger`, `SlogAdapter` and `NewSlogAdapter` are unchanged.

## [0.45.0] - 2026-08-24

### Fixed

- `div`, the two-argument `trunc` and `width_bucket` are computed instead of refused ([#498](https://github.com/nao1215/filesql/issues/498)). Five functions both engines define reached SQLite unchanged and failed there: `div(x, y)`, `trunc(x, n)` and `width_bucket(x, lo, hi, n)` under PostgreSQL, and `DIV(x, y)` and `TRUNC(x, n)` under GoogleSQL, each with a message naming something the caller had not written — "no such function: div" for a function PostgreSQL has had since 8.3, and "wrong number of arguments to function trunc()" for the scale-taking form. None of them needs anything SQLite lacks, and the same operation was already translated for MySQL, where `TRUNCATE(12.345, 2)` answers 12.34, so a query ported across the three dialects found truncation working in one and refused in the other two. The values were read from the engines: `div` truncates toward zero, so `div(-7, 2)` is -3 rather than the -4 a floor gives; `trunc` cuts toward zero at the scale and truncates to a power of ten for a negative one, so `trunc(12345.6, -2)` is 12300; and `width_bucket` numbers its buckets from 1 with 0 below the range and count+1 above it, takes its bounds in either order, and refuses a range of no width. A zero divisor raises for `div` and `DIV` as it does for the operators. The one-argument `trunc` is SQLite's own and is unchanged. GoogleSQL's two-argument `TRUNC` follows PostgreSQL's, which is what BigQuery documents; the emulator used for the other values implements only the one-argument form.

- A division or a modulo by zero fails the query under the dialects whose engines raise for it ([#496](https://github.com/nao1215/filesql/issues/496)). SQLite answers NULL for a zero divisor, and only GoogleSQL's `/` was rewritten to raise, so PostgreSQL's `/`, `%` and `mod()` and GoogleSQL's `MOD()` — along with the `%` this package accepts for GoogleSQL as an extension, since GoogleSQL itself spells the remainder only as MOD — all answered NULL, and a query the engine would have stopped came back with rows in it. The NULL it came back with reads as missing data rather than as arithmetic the engine refused, so a `COALESCE` quietly turns it into a default and a `SUM` skips it. The answers were read from the engines: postgres:17-alpine raises "division by zero" for all four spellings, and the BigQuery emulator raises for `MOD(7, 0)` and for `7 / 0` while rejecting `7 % 0` as a syntax error. PostgreSQL's `/` and the remainder of both dialects now route through helpers that raise, and MySQL keeps its NULL, which is what MySQL answers. Only the zero divisor moves: the helpers read each operand the way SQLite reads it, so PostgreSQL still divides two integers into an integer, `'7' / 2` is still 3, `'abc' % 2` is still 0, a remainder still comes back as a real when an operand is one, and a NULL operand is still NULL rather than a zero to raise on. The one value that moves is a float past the int64 range carrying a leading minus, where `-1e300 % 7` is 0 rather than the -1 SQLite gives, because the sign stays outside the rewritten call. `SAFE_DIVIDE` remains the way to ask GoogleSQL for the NULL.

- `frame` reads a negative zero as the zero it is ([#494](https://github.com/nao1215/filesql/issues/494)). The identity key of a number was `strconv.FormatFloat`, which spells negative zero `-0`, so a column holding both signs of zero was two values to the three operations built on the key and one value to the fourth: `Distinct` kept both rows, `GroupBy` made two groups, an inner join matched a negative zero only against another negative zero, and `Sort` compared the two equal, since it compares the numbers. A negative zero is ordinary in a data file — a rounding, a negation of a zero or a negated underflow writes one, and a float column written by pandas or NumPy carries it through a CSV round trip — so a grouped sum over a column of deltas reported the zero group twice, each holding part of the rows, with nothing to say it had happened. The same file through the loader answers with one group, `COUNT(DISTINCT)` of 1 and `-0.0 = 0.0` of 1, and the package's own rule is that what the inference preserves is the quantity rather than the way it was written, so the key now writes a zero of either sign the same way. Nothing else moves: a negative number stays apart from zero, and the integer kinds route through the same renderer for the values a float64 holds exactly.

- A `prep` cross-field comparison answers about the same values whichever of the six tags asks ([#492](https://github.com/nao1215/filesql/issues/492)). The four ordering tags parsed both cells as numbers before falling back to text and the two equality tags never did, so the family disagreed about what it was comparing and a pair could be neither greater than, nor equal to, nor less than its target: `007` against `7` answered false to `gtfield`, `eqfield` and `ltfield` alike, on a string field and on an int field. On a numeric field that is also plainly wrong on its own terms — `gtefield` and `ltefield` both held for `007` against `7`, which is only true of a pair that is equal, while `eqfield` said the two were different and `nefield` agreed with it: the same pair was equal and unequal at once. One comparison now answers for all six, and it follows the field the tag lands on: a string field orders the strings, any other field compares the numbers the cells spell, and a cell that spells no number falls back to its text so the fallback is the same for every tag. The conditional-required family and the substring pair read text whatever field they land on and are unchanged.

- A workbook whose later sheet fails leaves the database as the file found it ([#489](https://github.com/nao1215/filesql/issues/489)). Each sheet of an XLSX loaded in its own committed transaction, so when a later sheet was refused — a duplicate header, for one — the earlier sheets' tables were already committed to the caller's database, and under LoadInto's replacement step a caller table named like an earlier sheet was already dropped and gone: the call returned an error while the database had changed under it, the opposite of the documented contract that a file failing partway leaves the database as it was, including the table it was replacing. Every input now loads inside one atomicity scope — a transaction of its own on a database this package drives, a savepoint inside a transaction the caller owns — so a workbook is one all-or-nothing unit, as are an ACH file, which maps one file onto several tables, and a Fedwire file, whose message table and write-back row previously landed with no transaction of their own. Single-table inputs already loaded in one transaction and keep doing so, and Open is unaffected in what it answers, since a failed Open never handed out its database.

- A path input that fails partway through LoadIntoTx no longer leaves a partial table in place of the caller's ([#490](https://github.com/nao1215/filesql/issues/490)). A path input can be read twice, so it loads through the typed path, which drops the table it is replacing and fills the new one as chunks arrive; inside a caller-owned transaction nothing undid that on failure, so a file refused at row 3001 under MalformedRowStop left the caller's still-open transaction holding a table with the file's schema and the 3000 rows read before the refusal, where the caller's own table had been — and a caller who caught the error and committed the rest of their work committed the impostor.
