The same input through AddReader is staged and left the caller's table untouched, so what a failure left behind depended on which kind of input carried the data. Both kinds now run under the per-input savepoint above: a failed input rolls back to it, the caller's table stands with its schema and rows, and the transaction stays usable, which the staged path happened to give and which now holds for every input. The rollback runs under a context that cannot be canceled, because a canceled context is one of the ways a load fails and is no reason to leave the caller holding half an input.

- A dumped REAL column holding an infinity reloads as REAL instead of TEXT ([#478](https://github.com/nao1215/filesql/issues/478)). The dump spells an infinity 9e999 because that is a spelling SQLite's REAL affinity converts back to the value, but the inference refused it — strconv answers the saturated value beside ErrRange, and IsFloat required a clean parse — so the whole reloaded column was TEXT, the finite values in it included: 2.5 came back as the text '2.5', ordering turned lexical, and the dump-then-load identity broke for any table where a computed infinity sat in a REAL column, on every delimited format and XLSX. A spelling whose parse saturates now counts as a float, because SQLite's affinity saturates the same text to the same value — '9e999' to the infinity, '1e-400' to zero, measured on modernc.org/sqlite — and the value converters in parser and frame accept the saturated value through the same shared helper, so a cell the inference calls REAL cannot fall back to a string over the ErrRange beside it. The guards in front are unmoved: an integer literal past int64 stays TEXT, since there TEXT preserves digits a float64 would lose, and zero-padded and Go-only spellings stay TEXT.

- frame's ToCSV and ToTSV spell a non-finite float for the reader instead of as a word ([#479](https://github.com/nao1215/filesql/issues/479)). The writers rendered every cell with %v, so ±Inf became the words +Inf/-Inf and NaN the word NaN — spellings neither the inference nor SQLite's affinity reads as a number — and one non-finite value turned the whole reloaded column TEXT, changing Distinct, Join, Sort and the aggregates for the finite rows beside it. A ±Inf is now written 9e999/-9e999, the spelling the read side saturates back to the infinity, and a NaN as the empty cell, the missing value it already is to DropNA and the aggregates, and what SQLite stores for it — the same pair of choices the filesql dump made for the same values. Finite floats keep their spelling.

- A single-column frame row holding an empty value survives ToCSV ([#480](https://github.com/nao1215/filesql/issues/480)). Alone on its line, an empty cell — a nil, an empty string, or now a NaN — was written as a blank line, which a CSV reader does not read as a record, so the row silently vanished on reload: three rows out, two back, no error. The writer now writes the lone empty field as `""`, the record encoding/csv cannot produce on its own but reads back as one empty field, which is the form the filesql dump already writes for the same shape. Rows of two or more columns never blanked their line and are unchanged, and TSV needs no form of its own because the literal TSV reader takes a blank line as a one-column empty value.

- Row.Float refuses the spellings Row.Int and the loader refuse ([#477](https://github.com/nao1215/filesql/issues/477)). The accessor converted text through bare strconv.ParseFloat, so it accepted Go source syntax the module's own inference deliberately keeps as text — `1_000` answered 1000 where Row.Int answered no-number for the same cell, `0x1p4` answered 16 — and the digit-free words Inf, Infinity and NaN (any case) answered non-finite floats, a converted NaN being the quiet worst case since every comparison against it is false. The vocabulary is now the one a data file spells numbers in: a value with no ASCII digit or with Go-only numeric syntax has no numeric form. The deliberate acceptances stay — `007` is 7, `1e3` is 1000, an integer past int64 converts to the nearest double the way an int64 cell widens — and a saturating spelling like `9e999` now converts to the infinity, agreeing with what the loader stores for the same text.

- A column mixing a float with an integer past 2^53 is TEXT instead of REAL ([#475](https://github.com/nao1215/filesql/issues/475)). An integer between 2^53 and int64 max is exact in an INTEGER column, but one decimal anywhere in the column made the whole column REAL, and SQLite's REAL affinity then stored the nearest double: a CSV holding 9007199254740993 and 0.5 loaded as 9007199254740992.0 and 0.5 in whichever order the two values arrived. This is the column-level form of the loss the inference already refuses per value — an int64 overflow forces TEXT for exactly this reason — so the evidence now records an integer float64 cannot carry back, and a column where such an integer meets a real answers TEXT. A column of such integers with no float stays INTEGER and exact, a mix like 42 and 0.5 stays REAL since the widening is lossless there, and 2^53 itself beside a float stays REAL, as does any integer float64 carries back. The rule reaches CSV, TSV, LTSV and XLSX through the shared inference, and `frame` through the parser.

- A Parquet UINT64 column loads as TEXT, keeping its values exact ([#473](https://github.com/nao1215/filesql/issues/473)). The column was declared INTEGER from its physical INT64, but the logical type says unsigned, and the upper half of that range is past int64: SQLite's INTEGER affinity converts such a literal to the nearest REAL, so 18446744073709551615 loaded as 1.8446744073709552e+19 with `typeof()` answering `real` on those rows and `integer` on small ones of the same column. TEXT holds the whole unsigned range exactly, the same trade the reader already makes for DECIMAL and UUID, and the type follows the schema rather than the values — README says Parquet is not inferred — so a UINT64 column of small values is TEXT too. UINT8, UINT16 and UINT32 fit in int64 and stay INTEGER, and the rendered digits are unchanged for every unsigned width.

- A Parquet dump of a column mixing int64 and float64 writes STRING when DOUBLE would change a value ([#474](https://github.com/nao1215/filesql/issues/474)). SQLite types values rather than columns, so a session edit can leave an integer column holding a float, and the dump widened such a column to DOUBLE on the claim that float64 holds both — false past 2^53, so 9007199254740993 was written into the file as 9007199254740992, a corruption in the file itself that every downstream reader then shares. The column kind decision already visits every value, so it now records an int64 that fails a float64 round-trip and answers STRING for a mixed column holding one, the same answer a column mixing a number with text already gets; the exact digits of every value then survive the dump and the reload. A mix whose integers all survive — 42 and 0.5, or 2^53 itself beside a float — still widens to DOUBLE, and a pure integer column still writes INT64 whatever its magnitude.

- A `frame` sort orders every numeric kind numerically, from either side of the comparison ([#466](https://github.com/nao1215/filesql/issues/466)). The comparator enumerated concrete type pairs and knew (int, float64) but not (float64, int), and no uint, uint8-32, int8-32 or float32 at all, so those fell back to comparing the text the values print as: a uint column sorted `10` before `9`, and a column mixing float64 with int — which is what `NewDataFrameFromRecords` produces from an untyped literal — compared numerically one way and lexically the other, an inconsistency `slices.SortFunc`'s contract forbids, so the rows came back in arbitrary order with no error. Two strings still compare lexically and genuinely non-numeric mixes still fall back to their text, but a pair of numbers now compares as the quantities, the same identity rule `Distinct`, `GroupBy` and `Join` already apply, with integer pairs compared exactly so two distinct integers past 2^53 do not collapse into one float64 spelling. A property test pins the mirror-image contract for every pair of kinds a frame can hold.

- `AggMin` and `AggMax` answer with a value the group holds instead of a sentinel ([#467](https://github.com/nao1215/filesql/issues/467)). The running extremes were initialized to ±`math.MaxFloat64` and the found flag flipped as soon as any value converted, so a group whose only number is +Inf answered 1.7976931348623157e+308 from `AggMin`, -Inf answered the negation from `AggMax`, and a group holding only NaN answered the sentinel too — every comparison against NaN is false, so the extreme never updated while the flag said it had. The extremes now start from the first value accepted, and NaN is skipped by all four aggregates the way SQLite skips NULL, since SQLite cannot store NaN and turns it into NULL: `AggSum` and `AggMean` no longer answer NaN for a group one NaN touched, and a group holding nothing but NaN answers nil, the same answer as a group with nothing numeric at all.

- `SortBy` keeps the input order of rows that compare equal, as `Sort` always has ([#468](https://github.com/nao1215/filesql/issues/468)). `Sort` used the stable sort and `SortBy` the unstable one, so refactoring a single-column `Sort` into `SortBy` silently shuffled ties — the order a caller builds multi-pass sorts on. Both godocs now state the guarantee.

- `prep`'s boolean validator accepts what a bool struct field accepts ([#469](https://github.com/nao1215/filesql/issues/469)). The validator checked four literals — true, false, 0, 1 — while `setFieldValue` fills a bool field with `strconv.ParseBool`, which also reads t, T, TRUE, True and their false counterparts, so one row failed validation and loaded as `true` at the same time, and a CSV that spells booleans `True`/`False` — pandas' default — was rejected against the go-playground definition this package documents, which is ParseBool. The validator now runs ParseBool itself, so the two halves cannot disagree, and a processor-level test pins that any spelling the converter accepts the validator passes.

- The comparison validators follow the field they land on, the way the go-playground dialect defines them ([#470](https://github.com/nao1215/filesql/issues/470)). eq, ne, gt, gte, lt and lte parsed both the parameter and the value as float64 whatever the field, so `eq=admin` on a string field was refused outright in strict mode and, worse, silently dropped in the default mode — every row passed as if no check were declared — while `gt=3` on a string field errored on every row with "value must be a valid number", and `len=2` on an int field counted characters, passing 99 and failing 2, the exact opposite of the dialect's meaning. On a string field eq and ne now compare the string itself and gt, gte, lt and lte compare the character count, joining min and max, which were given exactly this treatment earlier; on every other field the comparisons stay numeric and len means the value equals the parameter. eq and ne defer their parameter check to the field: a string field takes any parameter as the string to compare, a numeric field still refuses a non-numeric one in strict mode and drops it otherwise. Struct definitions that relied on numeric gt/gte/lt/lte against string fields should declare those fields numeric, which is what the two package examples now do.

- One rule decides what an empty value means to a validator ([#471](https://github.com/nao1215/filesql/issues/471)). The validators split over the empty string along commit history rather than any semantic line: numeric, datetime, mac and the newer wave passed it — with tests pinning "empty is valid (use required for mandatory)" as the intent — while number, boolean, email, uuid, url, uri, ip_addr and cidr failed it, so two tags a reader cannot tell apart answered the same empty cell differently. The rule now lives in one place, ahead of every validator: an empty value passes everything except required, since an empty cell is how CSV spells a missing value, and a caller who needs both presence and format writes required alongside the format tag. This is where the package deliberately leaves the go-playground dialect, which fails most validators on an empty value; doc.go says so. omitempty is still accepted and is a no-op under this rule. A column validated with number, boolean, email, uuid, url, uri, ip_addr or cidr that relied on the validator also rejecting empty cells needs required added.

- A column whose name holds a double quote no longer fails the load ([#463](https://github.com/nao1215/filesql/issues/463)). Column names are taken from the data unchanged — a CSV header, an XLSX cell, a Parquet field — and a double quote is legal in all three, but the name was written into the `CREATE TABLE` statement between double quotes without doubling the embedded one, so the quote closed the identifier early and the rest became stray tokens: a file whose column was named `a"b` failed the whole load with SQLite's `unrecognized token` error, naming a token rather than the column at fault. Identifiers the loader emits now go through one escaping rule that doubles an embedded double quote, the rule the `dialect` package already applied to the SQL it rewrites, so the name survives to the table and back through a Parquet or CSV dump. Table names were already sanitized to letters, digits, and underscores before reaching a statement and never carried a quote; they are quoted through the same rule now so every identifier the package writes is escaped by one rule. The regression pins the name `a"b` across CSV, XLSX, and a Parquet dump round-trip, a name that is only a quote, and a name that mixes a quote with a reserved word.

- A Parquet file with two columns that are one column to SQLite is refused with the classified duplicate-column error instead of a raw `CREATE TABLE` failure ([#464](https://github.com/nao1215/filesql/issues/464)). SQLite folds ASCII case in identifiers and filesql trims surrounding whitespace, so `id` and `ID`, or a name and the same name with a leading space, are one column downstream, and the delimited, XLSX, and LTSV readers all catch the clash and report `duplicate column name: "ID"` with the `reader.KindDuplicateColumn` classification set. The Parquet reader did not validate its header, so the clash reached `CREATE TABLE` and surfaced as SQLite's raw `duplicate column name: ID (1)` wrapped in a database-operation error, a different and lower-quality answer than the same column names produced in a CSV. The Parquet reader now runs the same header validation the other readers do, under the same trim-and-fold comparison, before the table is built. The regression pins both the case-folded and the whitespace-only clash on the Parquet path.

- A damaged Parquet file fails in time and memory bounded by its own size ([#414](https://github.com/nao1215/filesql/issues/414)). Parquet is read by `github.com/parquet-go/parquet-go` — already this module's dependency, for its test fixtures — instead of the Arrow reader, whose page decoder was unbounded on damaged input: the issue's 433-byte file held a load forever while allocating some 350MiB a second, a deadline passed down expired without effect because the decode never checks its context, and Go gives no way to stop a goroutine that is allocating. The metadata check added earlier ([#443](https://github.com/nao1215/filesql/pull/443)) closed only the files whose metadata is visibly inconsistent; one whose metadata is consistent and whose page headers are not was still unbounded, which is what the issue stayed open for. parquet-go refuses both classes in microseconds, and the 433-byte file is pinned as a test that must fail quickly. What a file loads as is unchanged: the schema still decides each column's type, DATE and TIMESTAMP still render the raw counts the file stores, floats keep their spellings — a whole number with the point that keeps its column REAL, infinities as the literal SQLite overflows back to, NaN as NULL — unsigned columns keep their unsigned values, and the existing type, round-trip, and damaged-file tests pass as they stood, with `BenchmarkOpenParquet` at 314ms against 313ms and 219MB allocated against 308MB. The edges move: a DECIMAL column now renders as its decimal spelling and a UUID column in its canonical hex form, both TEXT; a TIME column renders the raw count the file stores, an integer, aligned with DATE and TIMESTAMP; a nested or repeated field renders as a bracketed list of its leaf values instead of the Arrow library's marshal form; and a half float is recognized by the FLOAT16 annotation the format defines, which arrow-go's own writer does not yet write, so a half-float column from that one writer reads as its two raw bytes rather than as a number. Fuzzing the new reader surfaced an inconsistency class of its own — a MAP annotation sitting on a node that has a physical type panics the library when the node is asked its kind, from two call paths — which the read now reports as damage, with the 458-byte find pinned beside the 433-byte one. The dump side moved off Arrow with it; see the Changed entry below.

- MySQL `TIMESTAMPDIFF` counts complete units instead of calendar boundaries ([#455](https://github.com/nao1215/filesql/issues/455)). The call was rewritten onto the shared `DATE_DIFF` helper, which counts the boundaries BigQuery counts, so every span short of a complete unit answered one too many: 23 hours was 1 DAY, January 31 to February 29 was 1 MONTH, and a span one day short of 4 years was 4 YEAR, which quietly counted every not-yet-complete age, tenure or billing period as complete. The rewrite now names a MySQL-only helper: DAY and WEEK are complete 24-hour and 7-day periods of the actual span, and MONTH, QUARTER and YEAR are complete calendar months — the month delta, minus one when the end's day and time of day fall short of the start's, with no special case for month ends, so a backward span truncates toward zero the way MySQL truncates. HOUR, MINUTE and SECOND already agreed and did not move, and neither did BigQuery's `DATE_DIFF` and `TIMESTAMP_DIFF`, whose boundary counting `goccy/bigquery-emulator` confirms; the two dialects' answers for the same spans are pinned side by side. The time units count in whole seconds rather than through a `time.Duration`, which saturates at about ±292 years while MySQL's DATETIME range spans nine millennia: `TIMESTAMPDIFF(DAY, '1000-01-01', '9999-12-31')` is 3287181. The MySQL values were read from mysql:8.4.

- MySQL `FROM_UNIXTIME` answers NULL outside MySQL's range instead of a datetime ([#456](https://github.com/nao1215/filesql/issues/456)). MySQL documents the function's domain as 0 through 32536771199.999999 seconds — 1970-01-01 through 3001-01-18 23:59:59 — and answers NULL beyond it; filesql converted any int64, so `FROM_UNIXTIME(-1)` was `1969-12-31 23:59:59` and a sentinel like -1 in an epoch column became a plausible date instead of the NULL that marks it absent. Both the one-argument and the format-taking form now answer NULL for a negative epoch and for one past the ceiling, with the boundary neighbors pinned so an off-by-one in the ceiling fails.

- MySQL `TIMEDIFF` answers in MySQL's TIME shape instead of SQLite's interval spelling ([#457](https://github.com/nao1215/filesql/issues/457)). The call was not translated at all and fell through to SQLite's own timediff(), which has existed since 3.43 and renders a signed year-month-day interval: `TIMEDIFF('2024-01-02 01:30:00', '2024-01-01 00:00:00')` was `+0000-00-01 01:30:00.000` where MySQL answers `25:30:00`. The call is now rewritten onto a helper that renders MySQL's TIME — hours that keep counting past 24, a leading minus when the first argument is earlier, a fractional part only when the span has one, and the clamp at ±838:59:59 that MySQL's TIME range imposes. Mixing a datetime with a bare time is NULL, as it is in MySQL, and so is a value that does not parse. A bare TIME argument is read by its own parser, because a TIME's hours pass 23 and carry a sign that no calendar layout reads: `TIMEDIFF('25:00:00', '00:00:00')` is `25:00:00`, `TIMEDIFF('-01:00:00', '01:30:00')` is `-02:30:00`, and each argument clamps to the TIME range before subtracting, the way MySQL clamps it, so `TIMEDIFF('2000:00:00', '1000:00:00')` is zero. Reading a fractional bare time also added a fractional time-only layout to the shared parser, which the other date functions now read too.

- MySQL `STR_TO_DATE` answers a date or a time when the format asks for one ([#458](https://github.com/nao1215/filesql/issues/458)). The helper always rendered a full datetime, so `STR_TO_DATE('2024-02-29', '%Y-%m-%d')` grew a midnight and `STR_TO_DATE('13:05:09', '%T')` grew the fabricated date `0000-01-01`, which no MySQL caller wrote and which breaks anything comparing the result against the column it came from. The shape now follows the format's specifiers, the way MySQL's manual defines the function: a DATE for date specifiers, a TIME for time specifiers, a DATETIME for both. A format that names part of a date without completing it — a year alone, a year and month, a weekday — is NULL, which is MySQL 8.4's answer under its default sql_mode rather than a default-filled date.

- A timestamp with a timezone suffix reaches the date functions as the time it names instead of as NULL ([#450](https://github.com/nao1215/filesql/issues/450)). `EXTRACT(HOUR FROM TIMESTAMP '2024-02-29 13:05:09Z')` is 13 in BigQuery and was NULL here, because no layout the shared time parser tried ended in a timezone, and the NULL is the worst of the answers available: it reads as a statement about the data rather than the query, so a `WHERE` built on it filters everything out and a `SUM` over it comes back NULL with no row count to show anything went missing. The parser now reads a trailing `Z` and an offset like `+09:00`, with either a space or a `T` before the time, and normalizes what it read to UTC before any field is taken, which is the timezone BigQuery extracts in by default: the hour of `TIMESTAMP '2024-02-29 13:05:09+09:00'` is 4. The layouts are shared, so `UNIX_SECONDS` and the other date functions read the same suffixes. Under GoogleSQL a value the parser cannot read at all is now refused by name rather than answered with NULL, which is how BigQuery treats an invalid literal; MySQL keeps its own answer of NULL for a date it cannot read.

- GoogleSQL `EXTRACT(WEEK ...)` begins the week on Sunday, and `ISOWEEK` and `ISOYEAR` are answered instead of refused ([#449](https://github.com/nao1215/filesql/issues/449)). Every dialect's `EXTRACT` was rewritten onto one shared helper whose `week` is the ISO week, which is right for PostgreSQL and one day off for BigQuery, whose weeks begin on Sunday with the days before the year's first Sunday in week 0: `EXTRACT(WEEK FROM DATE '2024-02-29')` is 8 in BigQuery and was 9, a report grouped by week came out grouped with every boundary a day late, and the two parts BigQuery defines for the ISO week were refused by name although the ISO week is exactly what the helper computes. `EXTRACT` now names a helper per dialect, so BigQuery's Sunday week — MySQL's mode 0, which `DATE_FORMAT`'s `%U` already computes — cannot move PostgreSQL's ISO week or the other way around. `DATE_TRUNC(x, WEEK)` truncates to the preceding Sunday for the same reason, `DATE_TRUNC(x, ISOWEEK)` to the preceding Monday, and the PostgreSQL spelling `DATE_TRUNC('week', x)` keeps its Monday under every dialect. The values were read through `goccy/bigquery-emulator`, except the year-boundary weeks, where the emulator disagrees with BigQuery's own documentation and the documented Sunday rule was taken instead.

- MySQL `EXTRACT(WEEK ...)` numbers the week the way MySQL does instead of the ISO way ([#453](https://github.com/nao1215/filesql/issues/453)). The cause is the shared helper above: MySQL's `EXTRACT(WEEK FROM x)` is documented as `WEEK(x)` under the session's `default_week_format`, whose default 0 begins the week on Sunday and numbers the days before the year's first Sunday as week 0, so `EXTRACT(WEEK FROM '2024-02-29')` is 8 in MySQL 8.4 and was 9, and at the year's boundaries the two numberings disagree by a whole year: mode 0 answers 52 for `'2024-12-31'` where the ISO week answers 1. MySQL's `EXTRACT` now has its own helper with the mode-0 week. It stays a separate helper from GoogleSQL's even though their week numbering agrees, because they disagree around it: MySQL answers NULL for a date it cannot read where BigQuery refuses the query, and MySQL has no `ISOWEEK` or `ISOYEAR` part. The three dialects' weeks are pinned side by side on the dates where the numberings disagree, so a change to any one of them that quietly moved another fails the table.

- `FORMAT_DATE`, `FORMAT_DATETIME` and `FORMAT_TIMESTAMP` write the value rather than the letter for twenty BigQuery specifiers ([#445](https://github.com/nao1215/filesql/issues/445)). `%j`, `%s`, `%C`, `%Q`, `%D`, `%x`, `%X`, `%c`, `%h`, `%k`, `%l`, `%P`, `%u`, `%w`, `%G`, `%V`, `%U`, `%W`, `%n` and `%t` came back as their own letter, which is also what BigQuery does for a specifier it does not know, so a report formatted with `'%F %X'` returned `2024-02-29 X` and looked like it had worked. The cause was structural rather than a missing table entry: the whole format string was turned into a Go reference-time layout, and a day of the year, a week number and an epoch second are computed rather than spelled, so there was nowhere for them to go. Rendering has its own walk over the format now and the layout is built only for `PARSE_DATE`, which is unchanged. Building the answer rather than a layout also stops the text around the specifiers being read as one: a literal `2006` or `1` in a format string used to reach `time.Format` and be rendered as the year or the month, so `FORMAT_DATE('year 2006 month 1', d)` came back as `year 2024 month 2` and is copied verbatim now. It is the same fault `DATE_FORMAT` had in [#436](https://github.com/nao1215/filesql/issues/436), in the other dialect's spelling.

- `ROUND(x, n)` with a negative `n` rounds to a power of ten instead of returning `x` unchanged ([#446](https://github.com/nao1215/filesql/issues/446)). `ROUND(12345, -2)` is `12300` in MySQL, PostgreSQL and BigQuery alike and was `12345` under every one of them, because the call reached SQLite's own `round()`, whose second argument is a digit count after the decimal point and which ignores a negative one. A column of amounts rounded to the nearest thousand came back at full precision with nothing to say the rounding had not happened. All three engines agree on every value, half away from zero, so one helper answers all three rather than each naming its own the way `mysql_lpad` and `postgresql_lpad` do. `dialect.SQLite` is not rewritten: ignoring a negative digit count is SQLite's documented behavior and is what a caller who named no dialect asked for. The one-argument form is left alone in every dialect.

- GoogleSQL `SUBSTR` and `SUBSTRING` follow BigQuery's rule rather than SQLite's ([#447](https://github.com/nao1215/filesql/issues/447)). [#428](https://github.com/nao1215/filesql/issues/428) gave MySQL and PostgreSQL each their own helper and left GoogleSQL on SQLite's, because no BigQuery was available to check its rule against. BigQuery's turns out to be a third rule: position 0 means position 1, a negative position counts back from the end, and a position that lands before the string clamps to its start with the length measured from there rather than consumed by the part that fell outside. So `SUBSTR('abcdef', 0, 2)` is `ab` where MySQL gives the empty string and PostgreSQL gives `a`, and `SUBSTR('abcdef', -10, 3)` is `abc` where both of the others give the empty string. Positions count characters, not bytes, as in the other two.

- `prep`'s `fqdn` validator refuses an all-numeric name the way the go-playground dialect does ([#483](https://github.com/nao1215/filesql/issues/483)). The validator checked every label against one alphanumeric pattern and required two labels, but it never required the last label — the top-level domain — to hold a letter, so `127.0.0.1`, `1.2.3` and `host.123` passed as fully qualified domain names, and a `validate:"fqdn"` filter meant to keep host names silently kept IPv4 addresses and bare numeric strings with no error. go-playground's `fqdn` requires a non-numeric TLD (`\.[a-zA-Z]{1}[a-zA-Z0-9]{0,62}` at the end), which this package's doc.go names as the dialect it follows, so the last label is now checked against a letter-initial pattern while the other labels keep the existing rule. A name whose TLD begins with a letter — `example.com`, `host.a1` — is unaffected.

- `prep`'s `cidrv4` validator refuses an address with host bits set the way the go-playground dialect does ([#484](https://github.com/nao1215/filesql/issues/484)). The validator parsed the value and checked only that the address was IPv4, so `192.168.0.1/24` passed where its network address is `192.168.0.0/24`, and a `validate:"cidrv4"` filter meant to keep well-formed network prefixes silently kept host addresses written with a prefix length. go-playground's `cidrv4` also requires the address to equal its network address (`net.ParseCIDR` plus `network.IP.Equal(ip)`), so `192.168.0.1/24` is now refused and `192.168.0.0/24` accepted. The rule is scoped to `cidrv4` alone: the general `cidr` tag and `cidrv6` impose no network-address requirement in go-playground, and neither does this package now, so `cidr` still accepts `192.168.0.1/24`.

- `prep`'s `alphanumunicode` validator accepts every Unicode number the way the go-playground dialect does ([#485](https://github.com/nao1215/filesql/issues/485)). The validator admitted a rune only when `unicode.IsLetter` or `unicode.IsDigit` was true, and `unicode.IsDigit` matches the decimal-digit category alone, so a Roman numeral like `Ⅷ` (category Nl), a circled number like `①` (No) and a superscript like `²` (No) were rejected. go-playground's `alphanumunicode` is `^[\p{L}\p{N}]+$`, and `\p{N}` covers every Unicode number category, so the check now uses `unicode.IsNumber`, which matches `\p{N}`. The sibling `alphaunicode` tag is unchanged and still rejects `Ⅷ`, since a number is not a letter.

- `prep`'s `oneof` validator accepts an allowed value that contains a space ([#487](https://github.com/nao1215/filesql/issues/487)). The parameter was split with `strings.Fields`, which splits on every run of whitespace and knows nothing of quoting, so `oneof='red green' blue` produced the three values `'red`, `green'` and `blue` — with the quotes kept — and a cell holding `red green` was rejected while the quoted spellings passed. go-playground splits the parameter on `'[^']*'|\S+` and then strips the single quotes, so a single-quoted run is one value with its spaces intact; `prep` now splits the same way, so `oneof='red green' blue` allows `red green` and `blue`, and `oneof='New York' 'Los Angeles' Boston` allows each quoted city and the bare one. One limitation is unchanged: the whole `validate` tag is split on commas before this runs, so a quoted value that itself holds a comma (`oneof='a,b' c`) is still broken upstream; a value with spaces but no comma, which is the common case, now works.

### Changed

- Two more direct dependencies are gone. `github.com/google/go-cmp` was one of two test-comparison libraries this module carried for the same job; its twenty-one `cmp.Diff` sites now go through testify's `assert.Equal`, whose failure output carries a diff of its own, and nothing outside tests ever used it. `github.com/tiendc/go-deepcopy` served one call: the deep copy an ACH write-back takes of the original file so editing never touches it. That copy now goes through moov-io/ach's own JSON round-trip — `MarshalJSON` carries the file's validation settings and `UnmarshalJSON` restores them, so the copy validates the way the original was read — and the round-trip test that writes a file back unedited and requires every value to match keeps it honest. go-deepcopy itself remains in the module graph as an indirect dependency, because excelize uses it; what changed is that no code in this repository depends on it.

- A Parquet export is written by `github.com/parquet-go/parquet-go`, and `github.com/apache/arrow/go/v18` — the heaviest dependency this module carried — is gone from go.mod, along with the sixteen indirect dependencies only it pulled in: grpc, genproto, flatbuffers, thrift, golang.org/x/tools, golang.org/x/telemetry, and the rest. The write keeps its contract: each column is written as the type its values call for with the declared type deciding an empty column, every column is nullable so a SQL NULL survives the round-trip, a blank in a numeric column is written as the null it means while a text column keeps its empty string, columns stay in the table's order, and a table with no rows is still a schema-only file — all of which the existing dump and round-trip tests pass unchanged. Writing is faster and lighter: the new `BenchmarkDumpParquet`, the 100,000-row table dumped per iteration, reads 264ms against 293ms and 136MB allocated against 294MB. The bytes of the file differ from the old writer's, as any writer change makes them, and the Arrow-specific `ARROW:schema` metadata entry is no longer embedded; the schema Parquet itself defines carries everything a reader uses.

- The GoogleSQL translations are checked against a BigQuery that runs them. `goccy/bigquery-emulator` serves the reference implementation locally, which is what the three fixes above were measured against, alongside MySQL 8.4 and PostgreSQL 17 for the dialects they also touch. Five of the emulator's answers are its own defects rather than BigQuery's and were taken from the documented definitions instead: it renders `%U` and `%W` as the ISO week, answers `%w` with 8 and `%I` with 13 where a 12-hour clock reads 01, drops the zero padding from `%j` and the week numbers, and slices bytes rather than characters in `SUBSTR` and `STRPOS`. Nothing is being reported to that project, per this repository's rule about third-party repositories.

### Breaking Changes

- A `prep` cross-field comparison on a string field orders the strings instead of parsing them as numbers ([#492](https://github.com/nao1215/filesql/issues/492)). `gtfield`, `gtefield`, `ltfield` and `ltefield` used to try `strconv.ParseFloat` on both cells whatever field they landed on, so a string column of numbers compared numerically: `"10"` was greater than `"9"`. On a string field they now compare the text, which is what the field says the values are and what the tag's single-field sibling already did in its own way — `gt` on a string field counts characters rather than parsing a number. A struct that relied on the numeric comparison declares those fields `int` or `float64`, where all six cross-field tags compare numbers. Columns whose values spell no number — dates, free text, identifiers carrying a letter or a symbol — are unaffected, since text was already how they compared; a column of digits alone moves with the change, so a zero-padded id that used to compare as one number with its unpadded twin now sorts apart. On a numeric field the two equality tags moved the other way: `eqfield` now passes and `nefield` now fails for two spellings of one number, so a struct that used `nefield` on a numeric field to catch a differing spelling declares those fields `string`.

- `prep`'s `numeric` and `number` validate tags now carry the meaning the go-playground dialect gives them, which is the reverse of what this package had ([#482](https://github.com/nao1215/filesql/issues/482)). The two were swapped: `numeric` accepted only a signed integer (`strconv.Atoi`) and refused a decimal, while `number` accepted a signed decimal — where go-playground's `numeric` is a signed decimal (`^[-+]?[0-9]+(?:\.[0-9]+)?$`) and its `number` is digits alone (`^[0-9]+$`). Since the tags are named after go-playground's, a struct tag copied from a go-playground model filtered the wrong rows silently: `validate:"numeric"` on a price column dropped every value with a decimal point, and `validate:"number"` on an id column kept `-1` and `1.5`. `numeric` now accepts an optionally signed decimal and `number` accepts digits alone. A struct that relied on the reversed meaning must swap the two tags: a field that used `numeric` for whole numbers only should now use `number`, and a field that used `number` for decimals should now use `numeric`. The two package examples that validated decimal amounts under `number` now use `numeric`.

- `prep.Validator`, `prep.CrossFieldValidator` and `prep.Preprocessor` are no longer exported. The three interfaces were internal plumbing that happened to be public: every implementation and every constructor was already unexported, so a caller outside this repository could implement one but never hand it to anything — no exported function accepts them — and neither sqly, the examples, nor the package's own documentation referred to them. A caller who declared a variable of one of these types drops the type name; validation and preprocessing are declared through struct tags, which is unchanged.

## [0.44.0] - 2026-08-21

### Added

- `parser.ErrTSVSyntax` reports tab-separated input that does not describe a table, which is the sentinel the TSV fix below needs; `ErrCSVSyntax` has been the one for CSV, and without a counterpart the two formats reported the same fault in two ways.

- `DumpOptions.WithLineEnding` sets the line terminator of csv, tsv, and ltsv output, and writing a table back in place with `EnableAutoSave("")` takes that file's own terminator without being asked ([#269](https://github.com/nao1215/filesql/issues/269)). A save kept a source's compression and its text encoding but wrote every record with `\n`, so a CRLF file saved in place came back LF throughout: a caller who edited one row got a file whose every line had changed, which is a whole-file diff in a repository configured for CRLF and a file the tools reading it no longer saw as they had. The terminator is read from the file about to be replaced — through its codec, so a `.csv.gz` is read as the text inside it — and a file with mixed terminators keeps whichever the majority of its lines use, so one stray ending cannot rewrite the rest. The whole file is counted, through a fixed buffer rather than in memory, and a line break inside a quoted CSV field is field data rather than a terminator: a workbook-style export with CRLF between records and LF inside a quoted address is a CRLF file. A dump to a new destination writes `\n` unless `WithLineEnding(LineEndingCRLF)` says otherwise, which is what every save wrote before this existed. `parser.WriteTSVRecordLineEnding` is the same choice for a caller writing TSV records directly. Parquet and XLSX are not line-based and are unaffected.

### Changed

- Every supported format is read by one implementation. Each of CSV, TSV, LTSV, JSON, JSONL, XLSX, and Parquet was written twice, once in the root package to load a file into SQLite a chunk at a time and once in the `parser` package to read the same file whole, and XLSX was written a third time for the workbook path; the compression codecs, the duplicate-column rule, the LTSV label folding, and the Parquet-to-Arrow reading each existed in two copies as well. The copies drifted, the same way the two type-inference implementations did ([#437](https://github.com/nao1215/filesql/issues/437)): a workbook loaded through `AddReader` did not get its date cells rewritten into ISO 8601 while the same workbook loaded from a path did, so the same file gave a datetime column one way and format-dependent text the other. There is now a single reader that hands its caller a chunk of rows at a time and reports the type every column requires once the last row has been read; the loader inserts those chunks, and `parser.Parse` collects them into the `TableData` it has always returned. A caller sees the same behavior except where the merge settled a difference: an `AddReader` workbook now normalizes its dates like every other Excel path, a workbook loaded from a path passes over leading rows that hold no cell before taking its header instead of failing on an empty one, and an XLSX sheet is inserted in chunks of the configured size rather than in one. Three of the differences are in what a failure says: a workbook with no bytes at all is reported as "empty XLSX file" rather than as the zip library's "not a valid zip file", `parser.Parse` names the position of a duplicate column the way the loader already did, and an error a chunk processor returns is handed back as it stands rather than wrapped in "chunk processor error", which named the mechanism and hid the sentinel underneath. Loading costs the same within measurement noise: `BenchmarkOpenContext` moved from 301ms and 162MB allocated to 302ms and 164MB. `parser.WithExcelSheetPolicy` and `parser.ParseValue` are kept although nothing in this module or in sqly calls them, because both are exported and a caller outside this repository may call them.

- The internal memory pool and the 512MB memory limit are gone. Both were internal, built at a hardcoded size by the streaming parser and reachable by no caller: the limit was a number this package chose and applied to nothing but the XLSX reader, where exceeding it failed the load with an error naming a limit the caller had never set. The pool measured slower than not pooling at all, at 34ns against 13ns per record slice in a benchmark run against it before it was removed, because the slices it recycled were small enough to fall inside the allocator's fast path. The only change a caller can reach is that a load that would once have failed with "memory limit exceeded during XLSX processing" now reads the workbook. Chunk size, the setting that governs how much a load holds, is unchanged and still set with `SetDefaultChunkSize`.

- The `MalformedRowPolicy` documentation says what the package does ([#409](https://github.com/nao1215/filesql/issues/409)). `WithMalformedRowPolicy` described `MalformedRowFill` as "truncating long rows to the header width", where a long row is refused — which is what the policy constant's own godoc says, two files away, and what the code has done since the decision that source data is never silently discarded. The type's godoc gave a reason for excluding XLSX that stopped being true when a workbook row wider than its header started being refused: it said XLSX has no per-row field-count mismatch. Both now describe the behavior, and two tests pin it: `Fill` pads a short record and refuses a long one, and a workbook wider than its header is refused under every policy.

- The README and the write-back functions say that an ACH or Fedwire write-back rewrites the whole file ([#395](https://github.com/nao1215/filesql/issues/395)). Both formats are written from the parsed structure rather than patched, so records nobody edited can come back formatted differently: loading one of the ACH files this repository ships and writing it straight back changes 891 bytes into 950, because the file header and file control in it are written short and the write-back writes every record at its full width, and a Fedwire file comes back with its tags in the order the format defines rather than the order the file had them. The values are the same either way. The serialization belongs to the libraries this package reads and writes those formats with, so the behavior is documented rather than changed: keeping a caller's byte-for-byte formatting would mean writing a NACHA and a Fedwire serializer here. What a caller can rely on is pinned instead: a test per format writes a file back with no edit in it, reloads it, and requires every column of every table to hold what the first load held.

- `github.com/nao1215/fileparser` is no longer a dependency. It was the module this repository's `parser` package was forked from, it has since been archived, and the only thing still importing it was one test comparing the fork against its origin. That comparison can no longer tell anyone anything: the reference is frozen, and `parser` has already diverged from it on purpose. Nothing in a build ever reached it — it was a test-only requirement — so no caller's build changes, and the archived module stops holding the floor under `excelize`, `moov-io/ach` and the rest at the versions it happened to pin.

- Dependencies: `github.com/moov-io/ach` 1.61.3 → 1.63.3, `github.com/moov-io/wire` 0.15.8 → 0.16.0, `modernc.org/sqlite` 1.55.0 → 1.56.0, `github.com/klauspost/compress` 1.19.1 → 1.19.2, `github.com/pierrec/lz4/v4` 4.1.27 → 4.1.28, `github.com/parquet-go/parquet-go` 0.30.1 → 0.32.0, `github.com/stretchr/testify` 1.11.1 → 1.12.0, `golang.org/x/text` 0.40.0 → 0.41.0.

### Fixed

- A Parquet half-float column loads as the number it holds. `FLOAT16` is declared REAL from the file's schema, like the other two float widths, but the value had no case of its own in the renderer and fell through to a general one: a whole number arrived without the decimal point that keeps its column REAL, and a NaN arrived as the word "NaN" sitting in a column declared REAL, where `typeof()` answered "text". A half float now renders at 32 bits, the narrowest width Go can format it at, and a NaN becomes NULL, which is what SQLite has in place of one -- the same treatment `FLOAT` and `DOUBLE` already got.

- `parser.CSVReader` refuses a negative `Comma` instead of splitting on an unrelated byte. The field is exported and the reader scans bytes, so it checks that the delimiter fits in one; the check caught a delimiter at or above `utf8.RuneSelf` but let a negative rune through to a conversion that produced a byte the caller never named. A delimiter that cannot be honored is now reported as `ErrCSVSyntax`, which is what the check exists to do.

- Chunk size no longer changes the data a load stores ([#377](https://github.com/nao1215/filesql/issues/377)). A column that read as a number until a later chunk proved it text was created numeric and rebuilt as TEXT when the text arrived, and the rows already stored then carried SQLite's spelling of the number rather than the file's: with `SetDefaultChunkSize(1)`, a file holding `1`, `2.50` and `abc` gave back `1.0` and `2.5` where the default chunk size gave `1` and `2.50`. The spelling was lost at the insert that preceded the rebuild, so no rebuild could recover it. A load now stores no row under a type a later row can still widen. A path, or a file from `AddFS`, is loaded under the types its first chunk calls for, which is the whole cost of the load when the first chunk was right; when a later chunk widens a column, the rest of the file is read for its types alone, the table is dropped, and the file is read again under the types the whole of it calls for, which costs a second read of that file and nothing else. A reader from `AddReader` cannot be read twice, so it is staged as text and typed once it has all been read, which costs one copy of the table inside SQLite and, while the copy runs, the table's memory twice. Loading the 100,000-row benchmark CSV by path takes the same time it did. Parquet brings its own schema and JSON is always one TEXT column, so neither is ever read twice or copied.

- The `parser` and `frame` packages type a column by the same rule the loader does ([#437](https://github.com/nao1215/filesql/issues/437)). The two packages each carried their own copy of type inference, and the copies had drifted: the loader recognized `15:04:05` and `2.1.2006` as datetimes where `parser.Parse` reported TEXT, and `parser.Parse` recognized `Jan 2, 2006` and `01-02-2006` where the loader reported TEXT, so a file read through `frame.NewDataFrame` and the same file opened with `filesql.Open` disagreed about its columns. One implementation now answers for both, in an internal package neither exposes. It is the loader's rule, since the README promises that the other packages report what the loader recognized, with one addition from the parser's side: a year-first date with slashes, `2006/01/02`, with or without a time of day, is recognized by both. The month-name and day-first-with-dashes layouts the parser alone recognized are not, since a datetime column is stored as TEXT either way and those layouts were never recognized by the SQL path.

- An in-place save writes onto the workbook it replaces ([#433](https://github.com/nao1215/filesql/issues/433), part of [#434](https://github.com/nao1215/filesql/issues/434)). The save built a new workbook from the tables it held, so everything the file carried besides those values was gone afterwards, with no query having to run: opening a workbook with `EnableAutoSave("")` and closing it was enough. Under `WithExcelSheetPolicy(ExcelSheetPolicyVisibleOnly)` that deleted the hidden sheets outright, turning "ignore this sheet" into "delete this sheet", and every workbook lost its column widths, its cell styles, its merged ranges and its comments. The save now opens the file it is replacing and writes the loaded sheets onto it: a sheet no table was loaded from is left exactly as it was, and within a sheet that was loaded, each cell is written over rather than rebuilt, so the style on it survives. Rows and columns the table no longer covers are removed after the write rather than the sheet being cleared before it, since clearing took the merges and comments with it. A file that cannot be reopened as a workbook is still written fresh, which is what every save did before. What a loaded cell holds beyond its text is covered by the entry below.

- A Parquet file whose metadata places a column chunk outside it is refused before any of it is decoded (part of [#414](https://github.com/nao1215/filesql/issues/414)). A file's footer says where each column chunk begins and how many bytes it occupies, and the decoder does not check that those bytes are in the file: handed 433 bytes declaring chunks that begin at offsets 3098 and 3116, it allocated some 350MiB a second for as long as it was left to run. It cannot be stopped from outside either, because the page decoding does not check the context it is given, so a deadline passed down expires without effect and a caller who bounds their own work cannot bound this; the only place to refuse such a file is before the decode starts. The check asks only that the bytes a chunk names are in the file, and reads metadata that has already been parsed rather than any of the file, so it costs nothing measurable: `BenchmarkOpenParquet`, added with it, loads a 100,000-row Parquet file in 315ms against 312ms without it, allocating the same 308MB. It narrows the hazard rather than closing it. A file whose metadata is consistent and whose page headers are not is still unbounded, which is what #414 stays open for; a 457-byte example was found by fuzzing while this was being written, and `github.com/parquet-go/parquet-go` refuses both files in under 300µs, which is the measurement that direction needs.

- A damaged Parquet file is an error rather than a panic, wherever in the file the damage is ([#442](https://github.com/nao1215/filesql/issues/442)). The Arrow library raises a nil dereference on some malformed input, and this package already guarded against that, but the guard covered the table read alone while the library panics while parsing the footer as well: one changed byte in the footer of `testdata/products.parquet` -- magic bytes, length and footer offset all still right, so nothing checked before the handover rejects it -- crashed `filesql.Open` and took the process with it. The guard now covers the reader construction too, and nothing else: the rows go to the loader after it, so a failure there is not reported as damaged data. The four inputs that crashed, found by mutating the shipped fixture at random and each expressed as offsets into it rather than as a new file, are pinned as errors carrying `ErrParsing`, alongside the undamaged fixture, which must still load.

- An in-place save leaves a cell it did not change alone, so a formula stays a formula and a date stays a date ([#434](https://github.com/nao1215/filesql/issues/434)). A cell holds more than the text a table can carry: it may hold a formula together with the value that formula last evaluated to, and it may hold a date as a serial number with a format that renders it. The save wrote every loaded cell, so writing the loaded text back over such a cell said nothing the cell did not already say and took the rest with it. A column computed by a formula came back empty, so the workbook no longer carried the rule that produced its numbers and a spreadsheet opening the file showed a blank column, and a date cell came back as text, so a column formatted `03-15-23` showed `2023-03-15` and no longer sorted or calculated as dates. The save now compares each cell against what the sheet already holds, read the way the loader read it, and writes only the cells whose value changed. A cell that was edited holds the text the table has, which means a formula whose value changed becomes that value; that much is what saving a table over a sheet means and is said in the README and on `EnableAutoSave`. This affects only `EnableAutoSave("")`. `DumpDatabase` and `EnableAutoSave("./dir")` write a new workbook, where there is nothing to keep. Not writing the cells that did not change makes the save cheaper rather than dearer: the new `BenchmarkOverwriteWorkbook`, which loads a 5,000-row workbook of eight columns and saves it back untouched, moved from 375ms and 323MB allocated to 349ms and 302MB.

- `DATE_FORMAT` writes the value rather than the letter for twelve MySQL specifiers ([#436](https://github.com/nao1215/filesql/issues/436)). `%f`, `%k`, `%l`, `%r`, `%T`, `%D`, `%u`, `%U`, `%v`, `%V`, `%x` and `%X` had no mapping and fell through to the branch that writes an unrecognized specifier as its own letter, which is what MySQL does and is why these went unnoticed: `DATE_FORMAT(shipped_at, '%Y-%m-%d %T')` came back as `2024-02-29 T` rather than failing, so the format string looked like it had worked and the output was wrong in a way a caller had to read to notice. The four week specifiers carry most of the change, because MySQL numbers weeks four ways -- `%U` and `%V` start the week on Sunday, `%u` and `%v` on Monday, `%U` and `%u` number from zero, and `%V` and `%v` borrow the previous year's last week, whose year `%X` and `%x` give, so `DATE_FORMAT('2024-12-31', '%v %x')` is `01 2025`. The calculation follows MySQL's own rather than being derived from Go's `ISOWeek`, and every one of the twelve was checked against MySQL 8.4 for all 15,001 dates from 1995 to 2035. A specifier MySQL does not know still comes back as its letter, which is now pinned as deliberate.

- A malformed JSON document reports `ErrInvalidData` whichever value it opens with ([#440](https://github.com/nao1215/filesql/issues/440)). A file holding `{"a":` failed with `ErrInvalidData` and a file holding `[{"a":` failed with `ErrParsing` alone, although both are the same fault -- bytes that are not JSON -- so a caller writing `errors.Is(err, ErrInvalidData)` to mean "this file is not valid JSON" matched one and missed the other, with nothing in the error text to explain the difference. The array branch reported every failure as a parse error because that is where the failure was found rather than because of what failed. A decoder failure is now classified by its cause: a syntax error, or an input that ended in the middle of a value, is invalid data, and anything the underlying reader reported stays `ErrParsing`, since a disk that went away is not a document that is not JSON. Every load failure still carries `ErrParsing`, which is unchanged.

- `LPAD` and `RPAD` follow the dialect they were written for ([#427](https://github.com/nao1215/filesql/issues/427)). One helper answered every dialect, and the two disagree at both boundaries it decided: a negative length is NULL in MySQL and an empty string in PostgreSQL, and an empty pad that cannot reach the requested length gives an empty string in MySQL and the input unchanged in PostgreSQL. So a negative length gave MySQL's answer to a PostgreSQL caller and an empty pad gave PostgreSQL's to a MySQL one, each silently: a NULL where an empty string was due empties a whole concatenation, and a padded value where MySQL gives up is a fixed-width field that is not fixed width. Each dialect's rewrite now names its own helper, the way the cast helpers already do. PostgreSQL's two-argument form, `lpad('abc', 5)`, pads with spaces instead of failing with SQLite's arity error. Truncation is unchanged in both, and GoogleSQL keeps the previous behavior, which no engine was available to check it against.

- `SUBSTRING` follows the dialect rather than SQLite ([#428](https://github.com/nao1215/filesql/issues/428)). Both dialects' rewrites handed the call to SQLite's `substr`, whose answers at position 0 and at a negative position are neither dialect's. MySQL reads position 0 as no position at all and returns an empty string, where SQLite reads it as the place before the first character: `SUBSTRING(s, LOCATE('x', s))` is empty in MySQL for a row without an `x` and was the whole of `s` here, which is the shape a lookup takes when it finds nothing. PostgreSQL counts positions from 1 and lets a start below 1 consume part of the requested length, so `substr('abcdef', -1, 3)` covers positions -1, 0 and 1 and is `a`, where SQLite read the negative start as an offset from the end and answered `f`. Each dialect now has its own helper, counting characters rather than bytes, and PostgreSQL raises on a negative length as it does. The comma form is routed with the keyword form, since it reached SQLite untouched before.

- A call nested in `SUBSTRING(... FROM ...)` or `POSITION(... IN ...)` is translated by the dialect it was written in ([#431](https://github.com/nao1215/filesql/issues/431)). Both rewrites are shared by the MySQL and PostgreSQL passes and recursed into their arguments with PostgreSQL's, hardcoded, so under MySQL any rewrite only that pass performs was skipped inside those two forms. `SUBSTRING(CONCAT('a', NULL) FROM 1 FOR 5)` was `a` where MySQL answers NULL, because MySQL's `CONCAT` is NULL when any argument is, and the rewrite that says so never ran; `LENGTH`, `HEX`, `UNHEX`, `ORD` and `CAST` were reached the same way. Both helpers now take the pass as a parameter, as every other shared rewrite in the package already did.

- `DATE_PART('dow', ...)` and `EXTRACT(DOW FROM ...)` return PostgreSQL's day numbering ([#426](https://github.com/nao1215/filesql/issues/426)). PostgreSQL numbers the day of the week from 0 for Sunday and MySQL from 1, and both spellings were answered with the MySQL rule, so every PostgreSQL answer was one too high: `WHERE date_part('dow', d) = 0` selects Sundays there and matched nothing here, and a `GROUP BY` on it labeled every group with the following day. The two spellings are now separate, `dayofweek` keeping MySQL's numbering for MySQL and GoogleSQL, and PostgreSQL's `isodow` is supported alongside them rather than failing as an unknown part.

- MySQL rounds a fractional count where it was truncated ([#429](https://github.com/nao1215/filesql/issues/429)). `REPEAT('ab', 2.7)` is `ababab` in MySQL and was `abab`, `SPACE(3.7)` is four spaces and was three, and `ELT(2.7, 'a', 'b', 'c')` is `c` and was `b`: the conversion to a whole number truncated toward zero where MySQL rounds half away from zero, so every fraction at or above one half came out one short. A fractional count is what an expression such as `REPEAT(' ', width / 2)` produces on an odd width. `FIND_IN_SET('', '')` is 0 as well, since an empty set holds nothing, including the empty string; an empty element inside a non-empty set is still a real element at its own position.

- `split_part` and `overlay` answer as PostgreSQL does at their boundaries ([#430](https://github.com/nao1215/filesql/issues/430)). A zero field position is refused rather than answered with an empty string, which is what makes an off-by-one in a computed position visible instead of reading as a field that happened to be empty. `overlay` follows the definition PostgreSQL gives it, `substring(s from 1 for start-1) || replacement || substring(s from start+count)`, so a start below 1 raises the negative-length error that definition produces and a negative count makes the tail begin before the overlaid position: `overlay('abcdef' placing 'X' from 2 for -1)` is `aXabcdef` rather than `aXbcdef`.

- A Parquet dump keeps a numeric column numeric when a cell is blank ([#424](https://github.com/nao1215/filesql/issues/424)). The column's type was read from its values, and the reader returned "string" for the whole column the moment it saw one value that was neither an `int64` nor a `float64`. SQLite stores a blank in a numeric column as the empty string, since `''` has no numeric value to convert to, so a single missing entry made the column a Parquet STRING: `2.5` was written as the three characters `2.5`, the file handed the next tool digits it compares and sorts as text, and reloading it here gave a TEXT column, while the same table dumped as CSV, TSV, LTSV or XLSX came back REAL. That is most real columns of numbers, since most have a missing entry somewhere. A blank is skipped when the type is decided now, the way a nil already was, and written as the null it means when the column is numeric. The cost is that a blank in a numeric column comes back from a Parquet round trip as NULL rather than as the empty string: that is what the format can express, and the column's type is the more valuable of the two, since it decides how every reader of the file compares it. A text column is untouched, so a blank in it is still the empty string it was, and a column that is blank in every row still takes its declared type.

- A saved REAL column loads as a REAL column ([#422](https://github.com/nao1215/filesql/issues/422)). A dump wrote a float in its shortest exact form, and the shortest form of a whole number carries no decimal point, so a column of `10.00` and `5.00` was written as `10` and `5` and reloaded as INTEGER: `SELECT amount/4` answered `2.5` before the save and `2` after it, because SQLite divides integers as integers. Which values were affected depended on their magnitude, since `1e6` renders as `1e+06` and reloads as REAL while `1000` renders bare. An infinity was written as `+Inf`, which nothing reads as a number; it is now written as a literal that overflows to one, which is what SQLite's own affinity converts and what the Parquet import has written for the same reason. Read back through this package that literal is text, by the rule that already keeps an integer past int64 as text: no float64 holds the value as written, so a numeric column would damage it. An INTEGER column is untouched, and a dump-and-load round trip is now checked as a property over two hundred generated tables built from the values type inference decides on, which is what caught this.

- Two files whose names differ only in case are refused rather than merged ([#418](https://github.com/nao1215/filesql/issues/418)). SQLite folds ASCII case when it matches identifiers, so `Users` and `users` are one table there, but the check that finds a clash compared the name as written and found none: the second file's `CREATE TABLE IF NOT EXISTS "users"` then matched the `Users` already created and did nothing, and its rows were inserted into that table by position. A directory holding `Users.csv` with columns `a,b` and `users.csv` with columns `c,d` gave one table with columns `a` and `b` holding both files' rows, so `3` and `4` were stored under headers they were never written under, with no error and no trace of the second file in `sqlite_master`. The same clash spelled identically has always been refused, and `xlsxSheetTableNames` has always folded case when two sheets of one workbook collide; the four cross-file checks now fold it too, which also covers two readers named `Users` and `users` through `AddReader`. Where replacing is asked for, as `LoadInto` does, the later input still wins outright: the table is dropped and recreated with that file's own columns.

- A file whose name begins `sqlite_` is refused with `ErrReservedTableName` ([#419](https://github.com/nao1215/filesql/issues/419)). SQLite keeps that prefix for its own tables, so `sqlite_stat1.csv` reached `CREATE TABLE` and came back as that library's `SQL logic error: object name reserved for internal use` wrapped in `ErrDatabaseOperation`: a message no caller could match with `errors.Is`, silent about what to do, and raised only after the file had been read. It is now refused before parsing, beside the `_filesql_` prefix this package reserves and whose guard already cited SQLite's rule as its precedent. The comparison folds ASCII case, as SQLite's does, so `SQLite_Notes.csv` is refused too, and the prefix is `sqlite_` rather than `sqlite`, so `sqliteish.csv` loads.

- A table named `sqliteish` or `sqlite2024` survives a dump ([#420](https://github.com/nao1215/filesql/issues/420)). The filter hiding SQLite's own tables from listings was written `NOT LIKE 'sqlite_%'`, where LIKE reads a bare underscore as any one character, so it excluded any name beginning with the six letters `sqlite` followed by anything. Such a table loaded and answered queries while appearing in no listing, and `DumpDatabase` writes what the listing returns: a database loaded from `sqliteish.csv`, `sqlite2024.csv` and `plain.csv` dumped as `plain.csv` alone, losing two files' contents without a word. The underscore is now escaped, sharing the `ESCAPE` the same statement already declared for the `_filesql_` clause beside it, which had escaped its own underscores from the start for exactly this reason. Names SQLite genuinely reserves stay hidden: a dump taken after `ANALYZE` still leaves out `sqlite_stat1`.

- A file that does not begin the way Parquet files begin is refused before it is read ([#414](https://github.com/nao1215/filesql/issues/414)). The format marks both ends of a file with the four bytes `PAR1`, and the reader underneath checks only the trailing one, so a file ending `PAR1` and beginning with anything at all was taken for Parquet and read into its metadata — where a 433-byte input grew the heap by hundreds of megabytes a second and never returned, ignoring the context it was given, and other damaged inputs reached panics. Both crashing inputs found by fuzzing lack the leading mark, so checking it turns them into an error naming the bytes the file actually begins with, which is also a better answer than the reader's for anything simply misnamed `.parquet`. Fuzzing the reader with the check in place ran 1.4 million inputs without a hang or a panic; without it, a worker died within thirty seconds. This is a guard, not a repair: a file that does begin `PAR1` and is damaged after that still reaches the reader, so the underlying defect stays open, and the guard is removable once the reader bounds its own allocation. A well-formed file is unaffected — the mark is what the format requires of it.

- A damaged Parquet file is an error rather than a crash ([#413](https://github.com/nao1215/filesql/issues/413)). The Arrow library this package reads Parquet with panics on some malformed input instead of reporting it — a corrupted page header reaches a nil dereference, and a row group that fails to read is cleaned up by releasing a column that was never built — and it did the reads in goroutines of its own, where a recover in this package could not reach them and the process died. The column reads are now done here, one at a time in the calling goroutine, so the boundary can be held: a panic becomes an error matching `ErrParsing`, the way every other malformed input in this package already is. Three inputs found by fuzzing are pinned as regression cases, along with the damage that was already reported properly, so the recover cannot hide a reader that stops reporting. A separate defect remains open in the same reader ([#414](https://github.com/nao1215/filesql/issues/414)): a small damaged file can allocate without bound, which nothing on this side can interrupt; the entry below keeps the inputs found so far away from it.

- `DumpDatabase`, `DumpACH` and `DumpFedWire` answer a nil database with an error rather than a panic ([#411](https://github.com/nao1215/filesql/issues/411)). All three reached into the `*sql.DB` they were given without checking it, so a caller who passed a nil database — which is what they hold after an error they did not check — got a nil pointer dereference and a dead process, where `LoadInto`, `AddReader` and `AddFS` all answer the same mistake with `ErrNilInput`. The two `WithTableSet` variants take a database too and are guarded with them. A dump with nothing to write also stops leaving an empty directory behind: the table list is read before the destination is touched, so a database with no tables returns `ErrNoTables` and creates nothing, which is what the connection check above it was already for.

- `prep` matches a struct field to a column whatever the header's case ([#407](https://github.com/nao1215/filesql/issues/407)). A field `Name` derives the column name `name` and the header was compared as written, so a file whose header says `Name` — which is what a spreadsheet writes — matched nothing and was refused with an error that listed the column it called missing among the columns the input has. The same file loads through `filesql.Open` without trouble, because SQLite compares identifiers without regard to case, so one half of the library read `Name` and `name` as one column and the other half as two. Both sides of the match are now folded, which is the rule the loader's duplicate-column check already follows; an explicit `name:"..."` tag folds with them. A header holding two columns that differ only in case is still refused before this, by the parser, so nothing becomes ambiguous.

- GoogleSQL's `STRUCT(...)` and `ARRAY(...)` are refused with the error the typed spellings already gave ([#405](https://github.com/nao1215/filesql/issues/405)). `STRUCT<a INT64>(1)` was rejected and `STRUCT(1 AS a)` was not, so the ordinary way to write a struct reached SQLite as a function call and failed with `near "AS": syntax error`, a message about a token the caller did write. `ARRAY(SELECT ...)` had the same shape and failed with `no such function: ARRAY`. Both are now refused by name, which is what `dialect/doc.go` says the rejection class is for: SQLite has neither type, so there is nothing to translate them into and the caller learns that instead of reading an engine message. A column or alias named `struct` or `array` is untouched, since the rejection needs a parenthesis or a type parameter after the word.

- The string helpers count characters, not bytes ([#400](https://github.com/nao1215/filesql/issues/400), [#401](https://github.com/nao1215/filesql/issues/401), [#402](https://github.com/nao1215/filesql/issues/402)). Three of them measured a string in bytes where all three dialects measure in characters, so text outside ASCII came back wrong in three different ways. `LPAD` and `RPAD` cut a multibyte character in half: `LPAD('日本', 4, '*')` returned the bytes `E697A5E6`, which is not valid UTF-8 at all, and this package's own reader refuses those bytes on the way back in. `LOCATE` and `STRPOS` returned a byte offset where the engines return a character position, so `LOCATE('本', '日本語')` was 4 where MySQL says 2, and `LOCATE`'s start position was counted in bytes too; `INSTR` and `POSITION` answer the same question correctly, so which spelling a query used decided whether it was right. `INITCAP` read an accented letter as a word separator, so `école du soir` became `éCole Du Soir` rather than `École Du Soir`. All three now work over runes, which costs a scan: the same 20,000-row query measures 5.10ms before and 5.99ms after for `LPAD`, and 2.93ms before and 3.49ms after for `LOCATE`.

- `UPPER` and `LOWER` fold the whole of Unicode in the MySQL, PostgreSQL and GoogleSQL dialects ([#402](https://github.com/nao1215/filesql/issues/402)). They reached SQLite untouched, and SQLite's own folding stops at ASCII, so `UPPER('école')` came back `éCOLE` while `'École' LIKE 'école'` was already correctly true — the `LIKE` rewrite exists for exactly this reason, and the two disagreed about the same pair of strings. The calls are now rewritten onto helpers of this package's own, the way `LIKE` and `CONCAT` already are. The SQLite dialect is unchanged and still gets SQLite's functions, since a caller writing SQLite wants SQLite's answers. This costs a call across the driver boundary per value: `SELECT COUNT(UPPER(v))` over 20,000 rows measures 1.62ms before and 3.72ms after. It is the same trade the `LIKE` rewrite already makes for the same reason, and it buys the answer every one of these engines gives.

- The streaming loader closes the decompression reader it opens ([#399](https://github.com/nao1215/filesql/issues/399)). Two places wrote `defer handleCloseError(closeFunc)`, and that helper returned a function rather than performing the close, so the defer ran the call that builds the closure and threw the closure away: every load of a `.gz`, `.zst`, `.zlib`, `.lz4`, `.s2` or `.snappy` source left its decoder to the garbage collector instead of releasing it when the load ended. The helper now performs the close and is deferred as `closeQuietly(closeFunc)`, which makes the two call sites correct as they were written and leaves no way to write the wrong thing at a fourth one. staticcheck reported this as SA9010 on main; `make lint` does not run staticcheck, `make lint-staticcheck` does.

- `parser.Parse` refuses a TSV row that does not fit its header ([#397](https://github.com/nao1215/filesql/issues/397)). A row with more cells than the header names, or fewer, was returned as it stood, so `TableData.Records` no longer lined up with `TableData.Headers` or `TableData.ColumnTypes` and a caller reading a record by header position indexed past the header list. `prep` then called such a row valid and wrote it back unchanged, so the pipeline its documentation recommends — hand the reader `prep` returns to `AddReader` — produced a file that failed to load with `filesql: column count mismatch`. The same shape in CSV was already refused, by the CSV reader itself, and the same file loaded through `filesql.Open` was already refused: the row is now refused in the one place that took it, with an error naming the line and both counts. The streaming loader is unchanged and still applies `MalformedRowPolicy`, which is what `Skip` and `Fill` need in order to see the row at all.

- A cast of a value past the integer range answers what the dialect answers ([#389](https://github.com/nao1215/filesql/issues/389)). `CAST(1e30 AS SIGNED)`, `(1e30)::bigint` and `CAST(1e30 AS INT64)` all returned `-9223372036854775808`: the conversion rounded the value and handed it to Go's float-to-integer conversion, which the language specification leaves implementation-defined for a value the integer type cannot hold, so a positive number came back as the most negative one with no error — the silent coercion the cast helpers exist to replace, and not a stable answer either, since it need not be the same on every platform this builds for. The range is now checked before the conversion, and each dialect gives its own answer: MySQL clamps to the bound of the type, which is what it answers with a warning, while PostgreSQL and GoogleSQL raise `ErrInvalidCast`, which `SAFE_CAST` turns into NULL. NaN takes the same two answers, and the bound itself is handled as the arithmetic requires: no float64 holds the largest integer, so the first float outside the range is 2^63, while -2^63 is exact and converts. A digit string too long for a float64 is part of the same fix: `strconv.ParseFloat` answers one with an infinity and `ErrRange`, which was read as a parse failure and sent the value down MySQL's numeric-prefix path, where a 400-digit number came back as 0. A digit string one past the range is answered from the integer parse rather than through a float, because no float64 tells `-9223372036854775809` from the bound itself.

- MySQL's `/` divides the expression to its left rather than the value beside it ([#393](https://github.com/nao1215/filesql/issues/393)). It is the defect below seen through the division pass, which picked its left operand the same way `DIV` did: `7 % 4 / 2` answered 1 where MySQL answers 1.5, because the remainder was taken of the quotient instead of the quotient of the remainder. A chain of multiplications and divisions has the same value however it is grouped, which is why this shows through a remainder alone. The operand now extends over the operators of that precedence level for `/` in MySQL and GoogleSQL; the two `^` operators keep taking one primary, since MySQL's bitwise XOR and PostgreSQL's exponentiation both bind tighter than `*`.

- MySQL's `DIV` divides the expression to its left rather than the value beside it ([#392](https://github.com/nao1215/filesql/issues/392)). MySQL puts `*`, `/`, `%`, `DIV` and `MOD` on one precedence level and associates them left to right, so `8 * 5 DIV 2` is `(8 * 5) DIV 2`; the translation took only the primary expression before the operator, and the query answered 16 where MySQL answers 20, `100 / 5 DIV 2` answered 50 where MySQL answers 10, and `8 % 5 DIV 2` answered 0 where MySQL answers 1. The left operand now extends back over the operators of that level, and is parenthesized in the output so the division pass that runs later sees one expression and cannot regroup it in turn. An operator that binds less tightly is still left outside, so `2 + 8 DIV 2` is 6 as before.

- MySQL's `MOD` operator is translated instead of being handed to SQLite ([#390](https://github.com/nao1215/filesql/issues/390)). `SELECT 7 MOD 2` came back as `near "2": syntax error`, an engine message naming a token the caller did write, while `DIV` — the same family of operator, in the same query — was already rewritten. `MOD` is now written as SQLite's `%`, which is the same operation at the same precedence and with the same sign rule, so nothing about the expression changes but its spelling. The function spelling `MOD(a, b)` already worked and is left alone; a call is told from an operator the way MySQL's own parser tells them apart, by whether the parenthesis follows the name with nothing between.

- A damaged UTF-16 file fails the load rather than arriving with characters it never held ([#385](https://github.com/nao1215/filesql/issues/385)). Text input is read through a decoder chosen by the byte-order mark, and that decoder answers a code unit it cannot use — half of a surrogate pair, or a file whose last unit is cut short by a truncated download — with U+FFFD: the load succeeded, the table held a replacement character no consumer can tell from one the file really contained, and nothing said so. It is the silent corruption the UTF-8 side already refuses, reached through the decoder rather than through a guess about the encoding, so a UTF-16 stream is now checked before it is decoded and a stream that is not well formed fails with `ErrEncoding` naming the byte offset it stopped at. The check reads the byte order from the same mark the rest of the package does, judges the bytes without editing them, and carries a unit and a pair split across two reads into the next one, so the verdict does not depend on where the reads land.
